This entire work, which contains 540 illustrations <note>original: "Repréſentations"</note> within 20 sections—of which 30 are on full-sized sheets and 199 are on half-sheets—is divided into three general parts.

# I. GENERAL PART.

Includes the sacred and religious ceremonies of all religions, sects, and denominations <note>original: "Partis"</note> found among the Christian and baptized nations.

# II. GENERAL PART.

Includes the religious ceremonies of the nations distinguished by circumcision <note>In the 18th century, European scholars often grouped Jewish and Muslim faiths together under the category of "peoples of the circumcision."</note>.

# III. GENERAL PART.

Includes the various types of idolatry <note>A historical term used by the author to categorize non-Abrahamic or polytheistic religions, such as those of the Americas, Asia, and Africa.</note> of the pagan nations.

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page serves as a preface or "Notice to the Reader," explaining the publishing history and organizational quirks of the volume. It provides context for the plate numbering and establishes the work's value as a visual companion to religious history, building on the structural overview provided on the previous page.</meta>

# NOTICE
## TO THE READER.

Since one might find it strange to see at the head of each of the first three Sections of the first general Part of this Work a particular Plate which serves as its Title, and to find that in each of these Sections the Illustrations have been numbered anew as *Plate I, Plate II, etc.*, we advise the reader that these three Sections initially formed small separate works. These were given to the Public as samples to gauge the public's taste for this Work. The publisher <note>original: "l'entrepreneur"</note>, having subsequently seen the eagerness with which the Public received these first pieces, and having been solicited to continue his Work and bring it to its perfection, resolved—only after seeing the happy success of his undertaking—to give the Work the order it currently has. For the convenience of enthusiasts <note>original: "Amateurs," referring to collectors and lovers of fine books</note>, he decided to divide it into 20 different Sections, in order to distribute them as they came off the Press. And as he was obliged in this matter to follow the German Text that accompanies the work, it has happened that in some Sections there are only a few, and sometimes no Plates at all.

But what we judge especially necessary to mention is that, as the Publisher initially had only a small number of copies of the said German Text printed—most of which are already either promised or sold—he felt he should remedy this defect by printing an Abridgment of the said Text. This ensures that those who still wish to provide themselves with this excellent Work are not deprived of the necessary explanations. It is this *Abridged Text* of which he has the honor to present here a French Translation for those to whom German is unknown, offering them this complete Work *at the price of 20 Florins, Imperial Money.* <note>The "Argent d'Empire" refers to the currency of the Holy Roman Empire.</note>

Furthermore, we do not believe it necessary to dwell much on the utility of this Work. Since it contains a natural Representation and a historical Explanation of all the different religious Ceremonies and Customs of all the Peoples of the World, it is of universal use and suited to all sorts of Persons, of whatever State, Rank, Condition, or Religion they may be. Everyone will find here a diversion for the mind which, far from offending their Religion in any way, can on the contrary contribute much to their edification. Youth especially, and particularly students, will find here an extreme ease in imprinting upon their minds the various religious Customs and Practices of all the different Nations, Religions, and Sects of the World, by means of the lifelike <note>original: "naïves"</note> Representations and solid Explanations that this Work offers them. The fair sex will find no less pleasure in satisfying their curiosity by contemplating so many curious and singular Figures and Representations. Moreover, this Work can be considered not only as an instructive and entertaining Book in itself, but also as a clarification and a necessary addition to Bibles filled with copperplate engravings <term>Tailles-douces: a method of printmaking using copper plates, common in high-quality 18th-century books</term> that are in the hands of so many people. Thus, just as the Holy Bible contains within itself the interior and the essential of true Religion, this Work, on the other hand, sets before our eyes everything that false religions have that is ridiculous and blameworthy, both in their Worship and in their other Religious Practices. Not to mention so many excellent Treatises of History, both Ecclesiastical and Secular, upon which this Work can shed much light by retracing for the Reader's eyes what the Historian only relates through words. This is especially true regarding a work, otherwise quite excellent, which the learned Englishman *Thomas Broughton* has just published in two volumes in folio under the Title: *AN HISTORICAL DICTIONARY OF ALL RELIGIONS etc.*, and which has also appeared in French. This magnificent Work could only be regarded as quite imperfect as long as it is not clarified by Figures, because it is impossible, even for the best pen in the world, to represent external Ceremonies as vividly through Descriptions as is done through the Art of the Engraver.

---

-> Things heard strike the mind more slowly, <-
-> Than those placed before the faithful eyes. <-
<note>original: "Segnius irritant animos demissa per aurem, Quam quæ sunt oculis subjecta fidelibus." — Horace, *Ars Poetica*.</note>

# N O T I C E
### TO THE
## R E A D E R.

Since one might find it strange to see at the head of each of the three divisions of the first general part of this work a plate which serves as its title, and to find that in each of those divisions the illustrations have been numbered anew as **Plate I. II. etc.**, we advise the reader that these three divisions initially formed small separate works, which were issued as trials to gauge the public's taste. The publisher <note>Dutch: "ondernemer"</note>, having subsequently seen the eagerness with which the public received these first pieces, and having been requested to continue his work and bring it to perfection, it was only after seeing the successful outcome of his undertaking that he decided to give the work the order which it now has, and—for the convenience of enthusiasts <note>Dutch: "liefhebbers"; typically referring to collectors, connoisseurs, or "amateurs" of fine books and art</note>—to divide it into 20 distinct sections, in order to issue them as they came from the printers. As he was obliged in this matter to follow the **High German text** <note>original: "Hoogduitschen text." In this period, "High German" simply referred to the German language as spoken in the interior, as opposed to "Low German" or Dutch</note> that accompanies the work, this is the reason why one finds very few plates in some sections, and none at all in others.

But what we judge especially necessary to mention is that, as the publisher had only a small number of copies of the aforementioned High German text printed—most of which are already spoken for or sold—he felt he should provide for this deficiency by having a summary <note>Dutch: "kort begrip"</note> of the said text printed, so that those who still wish to provide themselves with this outstanding work are not deprived of the necessary explanations; and it is of this ***abridged text*** that he has the honor to offer a **French translation** to those to whom German is unknown, delivering this work to them in its entirety at the price of **20 Guilders**, imperial money <note>Dutch: "rijks geld"</note>.

For the rest, it is not believed necessary to expand at length on the usefulness of this work; since it contains within itself a natural representation and a historical explanation of all the various ceremonies and religious customs of all the peoples of the world, it is suited for general use by all persons, of whatever station, rank, dignity, or religion they may be. Everyone finds in it a recreation for the mind which, far from offending him in his own religion, can on the contrary contribute much to his edification. Youth above all, and in particular the Gentlemen Students, will with outstanding ease imprint upon their minds the various customs and religious exercises of all nations, denominations, and sects of the world, by means of the simple representations and concise explanations which this work offers them. The fair sex <note>A common 18th-century term for women</note> will find no less pleasure in satisfying their curiosity by observing so many images and unusual, singular representations. What is more, it can not only [serve] as a book for instruction, and entertaining in itself—

<summary>This preface explains the work's publication history, detailing how its popularity led to an expansion into a twenty-part series and the creation of an abridged French translation to meet public demand. The publisher emphasizes the work's educational value for students and its general appeal to readers of all backgrounds and faiths.</summary>
<keywords>publishing history, religious customs, French translation, pedagogy, 18th-century printing, religious tolerance, student education</keywords>

be regarded, but also as an explanation and necessary supplement to the **Bibles with plates** <note>original: "Bijbels met platen"; referring to the popular 18th-century "Illustrated Bibles" containing many engravings</note> which are in so many hands. Thus, just as the Holy Scripture contains the internal and essential nature of the true Religion, this work, by contrast, sets before our eyes everything that the false Religions possess that is ridiculous and reprehensible, both in their worship and in their religious customs. This is to say nothing of the many excellent historical treatises—both ecclesiastical and secular—upon which this work can shed much light by placing before the reader’s eyes that which the Historian <note>original: "Historieschrijver"</note> reports only with words. This claim is especially well-founded with respect to another very excellent work, which the learned Englishman **Thomas Broughton** <note>Thomas Broughton (1704–1774) was an English clergyman and encyclopedist whose "Historical Dictionary" was a standard reference for comparative religion in the 1700s</note> published in two folio volumes under the title: **AN HISTORICAL DICTIONARY OF ALL (*) RELIGIONS** etc., and which has also appeared in French. That magnificent work could only be considered very imperfect as long as it is not clarified by illustrations, because it is impossible, even for the best writer in the world, to present external ceremonies as vividly as is done through the art of the **engraver** <term>engraver [Dutch: "plaatsnijder"]: A skilled artist who carves designs into copper or wood plates to produce printed illustrations.</term>.

<image-desc>An ornate rectangular headpiece depicting a group of figures in classical or academic attire, possibly scholars or deities, gathered in a grand architectural setting with columns and books, framed by baroque scrolls and floral decorations.</image-desc>

# I. GENERAL PART.

## I. SECTION

### On the Protestant Church.

->*This Section contains 13 Plates with their Explanations.*<-

<image-desc>A decorative square initial block featuring the letter 'L' surrounded by intricate geometric and floral patterns.</image-desc>

The Plate which stands at the head of the entire work, titled: *Tableau of the principal Religions of the World*;
This is an ingenious representation, which is left to each person’s own speculation. The main objects presented there are (a) in the middle of the plate: on one side, the Sovereign Pontiff of the Roman Church, invested with his power and accompanied by Cardinals, Bishops, Jesuits, and other members of religious orders, to whom the Greek Patriarch takes an oath—though smiling and covering his forehead with a mask. On the other side are the Protestants, whose Religion is represented by an open Bible, the Reformation by a pruning hook <note>original: "Serpette"</note>, and its effect by a neatly trimmed tree. The figures one can distinguish there are the principal Reformers, such as Luther, Zwingli, Calvin, Melanchthon, Jan Hus, Henry VIII King of England, Cranmer <note>original: "Kramer"; Thomas Cranmer (1489–1556), Archbishop of Canterbury</note>, Peter Martyr, Bucer, etc. Not far from there stands Menno Simons <note>original: "Mennon Simon"; the radical reformer from whom the Mennonites take their name</note>, who receives an adult girl into his communion, and seated beside him in ecstasy is a Quaker, representing the Enthusiasts. (b) At the bottom of the plate appears Ali <note>Ali ibn Abi Talib, the cousin and son-in-law of Muhammad, central to Shia Islam</note>, who explains the Quran <note>original: "Alcoran"</note> to the Turks, Tartars, and Blacks, where also a Persian holds in his hands the fundamental maxims of his religion, and where the annual sacrifice of those of that nation—consisting of a camel—is represented by that animal. (c) Finally, in the distance and in the empty spaces, appear the Mexicans, Peruvians, Southern Indians, and other Pagan and Idolatrous nations.

(a) This is an allusion to the subject of the first general Part.
(b) This is an allusion to the subject of the second general Part.
(c) This is an allusion to the subject of the third general Part.

The Portrait of Bernard Picart <note>original: "Bernard Picard"; 1673–1733, the famous French engraver known for this work</note>, called "the Roman," etc. The explanation is at the bottom of the plate itself. This plate serves as the title for the first Section, bearing at the top the motto: *Things heard stir the mind more slowly than those seen* <note>original Latin: "Segnius irritant animos &c."; a quote from Horace's *Ars Poetica* suggesting that visual images are more impactful than words</note>. At the bottom: *Truth must be investigated* <note>original Latin: "Exploranda eſt veritas &c."</note>.

The German inscription of this plate reads: *Sacred and Religious Ceremonies of the Christians of all the Earth, engraved according to the invention of the famous Picart, by David Herrliberger, and accompanied by a historical explanation.* First Section, containing the ceremonies of the Lutherans of the Augsburg Confession, the Reformed of the Dutch Churches, and others. The motto on the right is: *The Light of Truths.* That on the left: *The Witness of Times.*

**PLATE I.** No. 1. The **Laying on of Hands** performed for a Lutheran Candidate, received as a Minister in the Church of St. Anne in Augsburg, in the presence of 14 Ministers and the Magistrates, who hold Episcopal Jurisdiction. No. 2. Reception of a new Lutheran Minister in the Church of St. James in Augsburg. No. 3. The Children’s Catechism in the Church of the Minorites <note>The Minorites refers to the Franciscan "Barefoot" Church, which was used by the Lutherans in Augsburg.</note> in Augsburg. No. 4. Confession. No. 5. Absolution in the Choir of the Church of St. Anne in Augsburg.

**PLATE II.** No. 1. Custom observed at Schermerhorn during Pentecost. No. 2. Pentecost custom at The Hague. No. 3. The Star of the Kings <note>The "Star of the Kings" refers to Epiphany or Three Kings' Day celebrations.</note> paraded through Amsterdam.

**PLATE III.** The Lutheran Communion in the Church of the Minorites in Augsburg.

**PLATE IV.** No. 1. Lutheran Baptism in Augsburg. No. 2. The Blessing of a Lutheran Marriage. No. 3. Lutheran Funerals in Augsburg.

**PLATE V.** No. 1. Congratulations, as they are offered to the Betrothed in Amsterdam. **A.** The Groom and the Bride under a mirror. **B.** The Mother and female relatives of the Bride. **C.** The Mother and female relatives of the Groom. **D.** *Paranymphs* <note>original: "Paranimphes"; these are wedding attendants or groomsmen/bridesmaids.</note>, who go to meet the Ladies and present them to the Betrothed. **E.** Those who, after having paid their compliments, take their places on either side of the room. **F.** Apartment where the Men go to congratulate the Parents, after having complimented the Betrothed. **G.** Confections <note>original: "Confitures"; likely candied fruits or sweets.</note>, which are presented from time to time to the Ladies. **H.** A man appointed during the nuptial ceremonies to serve the confections, the Wine, and the *Hypocras* <note>Hypocras is a traditional spiced wine, often served at formal occasions.</note>. No. 2. A ceremony called by the Dutch *Palmknoopen* <note>original: "Palmknoopen"; literally "palm-knotting," a folk tradition of making decorative greenery for weddings.</note>, which consists of tying and arranging flowers and leaves of *Knotgrass* <note>original: "Trainafle"; specifically Polygonum, used for decorative garlands.</note>, which are thrown at the Married Couple on their wedding day. Young people of both sexes, invited to this ceremony, make this arrangement two days before the marriage. They also mix in gold and silver leaves, and apply the same to the leaves of the Knotgrass.

**PLATE VI.** No. 1. The Betrothed going to the Church to be married. **A.** The Groom and the Bride. **B.** A Maidservant throwing flowers for them. **C.** The Sledge in which the Betrothed will enter. **D.** Carriage for the *Paranymphs* and Friends who accompany them to the Church. **E.** The one who throws *Dragées* <note>Sugar-coated nuts or candies.</note> to the People. **FF.** Sergeants who accompany the Sledge to push back the crowd. No. 2. The Blessing of a Marriage among the Reformed in Amsterdam. **A.** The Groom and the Bride. **B.** The *Paranymphs* and Friends. **C.** The Minister. **D.** The Deacon with the purse, to receive the Alms of the Newly Married and the Guests.

**PLATE VII.** No. 1. Funeral Ceremonies as they are performed in Amsterdam and in several other Cities of Holland. No. 2. Funeral Ceremonies as they are performed at The Hague and in some other Cities of Holland.

**PLATE VIII.** No. 1. Mourning dress of the Ladies of Zurich. No. 2. Mourning dress of the Ladies of Augsburg. No. 3. Mourning dress of the Frisians. No. 4. Mourning dress of the women of Zaandam <note>original: "Sardam".</note>.

**PLATE IX.** No. 1. A Synod held in the Choir of the New Church at Amsterdam in 1730. **A.** The President of the Synod. **B.** The Assessor. **C.** The Secretary. **D.** The Commissioner. **E.** The Deputy of the Magistrate of Amsterdam. **F.** The Deputies of the *Classes* <note>A "Classis" is a regional governing body in the Reformed Church, similar to a presbytery.</note>, namely: **G.** Those of Alkmaar. **H.** Those of Haarlem. **I.** Those of Amsterdam. **K.** Those of Hoorn. **L.** Those of Enkhuizen. **M.** Those of Edam. The Correspondents of the Synods, namely, **1.** Those of Guelders. **2.** Those of South Holland. **3.** Those of Utrecht. **4.** Of Friesland. **5.** Of Overijssel. **6.** Of Groningen. **7.** Various Pastors and Elders. No. 2. The Laying on of Hands performed for a *Proponent* <note>original: "Propofant"; a candidate for the ministry who has passed his exams but is not yet settled in a parish.</note>, when he is received as a Minister in a Walloon and French Church in Holland.

**PLATE X.** No. 1. Reformed Baptism in Amsterdam. No. 2. Reformed Communion in Amsterdam.

## SECTION II.

-> Regarding the Church of England, the Quakers, the Anabaptists, the Adamites, the Flagellants, the Freemasons, and the Rhinsburgers. <-

-> *This Section contains XIV Plates with their Explanations.* <-

The Plate which serves as the Title for the first Section, bearing as a Motto at the bottom . . . . . . . *Nothing is not mortal* <note>original Latin: "Nil non mortale &c."</note> etc.

The German Inscription of this Plate reads: *Sacred and Religious Ceremonies of the Christians of all the Earth, engraved according to the invention of the famous Picart, by David Herrliberger, and accompanied by a historical Explanation. Second Section, containing the Ceremonies of the Church of England, the Quakers, the Anabaptists, the Adamites, the Flagellants, the Freemasons, and the Rhinsburgers, etc.*

**PLATE I.** *The Anglican Communion at St. Paul's.*

**PLATE II.** No. 1. The Ceremony observed in England when a pregnant Girl takes an Oath on the Bible before the Judge, that the Man whom she denounces (and whom perhaps she had never seen nor known) is the true Father of the Child she carries. No. 2. Domestic Baptism as practiced in England. No. 3. English Funeral Procession.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of plates illustrating religious customs across Europe. The previous page detailed Lutheran practices in Germany and festive customs in the Netherlands. This page transitions from English and Dutch dissenting groups to the Eastern Christian traditions.</meta>

-> ❧ ) ( 0 ) ( ❧ <-

**PLATE III.** No. 1. **Clandestine Marriage** in England, which, although forbidden by the laws of the country, has become so common there that for a small amount of money one can obtain permission to be married without **Banns** <note>Banns are the public announcement of an intended marriage in a parish church, intended to prevent invalid unions.</note> in a tavern or elsewhere. No. 2. **Funeral Ceremonies** of the English, which take place in the houses of the deceased, where the body is laid out for viewing in a certain room, and where a glass of wine is presented to the assembled relatives, both before the body is carried away and upon the return of the funeral procession.

**PLATE IV.** No. 1. An assembly of **Quakers** in London. **A.** A female Quaker preaching. No. 2. An assembly of Quakers in Amsterdam. **A.** A male Quaker preaching.

**PLATE V.** No. 1. An **Anabaptist** from East Frisia, in a black habit without pleats or pockets. No. 2. A female Quaker preaching. No. 3. A Quaker of Amsterdam. No. 4. An Anabaptist of Amsterdam.

**PLATE VI.** **Coins and Medals** struck by the early Anabaptists, the first of which shows the image of **John of Leiden**, a tailor and the "King of the Anabaptists." <note>John of Leiden was a leader of the Münster Rebellion in 1534, where he established a short-lived theocracy.</note>

**PLATE VII.** No. 1. The **Baptism of the Mennonites**, or the ceremonies with which adults are received into the Mennonite communion through the reiteration of baptism, etc. No. 2. The Blessing that follows the baptism.

**PLATE VIII.** The **Lord's Supper** of the Anabaptists. No. 1. The administration of the Consecrated Bread. No. 2. The administration of the Cup.

**PLATE IX.** No. 1. **Nocturnal assemblies of the Adamites**, who, it is claimed, previously stripped entirely naked in their meeting houses and mingled without distinction of sex—all of this, nevertheless, without committing actual acts of impurity, in order to imitate (as they said) the innocence of our first parents. No. 2. The Adamites as they ran through the streets of Amsterdam in broad daylight during the 16th century.

**PLATE X.** **Procession of the Flagellants**, that is, those who, stripped to the waist, whip themselves out of devotion. **Guibert**, an Abbot of the 11th century, is said to have been the founder of this cruel exercise. The plate was copied from a drawing found at the beginning of a manuscript in the Abbey of St. Martin in Tournai.

**PLATE XI.** The **Free-Masons** dressed in the regalia of their Order, with a list of their Lodges in England and the coats of arms that distinguish one from another.

**PLATE XII.** No. 1. An assembly of those called **Collegiants** in Amsterdam; these do not form a separate church or sect, but are merely private societies of pious or learned people from all Christian sects. In these private assemblies, each person freely shares their thoughts or meditations on a particular dogma of religion or passage of Holy Scripture; they are united only by a love for **liberty of conscience** in religious matters. No. 2. Their Lord's Supper at **Rijnsburg**, a village near Leiden, where they primarily hold their assemblies. Despite the diversity of their opinions, they all unite and commune together as brothers, deploring the misery of a Christianity torn apart by so many sects and thus unfortunately exposed to the insults of libertines and infidels.

**PLATE XIII.** The **Baptism of the Rijnsburgers**. A ceremony where the person asking to be baptized, after making a confession of faith before the whole assembly, descends into a kind of fountain or pond behind the Rijnsburgers' hospital to be immersed in the water. They are accompanied by the one who is to baptize them; if it is a man, he is dressed in a white shirt and matching breeches; if it is a woman, she wears a shirt and a petticoat, at the hem of which are pieces of lead <note>The lead weights were used to ensure the woman's clothing did not float up during immersion, maintaining her modesty.</note>. Afterward, lively and serious exhortations regarding constancy and the observation of God's commandments are addressed to them in the assembly.

**Plate** which serves as the Title for the third section, bearing at the bottom the motto: *Under the Guidance of Minerva.* <note>original: "Minerva Duce"</note> Also: *Lesser than the inventor.* <note>original: "Inventore minor"</note> *But I said it flows,* <note>original: "At dixi fluere"</note> etc.

Third Section, containing the ceremonies of the Greek Church, the Armenians, the Muscovites, the Russians, etc."*

**PLATE I.** No. 1. A **Bishop blessing the waters**. 1. A *Papas* or Greek Priest in church vestments. 2. An instrument used by the Greeks instead of bells. <note>original: "Instrument, dont les Grecs se servent au lieu de Cloches"; likely referring to a semantron, a wooden or metal percussion instrument.</note> No. 2. The **Patriarch of Constantinople**. No. 3. **a.** An Archpriest called a *Protopapas*. **b.** A *Papas* or Priest in a furred robe. No. 4. A Greek Bride seated in her finery on a sofa.

**PLATE II. A. 1. 2. Communion Bread, etc.** These are unleavened breads used by the Greeks and Muscovites <note>A historical term for Russians, referring to the Grand Duchy of Moscow.</note> in their Mass, which are marked with either a single or double cross; the characters seen in the four corners mean: **Jesus Christ Conquers**. <note>Original Greek: Ἰησὃς Χριστὸς Νικᾷ.</note> **B. The Star**, (as they call it) which they place over the dish filled with Consecrated Bread to cover it, so that the linen cloth does not touch the Bread. **C.** The Bishop holding in his two hands **g. h.** the candlesticks with two and three branches. <note>These are the Dikirion and Trikirion, used by Eastern bishops to bless the faithful.</note> **D. The Fan**. <note>The Rhipidion, a ceremonial fan used to keep insects away from the Eucharist.</note> **E. 1. 2. The Corban**, or Communion Bread of the Copts, <note>The word "Corban" or "Qurban" signifies an offering or sacrifice.</note> upon which several crosses appear arranged in order in relief, with the inscription **The Holy and The Strong**. <note>Original Greek: Ἅγιος ἰσχυρος.</note> The Priest **C.** is shown here dressed in all his vestments, which are: **a. The long robe.** **b. The stole.** **c. The sleeves of the robe.** **d. The round cloak**, which descends in front to the waist and behind to the calves of the legs. **e. A type of collar or cravat.** **f. A square piece of cloth**, which hangs from the belt. <note>This is the Epigonation, a symbolic thigh-shield representing a spiritual sword.</note>

**PLATE III. No. 1. The manner in which the Greeks at Easter await the Descent of the Holy Fire into the Holy Sepulchre**, which these poor fools take for a Miracle, although it happens quite naturally by means of a tool for making fire that the Priest keeps hidden in his pockets. **No. 2. The Distribution of the Holy Fire by the Patriarch**, which takes place amidst a furious tumult of the people.

**PLATE IV. No. 1. Mourning of Greek Women at Ramla**, where hired mourners, after having sat around the tomb for half an hour, stand up to form a circle and struggle together while uttering terrible cries, etc. **No. 2. Solemn Baptism of Greek and other Syrian Christians in the Jordan River**, where everyone, without distinction of sex, throws themselves with the greatest devotion—entirely naked and all together—into the Jordan to be baptized and to baptize one another. Some take away only wet linens, others draw water into flasks, and others even take earth and herbs from the riverbank back to their own countries.

**PLATE V. No. 1. 1. Armenian Priest in sacerdotal dress.** **2. Armenian Deacon.** **3. Sub-Deacon.** **No. 2. Armenian Monks, viewed from the front and back.** In the distance appears **Mount Ararat**, and **the three churches located in those quarters**. <note>Likely referring to the cathedral and churches of Etchmiadzin, the spiritual center of the Armenian Church.</note> **No. 3. Armenian girl who is going to be married**, led to the church by old matrons. **No. 4. Armenian man who is going to the church to be married**, accompanied by a friend or relative who carries his saber for him.

**PLATE VI. Armenian Church**, commonly divided into four parts, namely: **The Holy Place**, in the middle of which is **the Altar**; **the Choir**; **the Men's Place**; **the Women's Place**.

**PLATE VII. Commemoration of the Dead**, which is especially in use among the Armenians of Julfa, <note>A suburb of Isfahan, Iran, settled by Armenians.</note> and which is celebrated on the 26th of August, the Feast of the Holy Cross, with candles and lit fires into which incense is thrown; the whole accompanied by tears and lamentations. Only women dressed in white celebrate this mournful solemnity, with the exception of a few priests and some men dressed in black who are paid to pray there.

**PLATE VIII. Muscovite Bishop in Pontifical Habit, in Ceremonial Habit, and in ordinary habit.** Four representations.

**PLATE IX. No. 1. Baptism of the Russians**, which is performed with many ceremonies, such as plunging the child into a deep vessel filled with water, putting a grain of salt in his mouth, anointing him after the Baptism, and putting a white shirt on his body, etc. **No. 2. Russian Funerals**, which are simple for the burials of the poor and common people, but magnificent for the burials of people of quality, due to the numerous procession and the large number of mourners who are hired. Formerly, the dead were given passports in their coffins, or rather letters of Absolution, and even today a priest is sometimes hired to pray for the dead morning and evening in a hut near the tomb during the 40 days that the mourning lasts.

**PLATE I. No. 1. Figure representing the printed side of a *Ballot Paper for the Vote*, or for the Election of a Pope.** **No. 2. The Reverse of the same Ballot covered with floral ornaments.** **No. 3. The Structure and framework of a Cardinal’s *Cell* within the Conclave, or Place of Election.** <note>During a Conclave, cardinals are sequestered in temporary wooden apartments or "cells" built within the Vatican to ensure privacy and prevent outside influence.</note>

**PLATE II. No. 1. *Entry of the Cardinals into the Conclave*.** **No. 2. *Mass of the Holy Spirit*, which is celebrated before the Election of a Pope.** <note>This mass is held to invoke divine guidance for the electors.</note> **No. 3. *First General Congregation of the Cardinals*.** **No. 4. *The Ballot of the Cardinals for the Election of a Pope*.** **No. 5. *The Manner in which Provisions are carried to the Conclave*.** **No. 6. *The Examination of Provisions*.** <note>Food was strictly inspected to ensure no secret messages were being smuggled to the cardinals inside.</note>

**PLATE III. No. 1. *The Adoration of the new Pope in the Chapel*.** **No. 2. *The Adoration of the new Pope on the Great Altar of St. Peter*.** **No. 3. *Structures of burning flax extinguished before the Pope*.** <note>This is the ritual where a master of ceremonies burns a bundle of flax on a staff, saying "Sic transit gloria mundi" (Thus passes the glory of the world) to remind the Pope of his mortality.</note> **No. 4. *The Coronation of the Pope*.** **No. 5. *The Manner in which the Jews present the Pentateuch in the Colosseum*.** <note>A historical tradition during the Papal "Possesso" procession where the Jewish community of Rome presented the Torah to the new Pope as a sign of submission and received his acknowledgment of their law.</note> **No. 6. *The Manner in which the Keys of the Church are presented to the Pope under the vestibule of St. John Lateran*.**

**PLATES IV. & V. *The Manner in which the Pope goes to take Solemn possession of the Pontificate and the Sovereignty of the Christian Church, in the Church of St. John Lateran*:** As was done, among others, by Clement XI. Here, in the first Division, appear the Musicians, the Vanguard, all sorts of Pages, Palfreys, etc. In the second Division, the Roman Nobility, the Pope’s Officers, the Commissaries, the Governor of Rome, the Nephews and Relatives of the Pope, the Ambassadors of Crowned Heads, the Apostolic Sub-Deacon carrying the Holy Cross, etc. Then the Pope, in a Litter, or on a white palfrey, in the midst of 50 Roman Gentlemen, and escorted by a Detachment of Cavalry commanded by the Captain of the Swiss Guards. Near the Litter of His Holiness stands the Grand Chamberlain, then the Grand Cupbearer, the Secretary, the Physician, etc., who immediately precede the Swiss Guards. After these come the Cardinals two by two, the Pope's Guard, the Patriarchs, the Archbishops, the Bishops, the Apostolic Protonotaries, the Auditors of the Rota <note>Judges of the highest ecclesiastical court of the Catholic Church.</note> and the Treasurers: Finally, the led horses, the Trumpeters, and two light companies armed with Lances. This entire procession passes near the Capitol under a Triumphal Arch erected by the Roman People, and through the cattle market under another, erected by the Duke of Parma: The Clergy of the Church of St. John Lateran come in Procession to meet this Cavalcade, and the Pope receives the Keys of the Church there, seated on a Throne, as mentioned above.

**PLATE VI. *The Pope’s Chapel*.** **1.** The Altar, upon which seven Candles burn. **2.** The Pope's Throne. **3.** The Benches of the Cardinals. **4.** Places of the <term>Caudataries</term> the Rota, the one who removes the Pope’s Mitre, and the one who carries the Cross. **29.** The Pulpit. **30.** The Pope's Confessors. **31.** The Conservators. **32.** The Barons & Knights. **33.** **PLATES VII. VIII. IX. X. *The Ceremonies of Low Masses, represented in 35 figures with their relation to the Passion of Our Lord Jesus Christ*.** <note>This section provides an allegorical interpretation of the Mass, where every movement of the priest represents a stage of Christ's suffering.</note> **No. 1. *The Priest goes to the Altar*:** An image of Jesus Christ going to the Garden of Olives. **No. 2. *The Priest begins the Mass*:** Jesus Christ, having arrived at the Garden, makes his prayer. **N. 3. *The Priest says the Confiteor*:** <note>original: "Confiteor," meaning "I confess."</note> Jesus Christ, prostrate, sweats Blood. **N. 4. *The Priest kisses the Altar*:** Jesus Christ betrayed by a kiss. **N. 5. *The Priest goes to the side of the Epistle*:** Jesus Christ is taken and bound. **N. 6. *The Priest at the Introit*:** <note>The opening chant of the Mass.</note> Jesus Christ brought to the house of Annas. **N. 7. *The Priest says the Kyrie Eleyson*:** <note>original Greek: "Kyrie Eleison," meaning "Lord have mercy."</note> St. Peter denies Jesus Christ three times. **N. 8. *The Priest turned toward the People, says: The Lord be with you*:** <note>original Latin: "Dominus vobiscum."</note> Jesus Christ, looking at Peter, makes him weep for his sin. **N. 9. *The Priest reads the Epistle*:** Jesus Christ accused before Pilate. **N. 10. *The Priest bowed at the center of the Altar, says quietly: Cleanse my heart, etc.*:** <note>original Latin: "Munda cor."</note> Jesus Christ, accused before Herod, answers nothing. **N. 11. *The Priest reads the Gospel*:** Jesus Christ sent back from Herod to Pilate. **N. 12. *The Priest uncovers the Chalice*:** Jesus Christ stripped to be whipped. **N. 13. *The Offering of the Host*:** Jesus Christ tied to the Column and whipped. **N. 14. *The Priest covers the Chalice after having offered it*:** Jesus Christ crowned with thorns. **N. 15. *The Priest washes his hands*:** Pilate washing his hands declares Jesus Christ innocent. **N. 16. *The Priest turned toward the People, says: Pray, brothers*:** <note>original Latin: "Orate fratres."</note> Jesus Christ covered with a purple cloak. **N. 17. *The Priest says the Preface*:** Jesus Christ condemned to be crucified. **N. 18. *The Priest joining his hands, prays for the living Faithful*:** Jesus Christ carrying his cross, goes to die to make us live. **N. 19. *The Priest covers the Host and the Chalice with his hands*:** St. Veronica presents her veil to Jesus Christ. **N. 20. *The Priest makes the sign of the cross over the Host and over the Chalice*:** Jesus Christ is nailed to the

### PLATE XI. Solemn Mass, or High Mass, as it is celebrated in the Church of Notre-Dame in Paris.

## II. DIVISION,
->Of the Roman Catholics, containing 13 Plates.<-

### PLATE XII. **No. 1.** Consecration of the Great Cross, where the Bishop delivers a sermon in the presence of the Clergy, and where, after three tapers have been lit at the foot of the cross by an Acolyte, the Bishop offers his prayer. **No. 2.** Incensing of the cross, which takes place at its consecration after three lit tapers have been placed at the top and on the two arms of the cross. **No. 3.** *The Baptism of a Bell*, which is performed by sprinkling, washing, rubbing, and anointing, while the Bishop and Clergy sing and offer their prayers. <note>In this historical tradition, bells were "baptized" with water, oil, and salt, and even given godparents and names.</note> **No. 4.** Blessing of the Bell, which is consecrated in the name of the Holy Trinity and given the name of a Saint. **No. 5.** *The Incensing of the Bell*, using a censer placed beneath the suspended bell. **No. 6.** *Consecration of an Image*, performed by naming the Saint it represents, by sprinkling, and—if it is an image of the Virgin—by a triple incensing, songs, prayers, and signs of the Cross, etc.

### PLATE XIII. **No. 1.** *The Holy Shroud*, in which the body of Our Lord was reportedly wrapped, as it is kept in Besançon in Burgundy and shown to the people at Easter. **No. 2 & 3.** *The same Holy Shroud*, kept in Turin, seen from the front and the back.

### PLATE XIV. **No. 1.** *Blessing of the Sacerdotals* <term>Sacerdotals: The specialized vestments and garments worn by a priest during a service.</term>, which only the Bishop has the authority to perform. **No. 2.** *Blessing of a new small cross.* **No. 3.** *Adoration of said cross*, which is then kissed by the Celebrant and all the Clergy present. **No. 4.** *Blessing of the Sun or Ciborium.* <note>The "Sun" refers to an ostensorium or monstrance, used to display the Host, which often has a sunburst shape.</note> **No. 5.** *Blessing of a Man of War*, to whom the Bishop gives a sword sprinkled with holy water, and with which he then girds him after striking him three times lightly on the shoulder, exhorting him to be a soldier of peace, etc. **No. 6.** *Blessing of a Flag* by the sign of the cross, the sprinkling of holy water, etc.

### PLATE XV. **No. 1.** *The Consecration of Salt and Water*, to bless the first stone of a Church that is to be built. **No. 2.** *Blessing of the Ground* where the Church is to be built. **No. 3.** *The Bishop goes to lay the first stone.* **No. 4.** *The Bishop blesses the foundations of the Church.* **No. 5.** *The Bishop sprinkles the exterior of the Church.* **No. 6.** *The Bishop makes the sign of the Cross at the Door of the Church.*

### PLATE XVI. **No. 1.** *The Bishop writes the Alphabet* with his crosier on the Cross of Ashes made by the Sub-Deacon. <note>This ancient ritual involves the Bishop writing the Greek and Latin alphabets in a diagonal cross of ashes on the floor to symbolize the instruction of the faithful in the faith.</note> **No. 2.** *Figure of the Cross of Ashes* as traced on the pavement of the new Church. **No. 3.** *The Bishop makes the Cross with holy water in the center of the Altar.* **No. 4.** *The Bishop sprinkles the Pavement of the Church with holy water.* **No. 5.** *The Relics are carried in procession into the Church.* **No. 6.** *The Bishop encloses the Relics within the Altar.*

### PLATE XVII. **No. 1.** *The Bishop anoints the twelve crosses on the walls with Holy Chrism.* <note>Twelve crosses are often painted or carved on the walls of a consecrated church to represent the twelve Apostles.</note> **No. 2.** *He makes a Cross on the front of the Altar.* **No. 3.** *He blesses the Vessels* that will serve at the Altar. **No. 4.** *He pours Oil and Chrism onto the Altar Table.* **No. 5.** *He lights the five wax crosses* placed upon the five crosses of incense. **No. 6.** *He consecrates the Ornaments of the Altar.*

### PLATE XVIII. *The virtues of the Agnus Dei.* <term>Agnus Dei: Literally "Lamb of God"; here referring to a wax medallion blessed by the Pope.</term> **A.** An exact copy of the printed sheet that the Pope gives with the *Agnus Dei*, which is a type of wax medal representing a lamb, as seen at Letter **B.** This medal has the image of a Saint on the reverse. **C.** *The Coat of Arms of Pope Alexander VII*, who provided the original of the aforementioned sheet **A.** **D.** *The Scapular.* <note>A garment worn by members of religious orders or as a lay devotional object.</note> **E.** *The Rosary*, or chaplet composed of fifteen decades of beads. **F.** *An ordinary Rosary enriched with Relics.* **G.** *A Ring* used by the Knights of Malta to say their Rosaries: the Rose at the top turns on a spring and marks, with a small black dot on the 10 figures around it, the number of said decades or parts of the rosary. **H.** *One of these parts, or decades of the Rosary.* **I.** *Bread of Saint Genevieve*, a type of small dry biscuit distributed by the monks of Saint Genevieve for the fever. **K.** *Blessed copper medal* that the Sisters of Charity in Paris wear on their Rosaries to preserve their virginity. **N.B.** The virtues of the *Agnus Dei* are detailed at length within the Plate itself. **Z. A.**

The Pilgrims climb on their knees the *Scala Santa* <note>original: "Scala Santa"; the Holy Stairs.</note> or the Holy Staircase of 72 steps, by which Jesus Christ is said to have ascended to appear before Caiaphas: This Jubilee procures a plenary Indulgence. No. 3. The Roman Prelates & Barons, dressed as Penitents, wash the Feet of the Pilgrims and serve them. No. 4. The Pope blesses the Tables of the Pilgrims & serves them food, with the Cardinals and other Prelates. No. 5. The Pope distributes Rosaries, Medals, and *Agnus Dei* <note>Wax discs blessed by the Pope, often bearing the image of a lamb.</note> to the Pilgrims, and they kiss his Feet. No. 6. At the end of the Jubilee the Pope lays the first stone to close the Holy Door.

A. The Inquisitor, who orders the Question, and who has it increased, decreased, or ceased, when and as he pleases. B. His two Assistants with the Secretary. C. A Criminal, to whom the Water Torture <note>A form of torture where the victim is forced to ingest large quantities of water.</note> is given. D. A Criminal, to whom the Torture of the Strappado <note>original: "Estrapade"</note> is given. E. E. B. The Executioners disguised as Specters, who give the Strappado to a Criminal, that is to say, who pull him up to the Vault by his hands tied behind his back & then let him fall to the Ground.

ground, until his hips are dislocated. F. *A raised bench*, which can be widened or narrowed as needed, and upon which the criminal is laid to undergo the *Water Torture*. Through this machine passes a wooden bar, which lifts the body of the criminal in the middle, usually breaking his back and causing him inexpressible pain. G. *The criminal is filled with water* by means of a *Funnel* placed in his mouth. H. H. H. *Sergeants of the Inquisition*, who always keep water ready to fill the Funnel. I. *The Torture by Fire*, which is performed by stretching the criminal on the ground, his feet placed against a large fire to roast them there until he confesses everything they wish to know from him. K. *An executioner*, who rubs the feet of the Patient <note>In this context, "patient" refers to the person undergoing the torture.</note> with lard and certain corrosive or burning oils to increase his torment and pain. L. *The Physician of the Inquisition*, who gives warning when there is a danger of death.

No. 1. *Banner of the Spanish Inquisition*, upon which there is a knotted Cross, and on one side an olive branch, a sign of peace for Catholics; on the other a sword, a sign of war against Heretics, with the inscription: *Arise, Lord, and defend your cause. Ps. 73.* <note>original: "Exsurge, Domine, et judica causam tuam." This refers to Psalm 74:22 in modern numbering.</note> No. 2. *Banner of the Inquisition of Goa*, on which is shown the image of *St. Dominic*, the founder of the Inquisition, carrying in one hand an olive branch and in the other a sword: below the image is a dog, carrying a burning torch in its mouth and casting flames against the globe of the world: above the image one sees the inscription: *Mercy and Justice.* <note>original: "Misericordia et Justitia." The dog with the torch is a pun on the name of the Dominican Order: "Domini canes" or "the dogs of the Lord."</note> No. 3. *A man arrested for heresy, who accused himself before being judged.* No. 4. *A Nun who avoided the fire by confessing before being judged.*

# PART II. GENERAL.

---

## SECTION I.

### Concerning the Jews.

->This Section contains 13 Plates with their explanations.<-

The Plate which stands at the head of this second General Part, and which bears the motto: *Bile or laughter, etc.* <note>original: "Bilem aut risum &c." A Latin phrase suggesting a choice between anger or ridicule at the contents.</note> represents the Consecration of Muhammad near the Village of Hudaybiyyah.

The German inscription of this plate reads: *Ceremonies, Exercises, and Religious Duties of the Jews, Turks, etc. Engraved after the design of the famous Picart, by David Herrliberger, and accompanied by a Historical Explanation: Two Sections representing the ceremonies of nations distinguished by Circumcision. 1746.*

PLATE I. *The Dedication of the Synagogue of the Portuguese Jews in Amsterdam.* A. *The Hechal, where the Books of the Law are kept.* <term>Hechal: The Ark or cabinet containing the Torah scrolls.</term> B. *The Thebab, or pulpit, where the Chazzan and Hakham read the Law.* <term>Thebab: (Tebah) The raised platform for the reader; Chazzan: Cantor; Hakham: Rabbi.</term> C. *The two galleries for the women.* This Dedication of the Synagogue and the entry of the Books of the Law was celebrated for 8 days, beginning from the 10th of the month of Elul 5435, which corresponds to August 2, 1675, the day on which the commemoration is held every year.

# IV. DIVISION,
## Of the Roman Catholics, consisting of 12 Plates.

E. E. E. The Executioners disguised as Spectres, who administer the *Estrapade* to a Criminal, that is to say, who pull him up to the Vault by his hands tied behind his back and then let him fall to the Earth,

F. *A high bench, which can be widened or narrowed according to need, and on which the criminal is laid to be given the Water Torture.* Through this machine passes a wooden bar that supports the middle of the criminal's body, usually breaking the lower back and causing inexpressible pain. # II. GENERAL PART.

## SECTION I.

### Concerning the Jews.

->This Section contains XIII Plates with their Explanations.<-

The Plate at the head of this second General Part, which bears the motto *Bilem aut risum* <note>original Latin: "Bile or laughter," suggesting the content may provoke either anger or mockery.</note> etc., represents the Consecration of Muhammad near the village of Hudaybiyyah.

The German inscription of this plate reads: *Religious Ceremonies, Exercises, and Duties of the Jews, Turks, etc. Engraved after the invention of the famous Picart by David Herrliberger, and accompanied by a Historical Explanation: Two Sections representing the ceremonies of nations distinguished by circumcision.* 1746.

*PLATE I. The Dedication of the Portuguese Jewish Synagogue in Amsterdam.* A. *The Hechal <note>original: "Hechal" — the Holy Ark where the Torah scrolls are kept.</note>, where the Books of the Law are kept.* B. *The Tebah <note>original: "Thebab" — the platform or pulpit where the service is led.</note>, or pulpit, where the Chazzan <note>original: "Hatzan" — the Cantor.</note> and Hakham <note>original: "Hacham" — the Rabbi or Sage.</note> read the Law.* C. *The two galleries for the women.* This dedication of the Synagogue and the entry of the Books of the Law was celebrated for 8 days, beginning from the 10th of the month of Elul 5435, which corresponds to the 2nd of the month of August 1675, on which day the commemoration is held every year.

# II. GENERAL PART.

## SECTION I.

### Of the Jews.

->This Section contains XIII Plates with their Explanations.<-

The Plate, which is at the head of this second General Part, and which bears the Motto: *Anger or laughter, etc.* <note>original: "Bilem aut risum &c." Likely a reference to Horace’s "Spectatum admissi, risum teneatis, amici?"—can you help laughing?—suggesting a mixture of mockery and indignation toward the subject matter.</note> represents the Consecration of Muhammad near the Village of Hudaybiyyah.

The German Inscription of this Plate reads: *Religious Ceremonies, Exercises and Duties of the Jews, the Turks etc. Engraved after the Invention of the Famous Picart, by David Herrliberger, and accompanied by a Historical Explanation: Two Sections Representing the Ceremonies of the Nations distinguished by Circumcision.* 1746. <note>original: "CIO IO CC XXXXVI" is a Roman numeral format for 1746.</note>

**PLATE I.** *The Dedication of the Synagogue of the Portuguese Jews in Amsterdam.* A. *The Heichal <term>the Ark where Torah scrolls are kept</term>, where the Books of the Law are kept.* B. *The Tevah <term>the platform or bimah</term>, or Pulpit, where the Hazan <term>Cantor</term> and Hakham <term>Wise man or Rabbi</term> read the Law.* C. *The 2 Galleries for the Women.* This Dedication of the Synagogue and the Entry of the Books of the Law was celebrated for 8 days, beginning from the 10th of the Month of Elul 5435, which corresponds to the 2nd of the month of August 1675, on which day the Commemoration is held every Year.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of illustrations from the work. While the previous page focused on the grim proceedings of the Inquisition in Spain, this page shifts focus to the religious customs, ritual objects, and communal ceremonies of the Jewish people, specifically the Portuguese (Sephardic) and German (Ashkenazi) communities in the Netherlands.</meta>

->*) ( 0 ) (*<-

### PLATE II.
**A.** *Tallit* <note>original: "Taled"</note>, or *white veil*, which the Jews place over their heads at the Synagogue during their prayer, at the four corners of which hangs the *Tzitzit* <note>original: "Sitfith"</note>, or cord with 5 knots. **B. B.** *The left arm*, seen from the inside and outside with the *Tefillin* <note>original: "Tefila"</note>. **C.** *Tefillin shel yad* <note>original: "Tefila Schel-jad"</note>, or the *hand Tefillin*, shown in large scale, with the knot in the shape of the Hebrew letter *Resh* ר. **D. D.** *Tefillin shel rosh* <note>original: "Tefila Schel Rosch"</note>, or the *primary Tefillin of the head*, seen from the front (1) and from the side (2). **E. E.** *The knots*, in the shape of the Hebrew letter *Tet* <note>original: "Theth"</note> ט, behind the head. **F.** *A Jew covered by the Tallit*, and wearing the *Tefillin* on the head and arm.

### PLATE III.
**A.** *Garment* <note>Referring to the *Tallit Katan*, a fringed undergarment</note> that the Jews wore in former times, and which they now wear under their ordinary clothes so as not to be mocked by those among whom they live. **B.** *The Tzitzit cords* which are at the four corners. **C.** One of the said cords represented in large scale, with 5 knots, which signify the Name of God. **D.** *A Sabbath Lamp*. **E.** *Unleavened bread called Shimurim* <note>original: "Simurim"</note>, with which they observe Passover. **F.** *Ordinary unleavened bread called Matzot* <note>original: "Mathroth"</note>, which they eat throughout the duration of the Festival. **G.** *Branches of Palm*, *Willow*, and *Myrtle*, joined together, which they call *Lulav* <note>original: "Lulab"</note>, and which they hold in the left hand. **H.** *Citron* <note>the *Etrog*</note>, which they hold in the right hand: this is to bring these two things together and to shake them toward the 4 winds. **I.** *Shofar* <note>original: "Sophar"</note>, or *ram’s horn*, with which they blow on the *New Year’s Day* and at other Festivals. **K.** *Mezuzah* <note>original: "Mezufa"</note>, a stick or hollow cane attached to the right side of doorposts, in which is enclosed a parchment where the passages of Deuteronomy 6:4 and 11:13 are written.

### PLATE IV.
**No. 1.** *The Priests, descendants of Aaron, give the Blessing to the People*. <note>The *Birkat Kohanim* or Priestly Blessing.</note> **No. 2.** *Manner of displaying the Law to the eyes of the People* <note>The elevation of the Torah scroll, known as *Hagbah*</note> before beginning to read it: The buildings shown here are the different facades of the *Portuguese Synagogue in The Hague*.

### PLATE V.
**No. 1.** *The horn is blown on the first day of the Year*. **No. 2.** *The Kippur* <note>original: "Chipur"</note>, or *Day of Atonement*, as it is celebrated among the German Jews.

### PLATE VI.
**No. 1.** *The Search for Leaven* <note>original: "L'Examen du Levain"</note>, or the manner in which all leavened bread is gathered and removed from the houses. **A.** *The Mistress of the house*, who puts leavened bread in various places so that her husband, who is performing the search, may find it. **No. 2.** *The Passover Meal of the Portuguese Jews*. **1.** *The Plate* containing the bone of a lamb's shoulder, with a hard-boiled egg. **2.** *Plate* where the bitter herbs are placed. **3.** *Plate of figs*, apples, almonds, cinnamon, etc., chopped and cooked together <note>known as *Charoset*</note>, to represent the *mortar* with which their fathers made bricks in Egypt. **4.** *Plate of sauce* for dipping the bitter herbs. **5.** *Half of the Levite's cake* <note>The *Afikomen*</note>, from which the Father of the Family breaks pieces to distribute to those at the table: all Jewish servants on this occasion sit at the same table as the Master and Mistress. **6.** *Napkin* with which the cake was covered. **7.** *Basket* where the *Matzot* or *Passover breads* are kept.

### PLATE VII.
**No. 1.** *Procession of Palms* <note>The *Hoshanot* ceremony</note> among the Portuguese Jews. **No. 2.** *Meal of the Jews during the Feast of Tabernacles* <note>Sukkot</note>.

### PLATE VIII.
**No. 1.** *Simchat Torah* <note>original: "Simcha-Tora"</note>, or *Joy for the Law*: **A.** *The Hatan Bereshit* <note>original: "Cadan Bereſcid"; the Groom of the Beginning</note>. **B.** *The Hatan Torah* <note>original: "Cadan Tora"; the Groom of the Law</note>: both are Spouses of the Law, representing the Kings of Israel, who were obliged to begin and finish the reading of the Law on the last day of the Feast of Tabernacles. The *Hatan Torah* is the one who finishes it, and the *Hatan Bereshit* is the one who begins it. **C. C.** *Two Parnassim* <term>wardens or elected lay leaders of the synagogue</term>. This building represents the Synagogue of the Portuguese Jews in Amsterdam. **No. 2.** *Manner of escorting the Spouses of the Law to their homes*. **A.** *The Hatan Torah*, on the day he finishes the reading of the Law, is escorted back in the evening by the *Hatan Bereshit*, who is on his right, and a *Parnass* on his left, followed by other *Parnassim*, relatives, and friends, and escorted by some officers. And on the day that the *Hatan Bereshit* begins the Law, the *Hatan Torah* escorts him in turn, in the same order, with the difference that the *Hatan Bereshit* then occupies the middle place.

### PLATE IX.
**No. 1.** *Instruments used for Circumcision*. **1.** *Small plate* where the plasters are kept. **2.** *Another plate* containing sand, to place the foreskin. **3.** *A plaster*. **4.** *Flasks* containing astringent powders. **5.** *Knife* used for the circumcision. **6.** *Instrument* used to hold the foreskin. **7.** *Bandage* used to hold the plasters. **8.** *Small silver rod* over which the foreskin is drawn. **No. 2.** *The Book of the Law*: **A.** *Small chest* containing the names of all the members of the Synagogue, from which the names of seven persons destined to carry and read the Law are drawn by lot: this small chest is divided into 4 parts. The first, marked **1**, contains the names of the Levites. **2** is for placing the names of the Levites as they have read. **3** contains the names of all the other members of the Synagogue. **4** is the place for the names of those among the latter who have already read. **B.** *Sefer Torah* <note>original: "Sepher Tora"</note>, or the *Scroll of the Law* open. **C.** *The Scroll of the Law closed and wrapped in a very wide ribbon*. **D.** *The Scroll of the Law with its ornaments*. **E.** *The Rimonim*, or *silver ornaments with bells*, which are placed on the two top ends of the Scroll. **F.** *Pointer* <note>the *Yad*</note>, which serves to guide the person reading.

### PLATE X.
**No. 1.** *The Circumcision of the Portuguese Jews*. **A.** *The Father of the child*. **B.** *The Mother in another room with the Godmother*, for Jewish women do not attend this ceremony. **C.** *The Godfather holding the child on his knees during the operation*. **D.** *An empty seat to the right of the Godfather for the Prophet Elijah*. **E.** *The Mohel*, or the one who performs the function of circumcising. **F.** *The Rabbi, a relative, or a friend holding the Cup*. **NB.** The personages presented here with uncovered heads, and the ladies seen there, are Monsieur Picart <note>Bernard Picart, the famed French engraver who illustrated this series</note> with his entire family of that time, composed of his wife, three daughters, two disciples, and a servant, who attended a circumcision. **No. 2.** *The Redemption of the Firstborn* <note>The *Pidyon HaBen* ceremony</note> **A.** A Priest <note>a *Kohen*</note> descending from the family of Aaron carrying away the child. **B.** The Father offering money to redeem him, namely usually about six florins, corresponding to the five shekels of the Ancient...

# II. SECTION.
## OF THE MAHOMETANS. <note>A historical term for Muslims.</note>

->This Section contains 23 Plates, with their Explanations, in 4 Divisions.<-

### I. DIVISION.
->which has Two Plates.<-

**PLATE XIII.** Genealogy of Muhammad <note>original: "Mahomet"</note>, from Ishmael, Son of the Patriarch Abraham, taken from the newest Memoirs of the Tradition of the Mahometans. 1. Ishmael. 2. Kedar. 3. Selam. 4. Jesjab. 5. Hemaisa. 6. Jase. 7. Ader. 8. Ad. 9. Ednag, of a rare Beauty. 10. Maad, Great Captain, Enemy of the Jews. 11. Nizar, or Nafar, whose Standard the Turks say they still possess. 12. Modar. 13. El-yas. 14. Medrike, or Modrecas. 15. Chazaima. 16. Canane or Cenana. 17. Nodar. 18. Malic, famous Prophet. 19. Fahir or Fehr. 20. Galib. 21. Loway. 22. Kaab. 23. Morrha. 24. Kelab or Kaleb. 25. Koudai or Kofa. 26. Abd-Menaf. 27. Haschem. 28. Abdol-lotalleb. 29. Abdolla. 30. Muhammad the Prophet, whose Daughter Fatima was married to Ali.

**PLATE XIV.** *The Temple of Mecca.* **A.** *The Black Stone set into the Wall of the Kaaba* <note>original: "Caaba"</note>, *or the Temple.* **B.** *Place where the footprints of Abraham are shown, namely on a stone upon which he stood when he, with Ishmael, prayed to God while the Kaaba was being built.* **C.** *Building which contains the Well of Zamzam.* <note>original: "Zemzem"</note> **D.** *Door, or opening of the Kaaba.* **E.** *Place where the Pilgrims are given Water from the Well of Zamzam.*

### II. DIVISION.
->which has Two Plates.<-

**PLATE XV.** **No. 1.** *Dervish, or Turkish Monk, who whirls out of Devotion.* <note>A reference to the Mevlevi Order, often called Whirling Dervishes.</note> **No. 2.** *Saka, a charitable Dervish, who brings water through the city to give away out of charity.* **No. 3.** *A Turk performing his prayer.* **No. 4.** *A Dervish from the Indies.*

**PLATE XVI.** *A Liver-seller* <note>original: "Dgi-Guerdgi", likely from the Turkish 'Ciğerci'</note>, *an Albanian, who carries sheep's livers to the Bedesten* <note>original: "Bezenſtein"; a central bazaar or market</note> *to feed the Cats, animals highly esteemed by the Turks.*

### III. DIVISION.
->which has 6 Plates.<-

**PLATE XVII.** **No. 1.** *Turkish Lover, who scars his arm before the eyes of his Mistress to prove to her the greatness of his Love.* **No. 2.** *Turkish Boy being led to Circumcision: It is usually required that a Boy who is to be circumcised be 13 years of age.* **No. 3.** *Tchingui, a Turkish Male Dancer.* **No. 4.** *Tchinguis, a Turkish Female Dancer.*

**PLATE XVIII.** *The Religious Dance of the Dervishes to the sound of the Flute & other Instruments.*

**PLATE XIX.** *Turkish Marriage.*

**PLATE XXI.** **No. 1.** *A Persian.* **No. 2.** *Persian Woman.* **No. 3.** *Clothing of the Leaders of Barbary.* <note>The Maghreb region of North Africa.</note> **No. 4.** *An Arab of the Desert.*

**PLATE XXII.** *Burial of the Turks of Constantinople.*

### PLATE II.
**A.** *Tallit* <note>original: "Taled"</note>, or white Veil, which the Jews place over their Heads at the Synagogue during their prayer, at the four corners of which hang the *Tzitzit* <note>original: "Sitsith"</note>, or Cord with 5 knots. **B. B.** *The left Arm*, seen from the inside and outside with the *Tefillin* <note>original: "Tefila"</note>. **C.** *Tefillin shel Yad* <note>original: "Tefila Schel-jad"</note>, or the Hand Tefillin, in large scale, with the Knot in the shape of the Hebrew Letter *Resh* <note>original: "Resch"</note>. ר. **D. D.** *Tefillin shel Rosh* <note>original: "Tefila Schel Rosch"</note>, or the Master Tefillin of the Head, seen from the front. **1.** seen from the Side **2.** **E. E.** *The Knots*, in the shape of the Hebrew Letter *Tet* <note>original: "Theth"</note> ט, behind the Head. **F.** A Jew covered by the *Tallit*, and wearing the *Tefillin* <note>original: "Tefilins"</note> on the Head and arm.

### PLATE III.
**A.** *Garment* <note>referring to the Arba Kanfot</note>, which the Jews used to wear, and which they now wear under their ordinary clothes, so as not to be mocked by those among whom they live. **B.** *The Tzitzit Cords*, which are at the four Corners. **C.** *One of the said Cords shown in large scale*, with 5 Knots, which signify the Name of God. **D.** *A Sabbath Lamp*. **E.** *Unleavened bread* called *Shmurah* <note>original: "Simurim"</note>, with which they perform the Passover. **F.** *Ordinary unleavened bread* called *Matzot* <note>original: "Mathroth"</note>, which they eat during the entire duration of the Festival. **G.** *Branches of Palm*, *Willow* & *Myrtle*, joined together, which they call *Lulav* <note>original: "Lulab"</note>, and which they hold in the left hand. **H.** *Citron* <note>the Etrog</note>, which they hold in the right hand: This is to bring these two things against one another, and to wave them toward the 4 Winds. **I.** *Shofar* <note>original: "Sophar"</note>, or ram’s horn, with which they sound on New Year's Day and other Festivals. **K.** *Mezuzah* <note>original: "Mezufa"</note>, *Staff*, or hollow cane attached to the right side of doorposts, in which is enclosed a parchment, whereon are written the Passages from Deuteronomy 6:4 and 11:13.

### PLATE IV.
**No. 1.** *The Priests descending from Aaron give the Blessing to the People.* <note>The Kohanim performing the Priestly Blessing.</note> **No. 2.** *Manner of exposing the Law to the eyes of the People, before beginning to read it*: The Buildings shown here represent the different facades of the Portuguese Synagogue at The Hague.

### PLATE V.
**No. 1.** *The Horn is sounded on the first day of the Year.* **No. 2.** *The Kippur* <note>original: "Chipur"</note>, *or Day of Atonement*, as it is celebrated among German Jews.

### PLATE VI.
**No. 1.** *The Search for Leaven*, or the Manner in which they collect and remove all leavened bread from the houses. **A.** *The Mistress of the house*, who places leavened Bread in various places, so that her Husband, who performs the search, may find some. **No. 2.** *The Passover Meal of the Portuguese Jews.* **1.** *The Dish*, where the Bone of a lamb’s shoulder is placed, with a hard-boiled Egg. **2.** *Dish*, containing the bitter Herbs. **3.** *Dish of Figs, Apples, Almonds, Cinnamon etc.* chopped and cooked together <note>Charoset</note>, to represent the Material from which their Fathers made Bricks in Egypt. **4.** *Dish of Sauce* to dip the bitter Herbs into. **5.** *Half of the Levites' Cake* <note>The Afikomen</note>, from which the Father of the Family breaks pieces to distribute to those at the Table: All Jewish Servants sit at the same Table as the Master and Mistress on this occasion. **6.** *Napkin* with which the Cake was covered. **7.** *Basket*, containing the *Matzot* or Passover Breads.

### PLATE VII.
**No. 1.** *Procession of the Palms among the Portuguese Jews.* **No. 2.** *Meal of the Jews during the Feast of Tabernacles.* <note>Sukkot</note>

### PLATE VIII.
**No. 1.** *Simchat Torah* <note>original: "Simcha-Tora"</note>, *or Joy for the Law*: **A.** *The Chatan Bereshit* <note>original: "Cadan Berescid"</note>. **B.** *The Chatan Torah* <note>original: "Cadan Tora"</note>: Both are Grooms of the Law, representing the Kings of Israel, who were obliged to begin and finish the Reading of the Law on the last day of the Feast of Tabernacles. The *Chatan Torah* is the one who finishes it, and the *Chatan Bereshit* is the one who Begins it. **C. C.** *Two Parnassim* <note>Synagogue wardens or leaders</note>. This Building represents the Portuguese Jews' Synagogue in Amsterdam. **No. 2.** *Manner of escorting the Grooms of the Law to their homes.* **A.** *The Chatan Torah*, on the day he finishes the Reading of the Law, is escorted back in the evening by the *Chatan Bereshit*, who is at his right, and a *Parnassin* at his left, followed by other *Parnassim*, Relatives & Friends, and escorted by several Sergeants. And on the day the *Chatan Bereshit* begins the Law, the *Chatan Torah* escorts him in turn, in the same order, with the difference that the *Chatan Bereshit* then occupies the middle place.

### PLATE IX.
**No. 1.** *Instruments used for Circumcision.* **1.** *Small Dish*, containing the Plasters. **2.** *Another Dish, containing Sand*, in which to put the Foreskin. **3.** *A Plaster.* **4.** *Vials, containing Astringent Powders.* **5.** *Knife, used for Circumcision.* **6.** *Instrument, used to hold the Foreskin.* **7.** *Band*, used to hold the plasters in place. **8.** *Small Silver rod*, upon which the Foreskin is drawn. **No. 2.** *The Book of the Law*: **A.** *Small Chest*, containing the Names of all the Members of the Synagogue, from which the names of seven People destined to carry and read the Law are drawn by lot: This small Chest is divided into 4 parts. The first marked **1.** contains the Names of the Levites. **2.** Is for placing the Names of the Levites as they have read. **3.** The Names of all the other Members of the Synagogue. **4.** Place for putting the Names of those among the latter who have already read. **B.** *Sefer Torah* <note>original: "Sepher Tora"</note>, or the Scroll of the Law opened. **C.** *The Scroll of the Law closed and wrapped in a very wide Ribbon.* **D.** *The Scroll of the Law with its Ornaments.* **E.** *The Rimonim*, or silver Ornament with Bells, which is placed on the two top extremities of the Scroll. **F.** *Pointer* <note>original: "Touche"; commonly known as a Yad</note>, which serves to guide the one who reads.

### PLATE X.

Selam. 4. Jesjab. 5. Hemaïssa. 6. Jafé. 7. Ader. 8. Ad. 9. Ednag, of rare Beauty. 10. Maad, a Great Captain, Enemy of the Jews. 11. # SECTION IV.
## containing 13 Plates.

**PLATE XXIII.** The Sultan <note>original: "Le Grand Seigneur"</note>, in ceremonial robes on the day of Bairam <note>The festival celebrating the end of the fasting month of Ramadan.</note>.
**PLATE XXIV.** The Haseki Sultan <note>original: "Sultane Affeki"</note>, or Queen Sultana.
**PLATE XXV.** The Mufti <note>The supreme legal authority and head of the religious hierarchy.</note>, Head of the Clergy and the Law of the Turks.
**PLATE XXVI.** No. 1. The Kazasker <note>original: "Cadi-Leskier"</note>, or Chief Justice, deputy to the Mufti. There is one for Europe <note>Rumelia</note> and one for Asia <note>Anatolia</note>; one or the other usually succeeds the Mufti. No. 2. Emir, a Man of the Law (highly respected and powerful among the Turks), of the lineage of Muhammad. No. 3. Effendi, or Man of the Law, in his studies. No. 4. Imam, Priest and Minister of a Mosque.

**PLATE XXVII.** Emir, and Chief of the Emirs descending from Muhammad, who holds absolute power over them, accompanied by his guards.

**PLATE XXVIII.** A. Small Ensign, or cavalry standard. B. Flag of the Janissaries <note>The elite infantry corps of the Ottoman Empire.</note>. C. Cavalry Standard. D. Flag of the Gunners. E. The Standard of the Grand Vizier. F. The Horsetail <note>original: "La Queüe de Cheval". A tugh, or standard used to denote military rank.</note>.

**PLATE XXIX.** No. 1. Prior of a Dervish monastery. No. 2. Deli <note>original: "Deli"</note>, a type of daredevil or jester who follows the Grand Vizier. No. 3. Various robes of the Dervishes. No. 4. A Santon <note>A term used by early European travelers to describe Muslim ascetics or monks.</note>, otherwise called a Qalandar <note>original: "Calender"</note>, or Abdal: A type of Turkish monk, described as vagabonds and debauched, who roam the country half-naked, covered in animal skins, and carrying a certain machine in the shape of a club in their hand, with which they perform all sorts of sleight-of-hand tricks.

**PLATE XXX.** No. 1. The Grand Emir of the Desert Arabs. No. 2. A Persian criminal who carries his prison with him—that is, an iron collar <note>original: "Carcan"</note> around his neck. No. 3. A type of traveling Dervish. No. 4. A monk of the Adhamite <note>original: "Edhemites"</note> order, a type of Persian hermit who leads a very severe life in their hermitages.

**PLATE XXXI.** Bairam, or the Muslim "Easter" <note>The author uses Christian terms to explain Islamic holidays to a Western audience.</note>, during which all sorts of public entertainment are held: some are suspended by ropes and swung through the air, others spin on a "wheel of fortune," etc. This festival corresponds roughly to our New Year ceremonies and celebrations; but because it immediately follows Ramadan <note>original: "Ramadhan"</note>, or the "Great Lent" of the Turks, it is compared to our Easter.

**PLATE XXXII.** No. 1. The Turkish Carnival, which takes place two months before the Lent of Ramadan, on the first night of the month of Ramadan <note>original: "Remesan"</note>, in memory of the descent of the Quran <note>original: "l'Alcoran"</note> from Heaven. The streets are illuminated and swarming with masked musicians, people on foot, on horseback, and mounted on camels, and especially a great number of Santons (mentioned above) who make a terrible racket with their dances, songs, shouts, and howls. In the midst of this populace appears Sheik-al-Arfat <note>original: "Scheikel-Arfat"</note>, or Prince of the Horned, riding a female donkey; he is highly respected by the Turks and receives great cheers. No. 2. The Festival of Hussein in Persia, in which they mourn the death of Hussein and Hasan <note>original: "Hassein"</note>, sons of Ali and Fatima, killed in a battle. Some appear half-naked and stained with blood; others are blackened to represent the heat and thirst of Hussein. All run through the streets together, twisting themselves in a hundred ways, making all sorts of faces, and shouting at the top of their lungs: "Hussein! Hasan!" Some dig holes in the earth, bury themselves up to the neck, and stay there all day with their heads covered by an earthen pot. Others finally appear in war gear to represent the battle in which Hussein lost his life. A preacher delivers a eulogy for the saint that lasts two hours, and these devotions are followed by the strangest debaucheries and all sorts of dissoluteness.

**PLATE XXXIII.** No. 1. Procession of the Sultan to display his magnificence to the ambassadors of the Great Mughal <note>The ruler of the Mughal Empire in India.</note>. No. 4. The Mughal Weighing Ceremony, on his birthday: this monarch, magnificently dressed, places himself on a gold scale decorated with diamonds to be weighed. The weights, covered in cloth, are also made of gold. It is proclaimed aloud how much he weighs, and it is recorded. If he weighs more than the year before, bonfires are lit and public celebrations are held.

**PLATE XXXIV.** No. 1. Silahdar Agha <note>original: "Selictar-Aga"</note>, or the Sultan's Sword-Bearer; he carries it in a red scabbard. No. 2. The Ibridar Agha <note>original: "Ibriédar-Aga"</note>, who carries the Sultan's basin and ewer. No. 3. A Solak, or Janissary guard, carrying his bow and a quiver full of arrows in his hands. No. 4. A Peyk <note>original: "Peïk"</note>, a page or footman.

**PLATE XXXV.** The Tent of the Grand Vizier. A. Place where a superstitious note was attached above the window. B. Talisman. 1. The Divan Tent, where audiences are given. 2. Retirement tent for the Grand Vizier. 3. Another retirement tent. 4. An enclosure for certain needs.
