# III. GENERAL PART.

## SECTION I.

->Concerning the Idolatrous Nations of the East Indies, primarily the Brahmins & the Banyans <note>The term "Banyan" was used by European travelers to refer to Hindu merchants, and often by extension to the Hindu population of western India in general.</note>, their Idols & their Ceremonies.<-

->This Section contains 13 Plates, with their Explanations.<-

The Plate which stands at the head of this third General Part, and which bears the motto: *Who does not know what kinds [of monsters] a demented [Egyptian worships]...* <note>original: "Quis nescit qualia demens &c." A quote from the Roman satirist Juvenal, frequently used by 18th-century European authors to frame non-Christian religions as irrational or "monstrous."</note> represents at the top, *the Creation of the World according to the System of the Banyans*, and at the bottom several Idols and Creatures that these people adore.

The German Inscription on this Plate reads: *Religious Ceremonies & Practices of the Idolatrous Nations &c. Engraved after the design of the Famous Picart <note>Bernard Picart (1673–1733), a French engraver whose monumental work "The Religious Ceremonies and Customs of All the Peoples of the World" sought to document global religions with unprecedented detail.</note>, by David Herrliberger, and accompanied by a Historical Explanation. Eight Sections, representing the various types of Idolatry of the Pagan Nations, 1748.* <note>original: "CIƆ IƆ CC XXXXVIII"</note>

**PLATE I. No. 1.** *A Brahmin <note>term: Brahmin. A member of the highest, priestly caste in Hindu society.</note> who has made a vow to wear an iron collar weighing 24 pounds and measuring 4 feet square, until he has collected enough money in alms to build a Hospital.* **No. 2.** *A Brahmin who, out of devotion, swings for half an hour in honor of the God Efwara <note>A variation of Ishvara, a title for the supreme lord, often associated with Shiva.</note> over a fire which he stokes with wood placed on both sides.* **No. 3.** *A Brahmin who has had himself chained by one leg to the foot of a tree with an iron chain, resolved to end his days in this state.* **No. 4.** *Two Brahmins of the Shudra <note>original: "Soudracs"</note> family, called Yogis <note>original: "Joguis"</note>, dragging behind them long and heavy iron chains which pass from the leg where they are attached over their shoulders.*

**PLATE II.** *Brahma <note>original: "Brama, ou Bruma"</note>, the first of the Three Principal Deities of the East Indians: His name signifies Wisdom. The Brahmins attribute to him the Creation of all things visible and invisible, of Men, and of as many Worlds as there are parts of his body, from each of which a world emerged, namely: 1. From his Brain, the first World above the Sky. 2. The second from his Eyes. 3. The third from his mouth. 4. The fourth from his left ear. 5. The fifth from his palate and Tongue. 6. The sixth from his heart. 7. The seventh from his belly. 8. The eighth from his private parts. 9. The ninth from his left hip. 10. The tenth from his knees. 11. The eleventh from his heels. 12. The twelfth from the toes of the right foot. 13. The thirteenth from the sole of the left foot: And 14. the fourteenth from the air that surrounds Brahma. They say further that there is a great Sympathy between these worlds and the limbs of Brahma, such that men of the different worlds have affections and inclinations that relate to the nature of Brahma’s different limbs.*

**PLATE III. No. 1.** *Ixora <note>Another name for Ishvara/Shiva.</note>, a Deity of the East Indies: This is an Image of Matter, which several Ancient & Modern Philosophers say is eternal and infinite.* **No. 2.** *Quenevadi <note>A transcription of Ganapati, or Ganesha.</note>, son of Ixora. This Idol, through his insatiability, likely represents Time.*

**PLATE IV. A.** *Pulleyar <note>Pillaiyar, a common name for Ganesha in South India.</note>, who presides over Weddings and Marriages.* **B. B.** *Superstitious and partially lustful signs and figures.* **C. C. C.** *On the nose.* **D.** *Double Lingam, or Figures representing the power of generation in Nature, which the Indians wear around the neck &c.* **E. E.** *Lingam, or Phallus, for the same use.* **F. F.** *Figures that the Missionaries tolerated among converted Pagans. All these Figures are of different colors, such as a. Red. b. White. c. Flesh-colored. d. Musk-colored. e. Brick-red. f. Sulfur-yellow. g. Black.*

**PLATE V. No. 1.** *First Incarnation or Metamorphosis of Vishnu <note>original: "Vistnou"</note>, or the Preserver of created things, into a Fish.* **No. 2.** *Second Incarnation or change into a Tortoise.* **No. 3.** *Third Incarnation into a Boar.* **No. 4.** *Fourth Incarnation into a Monster <note>The Man-Lion, Narasimha.</note>.*

**PLATE VI. No. 1.** *Fifth Incarnation into a Brahmin <note>The dwarf Vamana.</note>.* **No. 2.** *Sixth Incarnation into a Child called Parashurama <note>original: "Prasseram"</note>, who killed the Giant who, wanting to steal his Father’s white cow, had killed his Mother.* **No. 3.** *Seventh Incarnation into a Man named Ram <note>Rama</note>, who killed the Monster Ravana, assisted by the God of Monkeys <note>Hanuman</note>.* **No. 4.** *Eight Incarnation into the Child Krishna <note>original: "Kishna"</note>.*

**PLATE VII. No. 1.** *Ninth Incarnation of Vishnu, in the Figure under which the Indians usually adore him, under the name of Buddha <note>original: "Boudhé"</note>.* **No. 2.** *Tenth Incarnation into a winged white horse <note>Kalki</note>.* **No. 3.** *Ixora, under the name of Mahadeva <note>original: "Mahadeü"</note>, which signifies the supreme God. He is represented as a round Column rising to a point, which is likely the Emblem of Priapus <note>The Greek god of fertility; here the author is comparing the Lingam to Western classical mythology.</note> of the Ancients, who was regarded as the God of Nature. The Plate represents, beside this Idol, the interior of the Pagoda <note>A term used by early travelers for Hindu temples.</note>, the Adoration of the Idol, and all sorts of Monstrous Figures, acting as Indian Hieroglyphics.*

**PLATE VIII.** *The ten Incarnations or Metamorphoses of Vishnu <note>original: "Vistnou." The ten avatars (Dashavatara) of the god Vishnu, the Preserver in the Hindu trinity.</note> represented in another manner.*

**PLATE IX.** No. 1. *The Pagoda <note>In 18th-century travelogues, "Pagoda" was a general term used for any non-Christian temple in the East.</note> of Kamakshi <note>original: "Kamaetsma." Likely referring to the goddess Kamakshi, a form of Parvati/Shakti.</note>, Wife of Shiva <note>original: "Ixora ou d'Eswara," referring to Ishvara or Shiva.</note>*, in which, besides several monstrous figures, is represented the deception of the Priests. During the Festival of this Goddess, they place a Child near the Fruits that the People come to offer in abundance, next to the opening of an underground passage. At nightfall, they close the doors of the Pagoda, retrieve the Child through the tunnel along with the fruits and offerings, and return him the next day to his place, adorned with flowers. No. 2. *The Procession of Vishnu*, where the Image of the Idol is on horseback and carried on a platform. Torch-bearers precede the Idol, which is covered by a Parasol to provide shade and from which flies are chased away. For Entertainment, a goat or a fox is released, which the Indians try to strike down during its flight with sticks held in their left hands.

**PLATE X.** No. 1. *A Penitent Brahmin woman, who always remains in the same posture*. A Devotee accompanied by her servant comes to recommend herself to the woman's prayers. No. 2. *Another Penitent woman*, who remains in a very constrained Posture, which she is not permitted to change. No. 3. *A Penitent*, a. who holds his arms raised in the air until death. b. The Posture in which such a Penitent sleeps, without ever lowering his arms. No. 4. *A Brahmin Pilgrim*.

**PLATE XI.** No. 1. *Religious Penitents of the Sect of the Yogis <note>original: "Joguis." Hindu ascetics or practitioners of yoga, often viewed by Europeans of this era as "fakirs" or extreme "penitents."</note>*. No. 2. *Two other Yogis*, who make incisions upon themselves. No. 3. *A Yogi, who feeds a Peacock out of devotion*. No. 4. *Brahmins, who feed birds out of devotion*.

**PLATE XII.** No. 1. *The Procession of Ganga <note>The personification of the river Ganges.</note>*, and the manner in which one fulfills the vows made to this Divinity in times of illness or on other occasions. No. 2. *The Festival of Holi <note>original: "Huly."</note>*, a kind of Carnival in India, where all sorts of ridiculous or monstrous Masks are seen running through the streets.

## SECTION II.

-> Concerning the same Banyans etc. in the Deccan, Golconda etc. Concerning the Peguans, the Siamese, the Tonkinese, the Moluccans, and the Sinhalese: Their Idols, Customs & Ceremonies. <-

-> *This Section contains 15 Plates, with their Explanations.* <-

**PLATE XIII.** *Various Pagodas & Penances of the Fakirs <note>term: Fakir. Derived from the Arabic for "poor," this term was used by Europeans to describe both Muslim Sufis and Hindu wandering ascetics.</note>*. 1. The Great Banyan Tree. 2. Pagoda of the Idol *Mamani-va*: On one side, those who come to offer prayers are marked on the forehead with vermillion; on the other, a *Brahmin* receives offerings of rice, etc. 3. Pagoda of *Ram*. 4. Another Pagoda dedicated to *Ram*. 5. Pagoda where the penitent Fakirs retreat. 6. A kind of Coffin or pit where a Fakir retreats several times a year, and where he receives light only through a small opening. 7. A Fakir who sleeps leaning on a rope. 8. Fakirs who remain in this posture their entire lives: Women give them food out of charity. 9. Several *Brahmins* whom women invoke and consult as Saints. 10. Various postures in which several *Fakirs* remain for several hours a day. 11. *A Brahmin* who has his mouth and nose covered, for fear of killing some small insect while breathing or swallowing it: This is also why he sweeps before him, to clear away worms or other insects upon which he might step. <note>This description specifically refers to the practice of Ahimsa (non-violence) common in Jainism, though here attributed to a Brahmin.</note> 12. *Fakirs* warming themselves. 13. *A Fakir* who feeds Animals out of charity.

**PLATE XIV.** No. 1. *Ceremony observed among the Banyans at the birth of Children*. A. The Mother offers her breast to the Infant. B. The Infant, having refused the breast, is exposed under a tree, or placed in a cloth attached by the four corners to the branches of the tree. C. The Infant, continuing for three days to refuse the breast, is thrown into the Ganges. <note>This likely describes a ritual test of a child's health or "spirit" rather than a universal practice.</note> No. 2. *Ceremony of naming a Child among the Banyans*: The Infant is placed in a bedsheet scented with rice by the Brahmin present, and twelve Children holding the sheet by the edges shake and roll the Infant from side to side for a quarter of an hour, until finally his sister bestows upon him such a Name as she pleases.

**PLATE XV.** No. 1. *The manner in which Women burn themselves in India with the bodies of their deceased Husbands <note>The practice known as Sati.</note>*. No. 2. The manner in which others bury themselves alive with the bodies of their Husbands.

**PLATE XVI.** No. 1. *A Sick person presented to Shiva <note>original: "Ixora."</note>*, to obtain a cure. No. 2. *A dying Sick person*, who receives the Urine of a Cow upon his face. Another dying person being plunged into the Ganges.

**PLATE XVII.** *A solemn Festival of Pegu <note>Modern-day Bago, Myanmar.</note>, called Sapan Giacche*: A kind of Pilgrimage that the King and Queen, with the Principals of the Court, make twelve Leagues from their Residence, mounted on a chariot so magnificent and so rich in Pearls and precious Stones that its brilliance is dazzling.

**PLATE XVIII.** No. 1. *The Water Festival of the Peguans*, at which the King, the Queen, the Nobility, the Men of War, and all the People baptize one another—the former with rose water, and the others with common water. <note>This refers to the Buddhist New Year water festival, known as Thingyan in Myanmar.</note> No. 2. *Funeral ceremonies of the Peguans for their deceased King*. First they dance

dance around the Body, then afterwards they carry it onto a pyre, built in the form of a scaffold made of fragrant wood, and set upon two boats fastened together, to burn it upon the water, while the *Talapoins* <note>A term used by early European travelers to describe Buddhist monks in Thailand and Burma.</note> perform their chants and prayers. The Dead of the common people are carried on a bier and placed on the lit pyre without many Ceremonies.

**PLATE XIX.** No. 1. *Sommonacodom* <note>A transcription of "Sammanakodom," the Thai name for the historical Buddha.</note>, the famous Divinity of the *Siamese*, formerly a man of singular Holiness and extraordinary strength, of the order of the *Talapoins*. No. 2. *Another representation of this Idol.* No. 3. *Another.* No. 4. *Talapat*, the Parasol of the *Siamese* Religious men, called *Talapoins*: It is a weave of Palm leaves, which these Religious men carry constantly.

**PLATE XX.** No. 1. Convent of the *Talapoins*, in the center of which is the Temple, surrounded by several Pyramides: The Cells are separated from one another, and each forms a private hut. No. 2. Stones in the shape of a Mitre, which are seen near the Pagodas. No. 3. Parasols *of honor*, which the King of *Siam* gives to the *Sancrats* <note>High-ranking Buddhist prelates or abbots.</note>, or Priors of the Convents.

**PLATE XXI.** No. 1. *Sommonacodom* surrounded by *other Idols*, which represent his Disciples. No. 2. *A type of Picture*, which is held in veneration among the *Siamese*, and perhaps resembles votive tablets <note>original: "Tabulae votivae"; these are offerings displayed in a consecrated place to fulfill a vow.</note>. No. 3. *Other Idols of the Siamese.* No. 4. The Temple of *Barkalam* <note>Likely referring to the temple associated with the Phra Khlang, the Siamese minister of foreign trade.</note> in *Siam*. A. The Building, which is properly the Temple of *Barkalam*. B. Another Pagoda, in which is found the *Sommonacodom* No. 1. The Idol is of pure cast gold, and worth at least twelve and a half million French Livres. It was placed there first, and the Pagoda was built around it.

**PLATE XXII.** No. 1, 2, 3. Pyramids of several styles and *other carved Stones*, which are seen in *Siam* around the Pagodas, and which may also be called Altars, since Sacrifices are made there.

**PLATE XXIII.** *Funeral pomp of the Kings of Tonkin <note>Northern Vietnam.</note>.* 1. Two Ushers carrying maces. 2. Twelve Officers dragging the Mausoleum, where the Name of the deceased King is written. 3. Twelve Elephants, which the King was accustomed to lead to war. 4. The Grand Equerry and two Pages on horseback, followed by twelve led horses, which sometimes precede the Elephants. 5. The Body of the King drawn by 8 Stags. 6. The New King and his Brothers. 7. The Princesses and Ladies of Honor, who carry food for the deceased. 8. The Princes of the Blood surrounded by instrumentalists. 9. Four Governors of the 4 principal Provinces, carrying bags of gold and incense on their backs. 10. Two chariots with eight horses, filled with gold, silk fabrics, etc. 11. A crowd of the King's officers, and Nobility, some on horseback, others on foot, according to their ranks and qualities. A. Galley, upon which the body of the King is embarked. B. Galley, where the Lords are, who go to be buried alive with the King. C. Galley with the Ladies, who go to be buried alive with the King. D. Two Galleys, which carry the treasures that are to be buried with the King. This entire Fleet follows the river, which passes through deserts, to a secluded and very distant place, where the King and all those who wish to keep him company are buried: The Secret is entrusted only to six Eunuchs, who are made to take an Oath that they will never discover the Place of the Burial.

**PLATE XXIV.** A. *Hieroglyphic Ornaments of the Islanders of the Moluccas.* B. *Abraxas of the Basilidians* <note>Gnostic charms or gems associated with the followers of Basilides.</note>, similar to these Ornaments. 1, 2, 3. *Mamakurs*, or Bracelets, which relate to the Necklaces of *Canada* <note>A comparison likely referring to wampum or indigenous beadwork.</note>.

**PLATE XXV.** No. 1. *Moluccans*, who play Instruments, such as for example the *Rabana* <note>A traditional frame drum.</note>. No. 2. *Various Musical Instruments of the Moluccans*.

**PLATE XXVI.** No. 1. First Wedding Ceremony of the Peoples of *Java*, where the Groom goes to fetch the Bride. No. 2. Second Wedding Ceremony of the Peoples of *Java*, where the Groom leads the Bride to his home.

**PLATE XXVII.** No. 1. The Divinity who, according to the *Sinhalese*, gives Wisdom, Health, Wealth, etc. No. 2. The Tutelary God of the Island of *Ceylon*.

**PLATE XXVIII.** No. 1. *Xekia* <note>Shakyamuni Buddha.</note>, the Idol of the *Chinese*. No. 2. *Another Representation of Xekia*, otherwise called *Fo* <note>The Chinese word for Buddha.</note>.

**PLATE XXIX.** The Gods of the *Chinese*, taken from *Kircher's China* <note>Referring to "China Illustrata" (1667) by the Jesuit scholar Athanasius Kircher.</note>. *Fo* or *Fé*, the Supreme Being among the *Chinese*, whose Name means *Savior*, is represented with his hands hidden, to show that his Power operates all things in an invisible manner. He has at his sides the two Principal Reformers of the Religion of the *Chinese*, namely on his right the Philosopher *Confucius*, who taught one single God, and on his left *Lanzu* <note>Laozi, the founder of Taoism.</note>, which means the old Philosopher, and who also recognized and taught only one supreme being. D. D. D. Are Deified Philosophers. E. Captain, placed in the Rank of the Gods. F. *Dragon*, covered in a Turtle shell, also representing the God *Fo*. These are the Divinities of the first order. Those of the second order G, G, G, H. are Sons of Mars the God of war, and Doctors of this

**PLATE VIII.** The ten Incarnations or Metamorphoses of <term>Vishnu</term> <note>One of the principal deities of Hinduism, known as the Preserver, who returns to earth in various forms to restore cosmic order.</note> represented in another manner.

**PLATE IX.** No. 1. The Pagoda <note>A term used by early travelers for a Hindu temple or the idols within.</note> of *Kamakshi*, Wife of *Ishvara* or *Eswara* <note>Referring to the goddess Parvati/Kamakshi and the god Shiva.</note>, in which, besides several monstrous Figures, is represented the deception of the Priests, who at the Festival of this Goddess place a Child near the Fruits that the People come to offer in abundance, beside the Opening of a subterranean passage; at nightfall, they close the doors of the Pagoda, take the Child home through the passage along with the fruits and offerings, and return him the next day to his place, adorned with flowers. The Festival of <term>Holi</term> <note>original: "Huly"; the Hindu spring festival known for the throwing of colored powders.</note>, a kind of Carnival in the Indies, where one sees all sorts of ridiculous or monstrous Masks running through the streets.

->Of the same <term>Banians</term> <note>A term historically used by Europeans to refer to Hindu merchants or Hindus in general.</note> etc. in the Deccan, Golkonda etc. Of the Peguans, the Siamese, the Tonkinese, the Moluccans, and the Sinhalese: Of their Idols, Customs & Ceremonies.<-

**PLATE XIII.** Various Pagodas & Penances of the <term>Fakirs</term> <note>In this context, Hindu ascetics or wandering monks.</note>. 1. The great Banyan Tree. 2. Pagoda of the Idol *Mariamman* <note>original: "Mamaniva"; likely a reference to a local mother goddess.</note>: On one side, those who come to offer prayers are marked on the forehead with vermillion; on the other, a Brahmin receives offerings of rice, etc. 3. Pagoda of *Rama*. 4. Another Pagoda dedicated to *Rama*. 5. Pagoda where the penitent Fakirs retreat. 6. Fakirs who remain in this posture their entire lives: Women give them food out of charity. 9. Several Brahmins, whom women invoke and consult as Saints. 10. Various postures in which several Fakirs remain for several hours a day. 11. A Brahmin who has his mouth and nose covered, for fear of causing some small insect to die by breathing or swallowing it: This is also why he sweeps before him, to push away worms or other insects upon which he might step <note>This describes the practices of Jain monks, though the text identifies him as a Brahmin.</note>. 12. Fakirs warming themselves. 13. A Fakir who feeds Animals out of charity.

**PLATE XIV.** No. 1. A Ceremony observed among the Banians at the birth of Children. A. The Mother presents her breast to the Infant. B. The Infant, having refused the breast, is exposed under a tree, or placed in a cloth attached by the four corners to the branches of the tree. C. The Infant continuing for three days to refuse the breast is thrown into the Ganges. No. 2. The Ceremony of giving a Name to a Child among the Banians: The Child is placed in a bedsheet perfumed with rice by the Brahmin present, and twelve Children holding the sheet by the edges shake and roll the child from side to side for a quarter of an hour, until finally his sister bestows upon him such Name as she pleases.

**PLATE XV.** No. 1. The manner in which Women burn themselves in the Indies with the bodies of their deceased Spouses <note>Referring to the historical practice of Sati.</note>. No. 2. The manner in which others bury themselves alive with the bodies of their Spouses.

**PLATE XVI.** No. 1. A Sick person presented to *Ishvara* to obtain a cure. No. 2. A dying Patient who receives Cow Urine upon his face <note>Part of the "Panchagavya" purification rituals.</note>. Another dying person being immersed in the Ganges.

**PLATE XVII.** A solemn Festival of Pegu <note>Modern-day Myanmar/Burma.</note>, called *Sapan Giacche*: A kind of Pilgrimage that the King and Queen, along with the Principal members of the Court, make twelve Leagues from their Residence, mounted upon a chariot so magnificent and so rich in Pearls and precious Stones that its brilliance is dazzling.

**PLATE XVIII.** No. 1. The Festival of Waters of the Peguans, in which the King, the Queen, the Nobility, the Men of War, and all the People baptize one another, the former with rose water and the others with common water <note>Likely the Thingyan water festival.</note>. No. 2. Funeral ceremonies of the Peguans for their deceased King. First they dance

dance around the Body, then afterwards they carry it onto a pyre, made in the form of a scaffold of fragrant wood, and set upon two boats tied together, to burn it on the water, while the Talapoins <note>A historical term for Buddhist monks in mainland Southeast Asia.</note> perform their chants and prayers. The Dead of the common people are carried on a bier and placed on the lit pyre without many Ceremonies.

**PLATE XIX.** No. 1. *Sommonacodóm* <note>A transliteration of Samanagautama, the historical Buddha.</note>, famous Divinity of the Siamese, formerly a man of singular Holiness and extraordinary strength, of the order of the *Talapoins*. No. 2. *Another representation of this Idol*. No. 3. *Another*. No. 4. *Talapat*, Parasol of the Siamese Religious, named *Talapoins*: It is a Fabric of Palm leaves, which these Monks carry constantly.

**PLATE XX.** No. 1. *Convent of the Talapoins*, in the center of which is the Temple, surrounded by several Pyramids: The Cells are separated from each other, and each forms a private hut. No. 2. *Stones in the shape of a Mitre*, which are seen near the Pagodas. No. 3. *Parasols of honor*, which the King of Siam gives to the Sancrats <note>A term for high-ranking Buddhist prelates or priors.</note>, or Priors of the Convents.

**PLATE XXI.** No. 1. *Sommonacodóm surrounded by other Idols*, which represent his Disciples. No. 2. *A kind of Painting, which is held in veneration among the Siamese, and perhaps resembles the* votive tablets <note>original: "Tabulæ votivæ"; small tablets or plaques left as an offering to a deity.</note>. No. 3. *Other Idols of the Siamese*. No. 4. *The Temple of Barkalam in Siam*. A. The Building, which is properly the Temple of *Barkalam* <note>Likely referring to a temple associated with the Phra Khlang, the Siamese minister of foreign trade.</note>. B. Another Pagoda, in which is found the Sommonacodóm No. 1. The Idol is of pure cast gold, and at least of the value of twelve and a half million French Livres. It was placed there first, and the Pagoda was built around it.

**PLATE XXII.** No. 1, 2, 3. *Pyramids of several styles and other carved Stones*, which are seen in Siam around the Pagodas, and which can also be called Altars, since Sacrifices are made there.

**PLATE XXIII.** *Funeral Procession of the Kings of Tonkin* <note>original: "Tunquin"; a historical name for northern Vietnam.</note>. 1. Two Ushers carrying maces. 2. Twelve Officers dragging the Mausoleum, where the Name of the deceased King is written. 3. Twelve Elephants, which the King was accustomed to lead to war. 4. The Grand Equerry and two Pages on horseback, followed by twelve led horses, which sometimes precede the Elephants. 5. The Body of the King pulled by 8 Deer. 6. The New King and his Brothers. 7. The Princesses and Ladies of Honor, who carry food for the deceased. 8. The Princes of the Blood surrounded by instrument players. 9. Four Governors of the 4 principal Provinces, carrying bags of gold and incense on their backs. 10. Two chariots with eight horses, filled with gold, silk fabrics etc. 11. A crowd of the King’s officers, and of the Nobility, some on horseback, others on foot, according to their ranks and qualities. A. Galley, upon which the King’s body is embarked. B. Galley, where the Lords are, who are going to be buried alive with the King. C. Galley with the Ladies, who are going to be buried alive with the King. D. Two Galleys, which carry the treasures, which are going to be buried with the King. All this Fleet follows the river, which passes through deserts, until a secluded and very distant place, where the King and all those who wish to keep him company are buried: The Secret is entrusted only to six Eunuchs, who are made to take an Oath that they will never discover the Burial Place.

**PLATE XXIV.** A. *Hieroglyphic Ornaments of the Islanders of the Moluccas*. B. *Abraxas of the Basilidians, similar to these Ornaments* <note>Abraxas refers to mystical symbols or gems associated with the early Gnostic sect of the Basilidians.</note>. 1, 2, 3. *Manakurs, or Bracelets, which relate to the Necklaces of Canada*.

**PLATE XXV.** No. 1. *Moluccans, playing Instruments, such as for example the Rabana* <note>A traditional Southeast Asian drum.</note>. No. 2. *Various Musical Instruments of the Moluccans*.

**PLATE XXVI.** No. 1. *First Wedding Ceremony of the Peoples of Java, where the Groom goes to seek the Bride*. No. 2. *Second Wedding Ceremony of the Peoples of Java, where the Groom leads the Bride* to his home.

**PLATE XXVII.** No. 1. *The Divinity, who according to the Sinhalese* <note>original: "Chingulais"</note> *gives Wisdom, Health, Goods etc.* No. 2. *The Tutelary God of the Island of Ceylon*.

# SECTION III.
## Of the Chinese.

->This Section contains XIV Plates with their Explanations.<-

**PLATE XXVIII.** No. 1. *Xekia* <note>A transliteration of Shakyamuni, another name for Buddha.</note>*, the Idol of the Chinese*. No. 2. *Another Representation of Xekia, otherwise called Fo* <note>The Chinese word for Buddha.</note>.

**PLATE XXIX.** The Gods of the Chinese, taken from the *China Illustrata* of Kircher <note>Referring to the 1667 work by Athanasius Kircher.</note>. **Fo** or **Fé**, the Supreme Being among the Chinese, whose Name means *Savior*, is represented with hidden hands, to show that his power operates all things in an invisible manner. He has at his sides the two Principal Reformers of the Religion of the Chinese, namely at his right the Philosopher *Confucius*, who taught a single God, and at his left *Lanzu* <note>Laozi, the founder of Taoism.</note>, which means the old Philosopher, and who also recognized and taught only one supreme being. D. D. D. *Are Deified Philosophers*. E. *Captain, placed in the Rank of the Gods*. F. *Dragon, covered with a Tortoise shell, still representing the God Fo*. These are the Divinities of the first order. Those of the second order G, G, G, H. are *Sons of Mars* <note>The Roman god of war used here to describe a Chinese deity of similar function.</note> *God of war, and Doctors of this trade*.

trade. I. K. L. M. Divinities of the third Order, established over sublunary things, of which one governs Water, another the Earth, another Fire, etc.

**PLATE XXX. No. 1.** *Puzza* <note>A corruption of *Pusa*, the Chinese term for Bodhisattva, specifically referring here to Cundi or the multi-armed Guanyin.</note>, or the *Cybele* of the Chinese. She has 16 arms, which carry Knives, Swords, Halberds, Books, Fruits, Flowers, Plants, Wheels, Vases, etc. This signifies, according to the explanation of the Chinese, that she is the Mother of all the Gods: and the more the Egyptians, Romans, and Chinese wished to represent the virtue, fertility, and productions of Nature through these Hieroglyphic Idols, the more these Divinities can be viewed as being but one, which produces all things: in a word, Nature. **No. 2.** A wild *Puzza* similar to that of *Isis*, seated on the *Lotus* flower, by which the other became pregnant and brought into the world a powerful Legislator.

**PLATE XXXI. No. 1.** *Idol*, which the Chinese call the *God of Immortality* <note>Likely Shoulao, the Star God of Longevity.</note>, and whom they have preside over their Fortune. **No. 2. A.** *Fotek*, or *Nimifo* <note>Likely representations of the Buddha (Fo) or Amida (Omituofo).</note>, Goddess of Voluptuousness. **B.** *Kin-Gan*, or according to the Jesuit Fathers *Chin-Hoan* <note>Chenghuang, the City God who protects the walls and moats of a city.</note>, Tutelary God, perhaps the aforementioned Captain placed among the rank of the Gods. **No. 3.** Another *Chin-Hoan*, Tutelary God of China. **No. 4.** *Divinity*, whom the Dutch in their Accounts call *Lincing*.

**PLATE XXXII. No. 1.** *Isis & Osiris*, with the *Lotus* flower on their heads, in the form of serpents. **No. 2.** *Isis* seated on the *Lotus* flower. **No. 3.** *Isis* nursing her Son *Horus*. **No. 4.** *Isis* with a Cow's head, nursing *Horus*.

**PLATE XXXIII. No. 1.** *Vitek* or *Nimifo*, the aforementioned Goddess of voluptuousness, worshiped in a Pagoda. The Chinese have her preside over impure Pleasures as well as innocent Pleasures, despite the fact that they count her among the rank of the *Xins* <note>Shen, meaning spirits or deities.</note>, or Holy Spirits. **No. 2.** *Matzu*, another Divinity of the Chinese, otherwise called *Neoma* <note>Mazu, the patron goddess of sailors, also known as Niangniang.</note>, who according to some was a Magician, and according to others a perfectly chaste virgin. She has two Devotees at her sides equipped with fans to provide her with wind. On both sides of this Temple appears the Image of *Hujumfim*, a famous Alchemist who invented the Philosopher's Stone and delivered the People from a Furious Dragon, which he chained to a pillar that is still shown today.

**PLATE XXXIV. No. 1.** *Quantecong* <note>Possibly a reference to Fuxi or the Yellow Emperor, though the description of a hero of prodigious strength suggests Guan Yu.</note>, a Chinese Divinity, whom they say was their first Emperor, and to whom they attribute the invention of various arts, the establishment of several useful Laws, the introduction of clothing, policing, and the form of their Government. Thus he appears not in the form of an ordinary man, but in that of a Hero of prodigious Stature and Strength: Behind him stands his Page *Lincheu*, a Black man <note>Likely a reference to Zhou Cang, Guan Yu's dark-complexioned attendant.</note>, who yielded nothing to his Master in Strength and Courage. **No. 2.** *Quonin* <note>Guanyin, the Bodhisattva of Compassion.</note>, a domestic Divinity of the Chinese, who lived as a Solitary and performed a great many miracles.

**PLATE XXXV. No. 1.** *Chinese Religious, in black*, with a Rosary in the manner of Roman Catholics. **No. 2.** *Chinese begging Religious, dressed in a habit of several Colors*, and covered by a hat so wide that it serves him as a Parasol. These Monks sit along the roads and ask for alms by striking a piece of wood against a bell. **No. 3.** *Chinese Religious in yellow*, with their rosaries, of a second order of the Sect of *Lanzu* <note>Laozi, the founder of Taoism.</note>, always walking two by two. **No. 4.** *Devout beggar*, whose head has been formed into a point, sitting on the high road and having a large Rosary hung from his neck: The Chinese look upon these People as great Saints.

**PLATE XXXVI. No. 1.** *Lamas*, Priests of the Tartars <note>The Manchus or Mongols.</note>. **No. 2. A.** *Begging priest of China*. See the preceding Plate. **B.** *Chastisement of an unchaste Priest*. **C.** *Punishment of another*, for having abandoned the Monastic life: He is locked in a wooden cage, lined on the inside with iron spikes, such that he cannot lean anywhere without wounding himself.

**PLATE XXXVII. No. 1.** *Devout beggar*, who strikes his head against a stone to obtain alms. **No. 2.** *Devout beggar*, who has incense or other drugs burned upon his head until he is given charity. **No. 3.** *Religious in Penance for having been surprised with a woman*. His Neck is pierced with a hot iron, and through this opening a nail is passed, from which hangs a chain ten toises <note>Approximately 64 feet or 19.5 meters.</note> long, which the wretch is obliged to drag through the city until he has collected a certain sum of money for his Convent. Another Religious follows him everywhere and gives him lashes of a whip every time he tries to put his hand to the chain to provide himself relief.

**PLATE XXXVIII. No. 1.** *Pagoda of China* <note>A term used historically by Europeans to refer to any Far Eastern temple.</note>. **1.** The Entrance or the great Gate of the Temple. **2.** Screen wall touching on both sides large pillars of stone or wood, upon which are placed Statues of Giants <note>Dvarapala or Temple Guardians.</note>, armed and dressed in the Chinese fashion. This screen hides the interior of the Pagoda, so that one can see nothing of what happens in the Sanctuary, even though the Gate is open. **3.** A large stone vase, in which Incense is burned. **4.** The middle, or the Interior of the Pagoda, where the Devotees assemble. **5.** Table before the Altar furnished with incense, offerings, and two candlesticks. It is there that one also sees the *Bamboo*, or the large Tube, in which there are several other smaller ones <note>Fortune-telling sticks, or *kau cim*.</note>, and upon which one reads Predictions: Also Vases, in which are placed the instruments of sacrifice. **6.** Altar of Idols. **7.** Two other Altars of Idols to the right and left of the great Altar. **8.** The Devotees, who perform their Devotions. **9.** Place of the pillars, upon which the Roof rests. **10.** Two reservoirs of water to the right and left between the Forecourt or the anterior part of the Pagoda and the Pagoda itself properly so-called. Fish are kept there: through these two openings and through the Gate enters the little daylight that is in the Pagoda. **11.** The Place of two rooms or chapels filled with Idols. **12.** Tables, on the wall of the Pagoda, upon which are prescribed the Ceremonies to ob

to observe when casting the Lot <note>A divination practice, likely *kau chim*, where sticks are shaken from a tube to determine one's fortune.</note>. 13. Chinese drum on a table. 14. Chinese bell suspended against the wall of the Pagoda <note>The term "Pagoda" was frequently used by early European travelers to refer to any East Asian temple, not just the tiered towers.</note>, of coarse workmanship, which is sounded by striking it with a wooden hammer; this is done, as with the drum, five or six times after the Devotees have finished their prayer before the Idol. 15. A. External wall around the Pagoda. B. B. The Gates. No. 2. Great Pagoda of China. 1. The Great Gate. 2. Small Gates to the right and left of the great one. 3. The Courtyard. 4. Water Reservoirs similar to those of Pagoda No. 1, except that here they are located between the Courtyard and the Gate of the Pagoda. 5. The Bridge, which passes between the two Water Reservoirs. 6. Hall, which touches the Courtyard but is more elevated than it. 7. Another Hall, even larger, paved with Marble, but without a roof, like the Temples of the ancient Orientals. 8. Rooms or Chapels to the right and left of the Halls, as in the other Pagoda. It was in these Rooms that the Emperors and the Lords of their Retinue formerly washed themselves before presenting themselves before the Gods. 9. \* Stone vase, in the form of a holy water font, as in the other Pagoda. Besides Incense, they also burn painted Papers torn into pieces, which certain Devotees come to offer to the Gods. In some Pagodas, instead of these stone vases, one also finds two or three artistically worked Braziers. 9. \*\* Devout worshippers. 9. Third Hall, even larger than the first two, and higher, corresponding roughly to the choirs of our Churches. 12. Columns and Pillars of the Building. 13. Doors of the rooms, in front of which there are galleries, and through which one must pass to go into the rooms. 14. Table, like that of the other Pagoda, where besides the two candlesticks one also sees other suspended Lamps and other censers. 15. The Altar of Idols. 16. Two stone Tables, furnished with Idols, and surrounded by worshippers. 17. Tables suspended from the walls with inscriptions and the regulations of the Lot, as in the other Pagoda. 18. The Bell. 19. The Drum. 20. Bench for the Parasols etc., as in the other Pagoda.

**PLATE XXXIX.** No. 1. *Charlatan Monks*, who involve themselves in selling the Wind in China. They usually go in pairs, one of whom carries with great gravity on his right shoulder a Bag full of this alleged wind, which he sells for money to those simple enough to buy it. These impostors also usually carry wooden hammers in their left hands, with which they often strike the Earth to make emerge, they say, the Genius or Spirit of the Wind, who then passes, according to them, through the Air seated in human form upon a Bird. No. 2. *Devout Beggars*: Also *Charlatans of China*, who walk about mounted on Tigers without bridle or chain, after having tamed them: They usually have in their retinue devout Beggars and Penitents, who strike their heads against one another with great Devotion.

**PLATE XL.** No. 1. *Magicians and Sorcerers of China*. No. 2. *Other Magicians*, who live in deserts and in caves, telling fortunes, giving Counsel and Philters <note>Magic potions, particularly those intended to cause someone to fall in love.</note> to those who come to see them, and working on the Philosopher’s Stone <note>A legendary substance in alchemy capable of turning base metals into gold and granting immortality.</note>, and who for all this are regarded as great Saints.

**PLATE XLI.** *Funeral Procession of a Chinese Noble*, where the coffin covered by a Canopy is preceded by a great number of young Boys and young Girls carrying Flowers and Images of Men, Women, Animals etc., to be burned in honor of the Deceased. The Priests carry large and heavy censers, and are followed by Musicians and Charlatans on foot and on horseback, who perform all sorts of feats of skill. After the coffin walk the Children of the Deceased on crutches, as a sign of their sadness. The Wives of the Deceased follow the Mourning carried in sedan chairs, and they are followed by other female relatives, and a crowd of people.

## SECTION IV.

->Of the Japanese, the Tartars, and the Lapps.<-

->*This Section contains XVII Plates, with their Explanations.*<-

**PLATE XLII.** No. 1. *Tiedebaïk* <note>Likely a reference to Daijizaiten (Shiva), often depicted in Japanese syncretic traditions.</note>, Divinity of Japan, richly adorned with gold and precious stones: She has the Head of a Pig, crowned with a golden Crown set with Diamonds, and four arms, one of which carries a Scepter, another a Flower, the third a Dragon’s head, and the fourth a Ring. She tramples underfoot a hideous Monster, which one could rightly call the Devil. One also sees there, as in the following Figure, the various ways of worshipping and incensing this Idol. The true meaning of this Idol is unknown. No. 2. *Amida* <note>Amitābha, the principal Buddha in Pure Land Buddhism.</note>, another Divinity of Japan, who is meant to represent the Supreme Being. To him is attributed the preservation and deliverance of Souls from merited punishments: Here he presents himself on the altar mounted on a seven-headed horse, with the Head of a dog, and a ring in the mouth to represent the course of Time, or perhaps Eternity, each horse head signifying a thousand Centuries. This Idol is magnificently adorned in gold and precious stones. No. 3. *Another Representation of the same Idol Amida*, with the various ways of drowning oneself in his honor: One places oneself, in the Hope of a
<meta>catchword: further</meta>
<sig>F</sig>

<meta>This page continues the description of Japanese religious practices, specifically focusing on ritual suicide related to the deity Amida, followed by detailed explanations of plates depicting various Japanese gods, temples, and mythical creatures.</meta>

-> ⁂ ) ( .0 ) ( ⁂ <-

...[obtaining] an even richer reward in the Paradise of **Amida** <note>Amida refers to Amitābha, the Buddha of Infinite Light, central to Pure Land Buddhism.</note>. On a small boat, weighted down with heavy stones around the neck, arms, and body, and after having music played for several hours, they cast themselves into the water. Others prepare for several days with long discourses on the vanity and nothingness of the world and all earthly goods. Both types, after having enjoyed themselves for the last time with their relatives and friends, are accompanied by them to the boat. There are some who drill holes in the bottom of the boat to drown themselves in this manner, etc. Here **Amida** appears with a woman’s face and large earrings; in other places this Idol appears in the form of a young man, and sometimes as a man with three heads.

### PLATE XLIII.
**N. 1. Pagoda of Canon, Divinity of Osaka**, where the preceding Idols are also found, along with the following one. This edifice has three stories: one ascends it by a long staircase. The windows are barred, so that the Devotees perform their prayers outside the grilles, through which they observe the Idols. The surrounding walls are decorated with images, and the location of the Temple beside a grove is very pleasant. **N. 2. Canon**, a divinity of Japan, according to some accounts the **Son of Amida** <note>Canon is Kannon (Avalokiteśvara), the Bodhisattva of Compassion. In European accounts of this era, Bodhisattvas were often misidentified as "sons" or "daughters" of Buddhas.</note>, presides over the water and the fish, and created the Sun and the Moon. She is represented with four arms, crowned with flowers, and submerged in a fish up to the waist. The first arm holds a scepter, the second a flower, the third a ring, and the fourth is raised in the air with a closed fist. Opposite this Idol, one sees the image of a man who, enclosed from below in a snail shell, acts as a petitioner before **Canon**. At some distance from there appear four figures on an altar, hands joined in a posture of supplication, and fountains flow from their fists. **N. 3. Another Representation of Canon**, with seven heads on the chest and thirty hands all armed with arrows. This is how he appears in the Temple of the Thousand Idols <note>A reference to the Sanjūsangen-dō in Kyoto, famous for its 1,001 statues of Kannon.</note>. **N. 4. Xantai, a completely new Divinity of Japan**, or the Emperor **Nobunanga** <note>Oda Nobunaga (1534–1582), a powerful warlord who famously demanded to be worshipped as a living god (Kami) at Sōzuchi-yama.</note>, who during his lifetime attributed divinity to himself, having built a magnificent temple on a hill. He had the principal Idols of the entire Empire brought there and placed himself on a pedestal much higher than the others, with an express prohibition against worshipping any other Divinity but his own.

### PLATE XLIV.
**N. 1. Toranga, God of Japan**, who from a hunter became Emperor, and who after his death was placed among the rank of the **Camis** <term>Kami: Shinto spirits or deities</term> or Blessed Spirits, and finally among the Gods. He is represented fighting a tyrant with an axe, supported by eight kings, and is thus depicted with eight armed arms. This reflects what he did in his lifetime, having delivered the Empire from a formidable tyrant, whom he tramples underfoot in the form of a fire-breathing serpent <note>Toranga refers to Tokugawa Ieyasu, the first Shogun of the Edo period, deified as Tōshō Daigongen.</note>. **N. 2. Pagoda of Toranga**, with a square roof, upon the four corners of which—as on all other **Mias** <term>Miya: A Shinto shrine</term> or Temples of Japan—there are as many gilded oxen. The wall of this one is adorned with figures of the ancient **Camis** or Demi-Gods. **N. 3. Japanese Preacher** on a pulpit almost similar to those in our churches. He has at his side the Tutelary God of his Sect or Order, to whom sacrifices are made. There are lamps hanging from both sides of the pulpit's canopy. At the base of the pulpit is the place where the young priests stay, some sitting, others standing. The Preacher is covered by a very large hat like a parasol and carries a fan in his hand. With a small bell at his side, he gives a warning from time to time to be attentive. The book from which he takes his text is a collection of maxims, either of morality or the doctrine of his sect; there are travelers who assert that one often hears fine pieces of eloquence and piety there.

### PLATE XLV.
**N. 1. The Supreme Divinity**, who according to the Japanese created the world, seated on twelve cushions in the manner of the country, on the trunk of a great tree floating on the water of a pond, carried by a turtle. This Creator is black like a Moor, and wears a crown on his head rising to a point. He has a bare chest and short, thick hair like Negroes. He carries a ring in one hand, a scepter in the other, a flower in the third, and in the fourth a vase from which water flows. All this undoubtedly has its meaning. The idol is of gold, as is everything it carries, as well as the tree trunk, and its clothes are trimmed with precious stones. It is from this tree trunk, according to the Japanese, that the Creator drew the primary matter of all things. Two monsters or devils on one side, and three kings on the other—one of whom is a **Sin** or Demi-God, and another of whom has four faces for having lived 4,000 years—pull at a serpent that has wrapped itself twice around the trunk to prevent the creation of the world. The Sun emerges from the water up to the waist in the form of a bearded man of middle age, and he pricks the turtle with a sting, his left hand full of these types of instruments. **N. 2. Xaca, another Divinity of Japan**, and even one of the foremost, also called **Fotoge** <note>Xaca is Shaka (Shakyamuni Buddha); Fotoge is "Hotoke," a term for a Buddha or a deceased soul.</note>. This is precisely the **Xekia** of the Chinese. **N. 3. Pagoda of Monkeys**, in the middle of which is the large image of a monkey on an altar furnished with offerings and a copper basin, upon which the **Bonze** <term>Bonze: A Buddhist monk</term> or priest strikes with an instrument to awaken the devotion of the people or to remind them of certain duties. On both sides of this Master Monkey, there are many others, each on its pedestal, with an offering before it. All this worship is symbolic and is based on **Metempsychosis** <term>Metempsychosis: The transmigration of souls or reincarnation</term>.

### PLATE XLVI.
**N. 1. A. Kirin of the Chinese. B. Kirin of the Japanese**, a monster of extraordinary gentleness and sanctity, which only reveals itself under certain constellations and at the birth of a **Sefin** <note>Sefin likely refers to *Seijin*, a sage or holy man.</note>, that is to say, a man useful and intelligent in the mysteries, etc. **N. 2. Tats, Dragons of the Chinese and Japanese**: the first has five claws, and the other has three. This animal is...

...maritime. N. 3. *Imperial Banners and Marks of Honor*, which the Emperor grants to Great Lords. N. 4. *A. Foo*, or the Phoenix of the Chinese. *B. Foo*, or the Phoenix of the Japanese: It only appears at the birth of a *Sefin* <note>original: "Sefin"; likely referring to a "Shengren" or Sage-king</note>, like the *Kirin* <note>The Qilin, a mythical hooved chimerical creature in East Asian cultures</note>, or on the eve of an extraordinary event.

**PLATE XLVII.** N. 1. *The Pagoda of the Bull*. The Bull seen there on a large square Altar is of pure gold, wearing a richly adorned collar around its neck, and it pushes with its horns an egg floating in the water of a hollow rock. By this, the Japanese, like many other Pagans, understand the *Chaos* from which the World was drawn; and by the Bull, they understand the Supreme Being, because in the Hebrew Language one of the Names for the Ox is also given to God <note>This likely refers to the Hebrew letter 'Aleph,' which originally represented an ox, or the term 'Abir,' meaning 'mighty one,' used for both bulls and God.</note>. N. 2. Pagoda of the Divinity *Daybot* <note>Daibutsu, or "Great Buddha."</note>. It has a Portal upon which stand, on either side, two armed Monsters with several arms, facing one another. After this Portal, there is a large square Courtyard with galleries, and after this Courtyard another Portal adorned with two lions carved in stone, through which one enters directly into the Pagoda. Those seen entering on the Plate are the *Dutch Ambassador* with his Retinue, accompanied by the Japanese. N. 3. *Daybot, Japanese Divinity*, one of the Principal ones of the Empire, and as the name implies, *the Great Goddess* <note>The author misidentifies the Daibutsu (Great Buddha) as a goddess, likely due to the soft features of the statue.</note>. It is perhaps the same thing as the *Amida*, or the *Budhu* <note>Buddha</note>, that is to say, the Supreme Being considered according to some of its particular properties. The Idol is seated in the Oriental manner upon an Altar Table, quite low. It is of enormous size, rising nearly to the Vault of the Temple: one of its hands is the thickness of a man of average height. It is made of wood, coated in plaster, and covered in gilded copper. Its Face and Breast show that it is a Woman, and its hair is short and thick like that of Negroes <note>A common historical observation of the "snail-shell" curls (ushnisha) typical of Buddha statues.</note>. It is entirely surrounded by a Ring of golden Rays, in which the Images of many *Camis* <note>Kami, the spirits or deities of Shinto.</note> are presented. To the right and left there are many other Images still, all crowned with Rays, much like those of the Saints among Christians, and at the foot of the Grand Altar there are several lit lamps.

**PLATE XLVIII.** *Temple of a Thousand Idols*, in Japan. In the middle of the Temple stands the Great Idol of enormous size, with pierced Ears, a bald Head, and a shaved Chin, like a Brahmin. Above the Idol, from the Canopy that covers it, hang five small bells. To the right and left of the Idol are the Images of many armed People, dancing Negroes, Magicians and Sorceresses, Demons, and even the Winds and Thunder. The surrounding Walls are lined with a thousand crowned Idols, all similar to the Canon <note>The Kannon (Avalokiteśvara) at the Sanjūsangen-dō temple in Kyoto.</note>, that is to say, with 30 arms and 7 heads on the chest. All these Idols, and all the ornaments of the Temple, are of fine gold. If one were to count all the Figures in this Temple, the number would far exceed a thousand.

**PLATE XLIX.** <insert>Quamvon</insert> <note>Kannon</note>, who resembles the Idol *Putza* <note>Pusa, the Chinese term for Bodhisattva.</note> in the number of its arms. Two of its arms are raised in the air and carry Children. Six small Children surround the Head of *Quamvon* in the form of a Crown of Rays. One Child stands above the head of the *Quamvon*, and yet another Child is seated upon the Head of that one. The Idol rests, like nearly all Japanese Divinities, upon a *Tarate* or sea flower <note>Likely a corruption of the Sanskrit 'Hridaya' or simply referring to the Lotus throne.</note>. This Figure is said to represent the principal appearances of the *Amida* and what he invented that was useful for the happiness of the human race. It is such that the various arms of an Idol always indicate the various qualities and operations of the Divinity represented by it.

**PLATE L.** N. 1. *Givvon* <note>Gion-tenjin, associated with disease prevention.</note>, a domestic Divinity who preserves one from various evils, and in particular from smallpox, which carries away many Children in Japan. N. 2. *Jebis* <note>Ebisu</note>, the Neptune of the Japanese. N. 3. *Daikoku*, the Plutus <note>The Greek god of wealth.</note> of the Japanese. N. 4. *Tossitoku* <note>Toshitoku</note>, the Divinity who presides over Fortune.

**PLATE LI.** N. 1. *Darma* <note>Bodhidharma, the founder of Zen Buddhism.</note>, a Saint of Japan, Son of a King of the Indies, who in his lifetime had hardly any equal. He is placed at the beginning of the sixth Century, at which time he preached to the Chinese, confirming his Doctrine by an extraordinarily severe life, so much so that having once allowed himself to be overcome by Sleep, he cut off his two eyelids (which is why he appears here without eyelids); these were subsequently changed into two small bushes, where Tea grows, unknown until then. This Saint is represented standing on a Reed in the middle of the waters, for it is thus, it is said, that he crossed seas and rivers. N. 2. *Rosaries* of Japan according to the different Sects of the Country: The first is that of the *Seodosju* <note>Jōdo-shū</note> Sect; the second that of the *Sensju* <note>Zen-shū</note> Sect; the third that of the *Fakmanhen* <note>Hokke-shū / Nichiren</note> Sect; and the fourth that of the *Ikosju* <note>Ikkō-shū</note> Sect. The Japanese and Chinese count their prayers on them, which are much longer than those of Christians. N. 3. & 4. *Biosju* <note>Ihai</note>, or Tablets for the Dead, which the Japanese place at the entrances of their houses, just as in the Netherlands mortuary Tables <note>Hatchments or 'rouwborden'</note> are posted above the Doors of Persons of Distinction, with the arms of the Deceased, his Name, his titles, the year and day of his death, etc.

**PLATE LII.** N. 1. *Nuptial Ceremony of Japan*. The Groom and the Bride go, each separately with their Parents, out of the city toward a certain Hill. The Groom is followed by carts full of presents to be given to the Parents of the Bride. One ascends the Hill by stone Stairs fashioned expressly for this Ceremony, and they place themselves under the Tent of the God of Weddings, the Groom entering from one side and the Bride from the other. Only the Parents and Musicians come there; the former place themselves behind the Bride, and the latter behind the Groom, all outside the Tent, and the rest of the Convoy remains at the bottom of the stairs. The Idol before which the newlyweds present themselves is on an altar under the Tent, and it has a Dog's head, as a sign of the fidelity that is mutually owed in the state of marriage. It also holds a Cord in its hands to indicate...

->( ) ( 0 ) (<-

the strength and necessity of the Conjugal Bond: Between the Married Couple stands the **Bonze** <note>A term historically used by Europeans to refer to Buddhist monks in East Asia.</note> or Priest who blesses the Marriage: Beside the Tent, torches burn, at one of one of which the Bride lights a torch, which she holds in her hand: While lighting it, she pronounces the words that the Bonze dictates to her; She then presents it to the Groom, who also lights his torch from it: This done, the entire company lets out cries of joy and congratulations, and a fire is lit at the bottom of the hill, into which the Bride's playthings are thrown: Others present her with a **distaff** <note>A tool used in spinning wool or flax, symbolizing domestic labor.</note> laden with Flax, to remind her of the household cares she is about to enter. A Sacrifice of two Bulls for the God of Marriage ends the Ceremony; the procession returns to the city, and the Bride is brought to the Groom's house. N. 2. Funeral Ceremony of Japan: The Procession is led by the Bonzes, who carry lit torches: There is singing and the playing of Instruments. The Relatives of the deceased and others walk before the Body, the men with extinguished torches and the women veiled. The Body follows on a bier, as if on a bed of State, in the posture of a man praying. The pyre toward which they walk, and which is set in a hollow, is covered with a white cloth and surrounded by walls: Beside this pyre are Tables laden with meats, braziers in the form of censers, etc. After circling the pyre three times, the Body is placed upon it, while the Bonzes and the Relatives of the deceased invoke his Tutelary God. The Bonze, who had been at the Head of the Procession, then takes a lit torch, and after touching the head of the deceased with it three times and circling the pyre three times, he throws the torch before the feet of the Relatives, or the youngest of the Sons of the deceased, who picks it up and throws it into the hollow where the pyre is, which is copiously furnished with oil, incense, and other combustible drugs, which, by catching fire, ignite the pyre itself. While the pyre burns, the Relatives fill the censer on one of the Tables with incense and scented wood and return home. Only a few Bonzes and the Poor remain there, the former to say their prayers, and the latter to eat what is on the tables. The next day, the ashes and bones of the deceased are gathered into precious Urns, which are buried covered with copper plates or Stones carved into different shapes, upon which are marked the Names of the Deceased and the God he worshipped. Persons of quality also have marble Statues erected over the tombs, upon which are engraved the principal actions or offices of the Deceased, as well as the day of his Birth and that of his Death, which bears a resemblance to our Monuments and Epitaphs. Often, the Image of the Deceased himself is placed there, carved in marble or stone. Furthermore, we wished to insert on the same plate the Figures of two Divinities of the *Japanese* who preside over the Dead. One, named *Jene*, has four faces and as many arms; in one she holds a sun, in another a crown of flowers, in the third a branch, and with the fourth she points her finger at a vase of incense. The Sun must represent Divine Providence, etc. This Divinity protects married Persons and the elderly. The other Divinity, named *Xiquami*, has oversight of Souls and Young people: She is represented as a young and beautiful Woman with four arms; in one she carries a Child, in another a Serpent, in the third a Saber, and in the fourth a knotted Ring. Her dress is scattered with stars: It is unknown what the Parrot at her side is meant to signify. When some great lord dies, it often happens that several of his Vassals, Subjects, and Slaves kill themselves to serve their Lord in the next world as well.

**PLATE LIII.** *N. 1. The Festival of Souls in Japan*: Toward evening, people go out of the City to speak to the Dead: they bring them food in their tombs: they invite them to come into the city, and in the belief that they are coming, they lead them there with lit torches. *N. 2. The manner in which the Souls are led back to their tombs on the third day*: They take leave of them by throwing stones, which everyone picks up along the streets and paths, to throw after them and drive them out of the city, for if even one remained, it would be the greatest of misfortunes.

**PLATE LIV.** *N. 1. A. Han, deified King of the Tartars. B. A Lama, who says his prayers, while another C. Turns a Cylindrical Instrument on its hub.* <note>A description of a Tibetan Buddhist prayer wheel.</note> N. 2. Trophies raised on the highest Mountains, which the *Lamas* go to worship for the Preservation of Men and horses, etc. N. 3. The Image of *Confutius* <note>Confucius</note>, as seen in public Colleges, and in the *Hutangs* or *Sutens* <note>Likely referring to 'Hutong' (shrine/hall) or 'Siyuan' (temple/monastery) areas.</note> of the Chinese. This Figure relates to what is reported of the cult of *Confutius* in the article concerning him. N. 4. The *Amida* of the *Japanese*: This Figure is placed here relative to the Divinities of the Chinese and Tartars, between which there is much similarity.

**PLATE LV.** N. 1. *Buth*: This is a young and furious Tartar, who kills everyone he meets. N. 2. *Manipu*: Divinity of *Lassa* <note>Lhasa, Tibet</note>, to whom those killed by *Buth* are offered. She appears here in the ordinary form of a woman, but with ten Heads in the shape of a Pyramid: It is especially in the Kingdom of *Tanchut* <note>Tangut, or the Western Xia region</note> that they try to make this Divinity propitious by these kinds of violent victims.

**PLATE LVI.** N. 1. *Jumala*, a Divinity that the *Lapps* <note>The Sami people of Northern Scandinavia.</note> formerly worshipped as the Supreme God. He is seated on an Altar in human form. The idol was made of wood, having a vase on its knees, in which the offering was placed. N. 2. *Wirku-Accha*. N. 3. *Thoron*. N. 4. *Stor-Junkare*, Divinities of the *Lapps*: The Idols are very crude and poorly fashioned, the first two of wood, whose upper end seems to represent a human head, and the other of stone. *Thoron*, or *Thor*, has a nail in his head, from which hangs a small stone, so that he might make fire whenever it pleases him. He is on a scaffold instead of an altar, and because this Sanctuary also somewhat resembles a Temple, it is surrounded by trees and bushes. He is the Supreme God of the *Lapps* of today.

# SECTION V.

->Of the Guebres, or Persian and African Sorcerers.<-
<note>The term "Guebre" (or Gaure) was historically used by Europeans to refer to Zoroastrians in Persia and India (Parsis).</note>

->*This Section contains XII Plates, with their Explanations.*<-

PLATE LIX. *N. 1. Temple of Fire. N. 2. & 3. Sepulchres of the Guebres, or Persians, who have kept their ancient Magical Religion, which they derive from Zoroaster.* <note>Zoroaster was the ancient Persian prophet who founded Zoroastrianism.</note> These Sepulchres are round stone Towers, 35 feet high, and 90 in Diameter, without doors. Dead bodies are exposed on these towers to the air and birds of prey, so that they may devour them, and when there are more than the space can contain, they are lowered through a hole in the middle of the Tower, and hung inside against the wall: All the bones remaining from the corpses fall into a very deep pit at the bottom of the tower. <note>These are known as Towers of Silence or Dakhmas, designed to prevent the "impurity" of the dead from contaminating the earth or fire.</note>

PLATE LX. *N. 1. High Priests of the Guebres or Persians before the fire, holding his ritual in hand, and his loins girded with a belt with 4 knots, to remind him of the four Precepts of his Religion, which he must never forget. N. 2. 3. 4. Three different Heads of Guebre Priests with the Miter and the mouth covered.* <note>The mouth cover, or Padan, is worn to prevent the priest's breath from polluting the sacred fire.</note>

PLATE LXI. *N. 1. Nuptial Ceremony of the Parsis or Guebres;* The newlyweds are seated together on a bed: Two *Hyrbads* <note>A rank of Zoroastrian priest.</note> or Priests stand before them, one for the Groom and the other for the Bride, with the Parents of the newlyweds at their sides holding hands full of rice, which is the emblem of fertility. The Priest of the Groom, placing his finger on the Bride's forehead, asks her: *Do you want this man here present to be your Husband?* The Priest of the Bride does the same regarding the Groom: Thereupon the newlyweds give each other their hands, the Groom gives some gold pieces to the Bride, and the assembly throws rice upon them, while the Priests and Parents bless them and pray for them: This entire Ceremony is performed before the Fire. *N. 2. Baptisms by water and by Fire, of the Guebres.* After a *Daroo* <note>A term for a priest or religious figure.</note> or Religious man has made the Child's Prognostic by contemplation of the Stars of its birth, the Mother gives it a Name, and then it is carried to the Temple, where the Priest sprinkles it with pure water, which he carries in the bark of a tree named *Holm* <note>Likely the Holm Oak; certain plants were held sacred for purification.</note>, praying to God for the purification of the Child, and to sanctify it, he holds it for some time over the fire. When the Child has reached the age of 7, it is brought back to the Temple to be confirmed: Two Persons hold it over the fire with the mouth and nose tied with a cloth, for fear of profaning this Sacred Element with its breath, and in this posture it must recite some prayers that the Priest has taught it. After this Ceremony, the Priest gives it water to drink and Pomegranate bark to chew, to purify it internally as well: He then puts it in a Tub of water and washes its whole body: This done, he dresses it in the *Soudra* <note>The Sudreh, a sacred white shirt worn by Zoroastrians.</note>, which is a reddish garment with wide sleeves, and puts on a belt of wool or goat hair, which passes twice around the body and is knotted at the back.

PLATE LXII. *N. 1. Parsi or Guebre in agony, whose Soul is received by a Dog. N. 2. Funerals of the Parsis.* Before exposing the dead body to the Birds, it is laid on the ground, and a piece of bread is put in its mouth: One of the deceased's Relatives brings a dog as close to the body as possible, but without touching the dog: The closer the dog approaches, the more it is esteemed that the deceased is close to his happiness, and if the dog jumps as far as the body to take the piece of bread from its mouth, it is a certain sign that the deceased is currently among the blessed: After the trial of the dog <note>This ritual is known as Sagdid, where the gaze of a dog is believed to ward off evil spirits from the corpse.</note>, two *Daroos* say a half-hour prayer in great haste, two hundred paces from the bier, with their hands devoutly folded: During this Ceremony, the deceased wears a paper on each ear, which hangs three fingers below the chin: Finally, he is carried to the designated place, and the entire procession follows two by two, with hands joined.

PLATE LXIII. *N. 1. Religious Ceremonies of the People of Guinea in honor of their Divinity.* A Priest accompanied by two women performs his magic Conjuration under a tree consecrated to the Divinity, named *Fetiche* <note>The term 'Fetish' originates from the Portuguese word for a charm or sorcery; it refers to an object believed to have supernatural powers.</note>, which responds there in the form of a dog sitting at the base of the tree: The Tree is decorated with some bands of straw. *N. 2. Another Ceremony to ask for rain. N. 3. Another Ceremony to circumcise a Child. N. 4. Their Commemoration of the Dead.*

N. 2. *Funeral Ceremony of Japan:* The procession is led by the Bonzes, who carry lit torches: There is singing and the playing of instruments. The relatives of the deceased and others walk before the body, the men with extinguished torches and the women veiled. The body follows on a bier, as if on a bed of state, in the posture of a man at prayer. The pyre toward which they walk, which is set in a hollow, is covered with a white linen cloth and surrounded by walls: Beside this pyre are tables furnished with meats, burners in the form of censers, etc. After circling the pyre three times, the body is placed upon it, while the Bonzes and the relatives of the deceased invoke his Tutelary <note>A guardian or patron deity.</note> God. The Bonze who had been at the head of the procession then takes a lit torch, and after touching the head of the deceased with it three times and circling the pyre three times, he throws the torch before the feet of the relatives, or the youngest of the sons of the deceased, who picks it up and throws it into the hollow where the pyre sits; this area is copiously furnished with oil, incense, and other combustible substances, which, upon catching fire, ignite the pyre itself. While the pyre burns, the relatives fill the censer on one of the tables with incense and scented wood and return home. Only a few Bonzes and the poor remain there—the former to say their prayers, and the latter to eat what is on the tables. The next day, the ashes and bones of the deceased are collected into precious urns, which are buried covered with copper plates or stones carved in various shapes, upon which are marked the names of the deceased and the God they worshipped. Persons of quality also have marble statues erected over their tombs, upon which are engraved the principal actions or offices of the deceased, as well as the date of their birth and death, which bears a resemblance to our own monuments and epitaphs. Often, an image of the deceased themselves, carved in marble or stone, is placed there. Furthermore: we wished to include on the same plate the figures of two Japanese deities who preside over the dead. One, named *Jene*, has four faces and as many arms, in one of which she holds a sun, in another a crown of flowers, in the third a branch, and with the fourth she points her finger toward a vase of incense. The sun is meant to represent Divine Providence, etc. This deity protects married persons and the elderly. The other deity, named *Xiquami*, has oversight over souls and young people: She is represented as a young and beautiful woman with four arms, in one of which she carries a child, in another a serpent, in the third a saber, and in the fourth a knotted ring. Her garment is sprinkled with stars: It is unknown what the parrot at her side is meant to signify. When some great lord dies, it often happens that several of his vassals, subjects, and slaves kill themselves to serve their lord in the other world as well. <note>This refers to the practice of *junshi*, or "following the lord in death."</note>

**PLATE LIII.**
N. 1. *The Feast of Souls in Japan:* Toward evening, people go out of the city to speak to the dead: they bring them food in their tombs: they invite them to come into the city, and in the belief that they are coming, they lead them there with lit torches. <note>This describes the *Obon* festival, a Japanese Buddhist custom for honoring the spirits of ancestors.</note>
N. 2. *The manner in which souls are escorted back to their tombs on the third day:* They take leave of them by throwing stones, which everyone picks up along the streets and paths, to throw after them and drive them out of the city; for if a single one remained, it would be the greatest of misfortunes.

**PLATE LIV.**
N. 1. *A. Han, a deified King of the Tartars.* <note>"Tartar" was a broad European term for the Mongolic and Turkic peoples of Central and Northern Asia.</note> *B. A Lama, who says his prayers, while another* *C. turns a cylindrical instrument on its axis.* <note>A description of a Tibetan Buddhist prayer wheel.</note>
N. 2. *Trophies erected on the highest mountains, which the Lamas go to worship for the preservation of men and horses, etc.*
N. 3. *The image of Confucius, as seen in public colleges, and in the Hutangs or Sutens of the Chinese.* <note>The translator uses "Hutang" and "Suten" to phonetically describe Chinese halls or temples.</note> This figure relates to what is reported regarding the worship of Confucius in the article concerning him.
N. 4. *The Amida of the Japanese:* <note>Amida Buddha, the principal deity of Pure Land Buddhism.</note> This figure is placed here in relation to the deities of the Chinese and the Tartars, among whom there is much commonality.

**PLATE LV.**
N. 1. *Buth:* This is a fierce young Tartar who kills everyone he meets.
N. 2. *Manipa: A deity of Lhasa, to whom those killed by Buth are offered.* <note>Likely a reference to a multi-headed form of the Bodhisattva Avalokiteshvara, whose mantra is *Om Mani Padme Hum*.</note> She appears here in the ordinary form of a woman, but with ten heads arranged in the shape of a pyramid: It is especially in the Kingdom of *Tanchut* <note>The Tangut Empire of Western Xia.</note> that they seek to make this deity propitious through these kinds of violent victims.

**PLATE LVI.**
N. 1. *Jumala, a deity whom the Laplanders formerly worshipped as the Supreme God.* <note>Jumala is the word for "God" in Finnish and related Samic languages.</note> He is seated on an altar in human form. The idol was made of wood, having a vase on its knees in which offerings were placed.
N. 2. *Wirka-Accha.*
N. 3. *Thoron.*
N. 4. *Stor-Junkare, deities of the Laplanders:* The idols are very crude and poorly fashioned; the first two are made of wood, with the upper end appearing to represent a human head, and the other is made of stone. *Thoron*, or *Thor*, has a nail in his head with a small stone hanging from it, so that he might make fire whenever he pleases. He is set upon a scaffold instead of an altar, and so that this sanctuary might resemble a temple even slightly, it is surrounded by trees and bushes. He is the Supreme God of the Laplanders of today, for <note>original: "car"</note>

for they say he is the Master of Thunder, & they attribute to him absolute Power over Men & Spirits. The wooden Hammer he holds serves to punish impious Men & Demons. *Stor-junkare* <note>A title meaning "Great Lord," referring to a high-ranking deity in Sami mythology, often seen as a lieutenant to the thunder god.</note> is a lesser God than Thor, and in some way his Lieutenant: *Junkare* signifies a Governor or Lieutenant: It is through his Channel that Men obtain all the good they receive: He is also made a God of the Chase <note>Hunting</note>, and a Tutelary <note>Protective</note> God: Each family has its own.
