# SECTION V.
## Of the Guebres, or Persian & African sorcerers.
### *This Section contains XII. Plates, with their Explanations.*

PLATE LIX. *N. 1. Fire Temple. N. 2. & 3. Sepulchers of the Guebres <note>Historically used to refer to Zoroastrians.</note>, or Persians, who have kept their ancient Magical Religion, which they derive from Zoroaster.* These Sepulchers are round stone Towers <note>Known as Dakhmas or "Towers of Silence."</note>, 35 feet high, & 90 in Diameter, without doors. The dead bodies are exposed on these towers to the air & to birds of prey, so that they may devour them, & when there are more than the place can contain, they are lowered through a hole, which is in the middle of the Tower, & they are hung by the inside to the wall: All the bones that remain from the corpses fall into a very deep pit, which is in the bottom of the tower.

PLATE LX. *N. 1. High Priests of the Guebres or Persians before the fire, having his ritual in hand, & his loins girt with a belt with 4 knots, to remind him of the four Precepts of his Religion, which he must never forget. N. 2. 3. 4. Three different Heads of Guebre Priests with the Mitre & the mouth covered <note>A ritual mouth-cover called a padam, used to prevent the breath from polluting the sacred fire.</note>.*

PLATE LXI. *N. 1. Nuptial Ceremony of the Parsis or Guebres; The newlyweds are seated together on a bed: Two Hyrbads <note>Zoroastrian priests.</note> or Priests present themselves before them, one for the Groom & the other for the Bride, having at their sides the Parents of the Newlyweds who have hands full of rice, which is the emblem of fertility.* The Priest of the Groom, putting his finger on the Bride's forehead, asks her: 
> Do you wish for this man here present to be your Husband?
The Priest of the Bride does the same regarding the Groom: Thereupon the Newlyweds mutually give each other their hands, the Groom gives some pieces of gold to the Bride, & the assembly throws rice upon them, while the Priests & the Parents bless them & pray for them: All this Ceremony is performed before the Fire. *N. 2. Baptisms by Water & by Fire, of the Guebres.* After a *Daroo* <note>A priest or religious teacher.</note> or Religious man has made the Prognostic of the Child by contemplation of the Stars of his birth, the Mother bestows the Name upon him, & then he is carried to the Temple, where the Priest sprinkles him with pure water, which he carries in the bark of the tree named *Holm* <note>Likely the Holm Oak.</note>, praying to God for the purification of the Child, and to sanctify him he holds him for some time over the fire. When the Child has reached the age of 7 Years, he is brought back to the Temple to be confirmed: Two People hold him over the fire with his mouth & nose tied with a cloth, for fear of profaning this Sacred Element with his breath, and in this posture he must recite some prayers, which the Priest has taught him. After this Ceremony the Priest gives him water to drink, & Pomegranate bark to chew, to purify him also internally: He then puts him in a Vat of water, & washes his whole body: This done, he clothes him in the *Sondra* <note>The Sudreh, a sacred white undergarment.</note>, which is a reddish garment with wide sleeves, & puts on him a belt of wool or goat hair, which passes twice around the body and is knotted at the back.

PLATE LXII. *N. 1. Dying Parsi or Guebre, whose Soul is received by a Dog <note>The "Sagdid" ritual, where a dog's gaze is believed to confirm death and ward off demons.</note>. N. 2. Funerals of the Parsis.* Before exposing the dead body to the Birds, it is laid on the ground, & a piece of bread is put in its mouth: One of the relatives of the deceased brings a dog as close to the body as he can, but without touching the dog: The closer the dog approaches, the more it is esteemed that the deceased is close to his felicity, & if the dog jumps as far as onto the body, to take the piece of bread from its mouth, it is a certain mark that the deceased is currently among the blessed: After the trial of the dog, two *Daroos*, very hastily, two hundred paces from the bier, perform a half-hour prayer, hands devoutly folded: During this Ceremony the deceased wears a paper on each ear, which descends three fingers below the chin: Finally he is carried to the destined place, & the whole procession follows him two by two, hands joined.

PLATE LXIII. *N. 1. Religious Ceremonies of the Peoples of Guinea in honor of their Divinity.* A Priest accompanied by two women performs his magic Conjuration under a tree consecrated to the Divinity, named *Fetish* <note>Original: *Fetiche*.</note>, which responds in the form of a seated dog at the foot of the tree: The Tree is adorned with some bands of straw. *N. 2. Another Ceremony to ask for rain. N. 3. Another Ceremony to circumcise a Child. N. 4. Their Commemoration of the Dead.*

### PLATE LXIV.
N. 1. *Manner in which the Negroes <note>The term used historically in the text to describe the people of West Africa.</note> of Guinea take their Oath*: namely, by bringing their face close to the right foot of the person asking for the assurance. N. 2. *Manner in which Women justify themselves against an accusation of adultery*: a woman must swear by her Divinity and drink a certain beverage, which it is believed will cause her death if she has sworn falsely. N. 3. *Punishments of the Peoples of Guinea*. The criminal is struck down with a club, while at the same time a spear is passed through the body from behind: his head is then cut off, which the Relatives take away to hang beside their hut: the body is cut into 4 parts and carried away in pieces; after this, the Women mourn the Deceased.

### PLATE LXV.
N. 1. *Manner of greeting one another in the Morning in Guinea*. N. 2. *Their manner of marrying*: The Man simply gives his hand to the woman who pleases him, but she promises him much more in return. N. 3. *Ceremony of childbirth*. N. 4. *The woman who has given birth bathes her Child in the River*.

### PLATE LXVI.
N. 1. *Ceremony observed at the birth of Children among the Cafres* <note>A historical term used by early travelers to refer to various Southern African peoples, likely the Xhosa or Khoikhoi in this context.</note>. a. *The Purification of newborn Children with cow dung, and their exposure to the air to dry*. b. *Exposure of Children born with some defect*. Some are simply thrown into the dump <note>original: "la voirie"</note>, and others are buried halfway. N. 2. *Marriage of the Cafres*. The Relatives form two circles, one of men and the other of women: In the first, the Groom presents himself, and in the other, the Bride stands in the middle: The one who blesses their marriage sprinkles water three times over the newlyweds, and then pronounces a Blessing over them in his own fashion.

### PLATE LXVII.
N. 1. *Religious Honors that the Cafres render to the Moon*. This Ceremony is properly practiced at the full Moon and lasts all night with a great noise: Some dance, let out cries, and clap their hands: others consider the Moon with admiration, either murmuring or singing unknown words aloud. Others cover their faces or hide in Caves: Often this Ceremony is accompanied by other extraordinary gestures, such as when they throw themselves on the ground, only to rise soon after to admire the Moon or to cry out at the top of their lungs:
> We salute you: Give us milk and honey: Increase our Cattle etc.

or finally, when they rub each other's foreheads with red earth etc. N. 2. *Religious Honors that the Cafres render to a Beetle <note>original: "Haneton," referring here to a species of scarab or cockchafer beetle.</note>*. When this insect appears (which they regard as a great Blessing for their huts and as a sign of the pardon of their sins), they sing and dance in its honor; they throw the powder of the *Buchu* herb (*Spiraea*) <note>Buchu refers to aromatic plants of the Rutaceae family, traditionally used by the Khoisan people for medicinal and cosmetic purposes.</note> around it; they also sprinkle it in their huts, and they offer two sheep to this insect. If it happens that the Beetle lands on someone, that person is regarded as a great Saint; they offer him two sheep and kill an ox in his honor, with the fat and intestines of which they rub and clothe the Saint, hanging the fatty skin of the intestines around his neck while still warm, richly powdered with *Buchu* etc.

### PLATE LXVIII.
N. 1. *The Initiation of Young Men received into the rank of Men*. The entire assembly crouches in a circle, outside of which the Recipient is in the same posture: The eldest harangues the Assembly and asks for its consent regarding the Reception of the Young man: Having obtained it, he approaches the Young man, gives him an exhortation on how he must conduct himself henceforth, and urinates on him, which the other endures very devoutly, carefully rubbing his body with this Sacred water. The Ceremony ends with congratulations and a meal at the expense of the Young man, who, however, does not yet dare to eat this time until after everyone else: In the same plate, there appears also the representation of *another Ceremony* that precedes the one just mentioned: This is the Reception into Civil Society. As among the Hottentots <note>An archaic name for the Khoikhoi people of Southern Africa.</note> no man dares to marry unless he has previously given a part of his body, as soon as he has reached the age of manhood, his left testicle is cut off: For this purpose, his arms and legs are tied, the Operator sits on his belly, opens the area with a sharp knife, removes the piece, fills the wound with a ball of fat and *Buchu* of equal size, and then dresses it. During the operation, some Hottentots kill a ewe for the meal following the Ceremony: After the operation, the patient's entire body is rubbed with melted fat, and after the operator has urinated on him, he is left lying there on the ground until he recovers and is able to crawl to the hut built specifically for him nearby, where he remains for 3 or 4 days. Neither he nor the women are admitted to the meal held on this occasion. N. 2. *Funerals of the Cafres and Hottentots*. The dead body is wrapped—the head folded against the legs—in the skin that the person had used for cover during life, and it is thus carried toward the place of Burial on the shoulders: It is not through the ordinary door of the hut that it is taken out, but through a hole made specifically for this purpose on the side of the fields: The entire Kraal <note>original: "Cralle," a traditional Southern African village enclosure.</note> or Company accompanies the body in great disorder, making lamentable gestures and letting out terrible howls: The cave where the body is deposited is carefully closed.

### PLATE LXIX.
N. 1, 2. *Musical Instrument played by the Cafres*. It is a Bow strung with a gut string: at one end of the string there is a quill tube, and along the string a movable mechanism in the form of a bowl to direct and vary the tone. This Instrument is named *Gougou* <note>Likely the 'gora' or 'lesiba', a mouth-blown stringed instrument.</note>. N. 3, 4. *Musical Instrument played by Women*: It is made in the form of a pot or Kettledrum covered with a sheepskin: It has roughly the tone of a Drum, and the Women play it with their hands and fingers.

### PLATE LXX.
N. 1. *Funeral ceremony of the Inhabitants of Guinea.* If the deceased is buried at their place of residence, it is the women who perform the burial. The pit where the body is placed is 4 to 5 feet wide, and it is positioned in such a way that the earth does not touch the body; this is achieved by surrounding it with wood or planks. The things they loved most during their life are also buried with them. The women who perform the burial process around the tomb—which is covered by a roof in the shape of an open hut—while uttering terrible cries and howls. Some crawl over the tomb, and almost all walk with crutches as a sign of sadness. N. 2. *Burial of a King of Guinea.* His <term>Fetishes<note>original: "Fetiches." In this context, sacred objects or amulets believed to have spiritual power.</note></term> or sacred objects, his weapons, and everything believed necessary for him are placed upon his tomb. While he is being buried, the populace kills everyone they encounter—men and women, boys and girls, free people and slaves—so that they may serve the King in the other world. They are killed by surprise; their bodies are buried with that of the King, and their heads are placed on stakes around the tomb. Sentinels are also stationed there so that no one comes to plunder it.

## SECTION VI.

->On the Idolatrous Nations of the West Indies, and in particular the Peoples of Canada and Virginia.<-

->*This Section contains 10 Plates, with their Explanations.*<-

### PLATE LXXI.
N. 1. *Necklaces and Branches of Wampum, which also have their mystical meanings.* <note>original: "Porcelaines." In early colonial accounts, shell beads or wampum were often called "porcelain" due to their appearance.</note> N. 2. *Cage for the Huskanawers or novice priests, in which they must complete their novitiate.* <note>The "Huskanaw" was a rite of passage for young men among the Powhatan and related Virginian Algonquian peoples.</note> N. 3. *Tomahawk or head-breaker of the Indians.* N. 4. *Calumet of peace.* N. 5. *Peace pipe of the Virginians.*

### PLATE LXXII.
N. 1. *The Great Sacrifice of the Canadians to Gitche Manitou, or the Great Spirit.* <note>original: "Quitchi-Manitou."</note> A fine day is chosen, upon which a large pile of all sorts of valuable merchandise is made—often to the value of 40,000 to 50,000 crowns—which is then set on fire while dancing around the pyre, singing, and carefully presenting the calumets, pipes, etc., to the Sun from morning until evening.

### PLATE LXXIII.
N. 1. *A Native man lighting a torch to go find his mistress.* <note>original: "allumette," referring here to a splinter of wood or small torch used for light.</note> N. 2. *A Native man in conversation with his mistress, sitting at the foot of her bed.* N. 3. *A Native man whose mistress hides in her blanket, not wishing to receive him.* N. 4. *A Native man whose mistress extinguishes the light to receive him.*

### PLATE LXXIV.
N. 1. *Marriage Ceremony of Canada.* The relatives from both sides assemble in the hut of the Elder, where they enjoy themselves by singing and dancing. The relatives of the groom depart, with the exception of four of the eldest. Meanwhile, the bride arrives before the door of the hut accompanied by four of her oldest female relatives. The Elder receives her and has her enter the hut, where he places her beside the groom on an outstretched carpet. They are given a rod, which they hold as long as a certain discourse lasts, after which they break the rod and give pieces of it to all the witnesses present. From then on, the newly-married woman is escorted back by her female companions, who wait for her outside the hut, to her father's house; she remains there, and her husband visits her, until she has a child. Then she moves to her husband's hut and lives with him as long as the marriage lasts. N. 2. *Manner in which the Peoples of Canada perform Divorce.* As it would be an unbearable constraint for the Canadians to be married for their entire lives to the same persons, they separate when it pleases them. When this occurs, the pieces of the aforementioned rod are brought back to the same hut where the betrothal took place, and they are solemnly burned there. From that moment, the divorce is finalized in due form and is beyond all dispute. The children are divided equally between the separated parents; if the number is unequal, one extra is given to the mother, so that she has an advantage over the man, because children are regarded by these peoples as their treasures and their primary wealth.

### PLATE LXXV.
N. 1. *A Shaman who comes to heal a sick person.* <note>original: "Jongleur," a term used by French explorers for indigenous medicine men or spiritual leaders.</note> N. 2. *Slaves mourning a deceased person.* N. 3. *The relatives ask the deceased the cause of their death.*

### PLATE LXXVI.
N. 1. *Rejoicings of the Peoples of Canada while carrying the deceased to the hut of the dead.* N. 2. *Funeral procession of the Peoples of Canada.*

### PLATE LXXVII.
N. 1. *Kiwasa, an idol of the Virginians.* <note>original: "Kivvaſa."</note> It is seated in a reed hut upon a type of altar that the Virginians call a *Pawcorance*. <note>original: "Pavvorance."</note> They consecrate chapels and places of prayer to this divinity. They have images of it in their dwellings, though they differ according to the various qualities of the divinity. They consult it on everything, and it is their tutelary deity, from whom they derive all blessings upon their families. N. 2. *The God of the Winds, another idol of the Virginians.*

### PLATE LXXVIII.
N. 1. *Priest of Virginia, seen from the right side.* N. 2. *Priest of Virginia, seen from the left side.* They have their heads shaved up to the crown, where they keep a row of hair

hair from the forehead to the nape of the neck, in the form of a crest: it is the same above the forehead, where a row of thick hair rises very high, like horsehair, in the form of a topknot, or rather in the shape of the front of a <term>grenadier's cap<note>A tall, pointed ceremonial hat worn by specialized soldiers (grenadiers) in the 17th and 18th centuries.</note></term>. Their mantle is the folded skin of a wild animal, with the fur turned outward: they always keep one arm under the mantle and the other outside to use as needed. No. 3. *Sorcerer or Diviner of Virginia.* This one also has a crest of hair, and his right ear is covered with the skin of a dark-colored bird: these sorcerers, like the priests mentioned above, blacken themselves with soot from the chimney: an otter skin hangs from their belt, and a pouch with some ribbons hangs along the right thigh.

**PLATE LXXIX.** *The manner in which the Virginians worship Fire, and how they rejoice after being delivered from some great danger.*

**PLATE LXXX.** *Tomb of the Kings of Virginia.* The corpse is skinned as carefully as possible: the flesh is removed without damaging the nerves and tendons, so that all the bones and tendons remain encased and in order. The skeleton is thus dried in the sun, while the skin is preserved in oil or grease until it is placed back over the dried skeleton; this is done by sewing it around the skeleton and filling the empty space with sand, so that the corpse appears to be whole. It is then carried to its place of burial, which is a kind of scaffold inside a reed hut, where the body is deposited and covered. The removed flesh is also dried and placed in a basket at the feet of the corpse. The idol *Kiuvasa* <note>An ancestral spirit or protective deity figure often placed in Virginia Algonquian mortuary houses.</note> is placed beside the dead to keep watch over them; a priest remains there day and night near a fire.

# SECTION VII.

## On the Floridians, the Peoples of the Spanish Island, and the Mexicans.

->*This Section contains XIII Plates, with their Explanations.*<-

**PLATE LXXXI.** No. 1. *Sacrifice that the Floridians make to the Sun of their first-born.* The mother of the child remains bowed with her face covered before the block, while the other women dance. One of these women shows the child to the Prince, whereupon the priest rises from his bench, takes the child, and crushes it against the block. No. 2. *Offering of a stag that the Floridians make to the Sun:* They take the skin of the largest stag they can find, fill it with all sorts of herbs, surround it with flowers and fruits, and expose it thus at the top of a tall tree, with its head turned toward the rising Sun. The <term>Paraousti<note>A title for a chief or king among the Timucua people of Florida.</note></term> (Prince) and the principal <term>Jouanas<note>A term for a priest or shaman among the Timucua.</note></term> (Priest) say the prayers and lead the singing. On these occasions, they ask the Sun for an abundance of fruit and the fertility of the fields.

**PLATE LXXXII.** No. 1. *Ceremony observed by the Kings of Florida before undertaking a war expedition.* The King conjures the Sun to be favorable to him, takes water in a wooden bowl, and throws it toward the Sun in such a way that it partly falls back onto the soldiers surrounding him, saying to them:
> *May you thus shed the blood of your enemies!*
After which he fills the bowl with water again and throws it into the fire beside him, saying to his soldiers:
> *May you exterminate your enemies, just as I extinguish this fire!*
This ceremony is accompanied by terrible cries and furious gestures. No. 2. *A King of Florida consulting his Sorcerer before marching against the enemy.*

**PLATE LXXXIII.** No. 1. A. *Floridian women who, having lost their husbands in war, come to implore the assistance of the King.* B. *Hermaphrodites<note>A term used by early European explorers to describe Two-Spirit individuals or those who performed labor roles usually associated with both genders in Timucua society.</note> destined to serve the sick and bury the dead.* No. 2. *Widows of Florida sowing their hair upon the tombs of their husbands.*

**PLATE LXXXIV.** *Manner of burying the Kings and Priests of Florida.* The tomb is surrounded by arrows driven into the ground. The Prince's cup is placed upon the tomb. They weep and fast near the tomb for three consecutive days, and the professional mourners do so especially three times a day: the Allied Princes attend. Everything the deceased possessed during his life is burned: the same is done at the burials of priests: they are buried in their houses, and then the fire is set and all the deceased's possessions are burned.

**PLATE LXXXV.** *Religious ceremony of the inhabitants of the Spanish Island <note>Hispaniola, modern-day Haiti and the Dominican Republic.</note>, by which they formerly honored demons in their temples and through their monstrous images, by offering them certain cakes baked by the hands of their women.*

**PLATE LXXXVI.** No. 1. *Vitzliputzli:* <note>A corruption of Huitzilopochtli, the Aztec god of war and the sun.</note> It is under this name that the Mexicans recognized the Supreme Being. He was represented in human form, seated on an azure throne placed above a portable globe, the poles of which, passing right through the globe, had serpent heads at both ends: it was by these means that the priests carried the idol in procession from time to time. It had on its head a helmet in the shape of a bird with feathers of various colors and a gilded beak. Its face was grave: it had two blue streaks on its forehead and across its nose. Its right hand rested on a serpent, and its left held four arrows and a sh-

<summary>This page describes the intricate mummification and burial practices of Virginian royalty, the sun-worship and war rituals of Florida's Timucua people, and the iconography of the Aztec deity Huitzilopochtli.</summary>
<keywords>Virginia, Timucua, Florida, Huitzilopochtli, Vitzliputzli, Hispaniola, mummification, sun sacrifice, funerary rites, Indigenous Americans</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the description of Central American religious and social structures, specifically focusing on the Aztec (Mexican) deities and their calendar system. The first word on this page, "clier," completes the word "bouclier" (shield) from the previous page's catchword.</meta>

❊ ) ( o ) ( ❊

<meta>catchword: They (Ont)</meta>

shield with 5 white feathers in a cross. Everything there was significant. The Globe, for example, signified the immense Power of the Gods; for the rest, the Idol was entirely surrounded by curtains. No. 2. *Tlaloc*, or *Tezcatlipoca*. They said he was the Brother of the Preceding one <note>Likely referring to Huitzilopochtli, mentioned on the previous page</note>, of the same will and equal power, especially regarding war. Some believe that *Tlaloc* and *Tezcatlipoca* are two different Divinities and give the following Figure to the latter. No. 3. *Tezcatlipoca*: He was, it is said, the God of Repentance. The Mexicans invoked him in times of Calamity because they believed it was He who punished men's sins with plague, famine, etc. No. 4. *Mexican Priests*.

### PLATE LXXXVII. N. 1. *Captive flayed after being defeated*. N. 2. *Captive fighting against a Mexican Priest*. The captive destined for Sacrifice was tied by his feet to a large stone and given weapons to defend himself against the Priest who wished to sacrifice him. He dared not only to parry the blows but also to attack; if he had the good fortune to defeat the Priest, he was given his freedom; if not, the Priest, after killing him, <term>flayed<note>The ritual removal of skin (écorché), often worn afterward by priests in Aztec ceremonies.</note></term> him in the aforementioned manner. N. 3. *The great Temple of Vitzliputzli in the City of Mexico*. It was a square half-pyramid, which one ascended by a Staircase of 126 steps, and which was higher than all the towers of the City. The courtyard or the temple square alone would have been enough to build up to 500 houses.

### PLATE LXXXVIII. N. 1. *Penances of the Mexicans*. They burned incense to the Idol, while some musicians summoned the people by sounding the horn. Each Idolater began his Penance by using a stone instrument to make an incision in his heel, until he had enough blood to rub on his body and to sprinkle part of the temple, after which he went to wash in a river. Other penances performed by the Priests, as Mediators between God and Men, consisted of some whipping themselves in the presence of the God of Repentance *Tezcatlipoca*, doing so with whips made of knotted cords, while others struck one another with stones, etc. N. 2. *Sacrifice of the Captives*. The victims were led near a charnel house, which was a scaffold supported by beams. There, the Captives were obliged to wait at the bottom of the scaffold under the guard of Mexican Soldiers, until they were sought out to be immolated, with no other consolation than having before their eyes thousands upon thousands of fleshless heads. A Priest then came to present them with an Idol made of wheat and honey, and then, withdrawing to the side, the victims were led onto the scaffold. Six Priests would take hold of one of the wretches and stretch him out on a stone carved in the shape of the back of a wooden horse. Two held him by the feet, two by the arms, the fifth held his head, and the sixth—namely the Sacrificer—opened his chest, tore out the heart, presented it while still steaming to the Sun, then turned toward the Idol and threw it at its nose. This done, they threw the body down the stairs from the top of the scaffold.

### PLATE LXXXIX. N. 1. *The Mercury of the Mexicans*, as he was formerly worshipped at *Cholula* under the Name of *Quetzalcoatl*. It was primarily the merchants who paid him homage. Forty days before the feast of the God, they would buy a handsome Slave, who for all that time represented the Divinity, and to whom he would finally be immolated. He was dressed magnificently like a God; many Sacrifices were offered to him; he spent all those days dancing and enjoying himself. Nothing was lacking for him, and people entertained themselves with him, even until the end of the last day. But at midnight, he himself was immolated, and his body was thrown down from the temple. N. 2. *Divinity who presides over the hunt*. This one, during his life in the world, was extraordinarily fond of hunting; thus, they rendered him divine honors through solemn hunts. The Idol sat at the top of a mountain upon an altar. Devout hunters lit fires at some distance from the Idol and gave chase to wild beasts, which, to avoid the fires, turned toward the summit of the mountain, where the hunters killed them with arrows, javelins, and clubs before the Idol, offering it the hearts of the slain animals.

### PLATE XC. N. 1. *Mexican Century, or Hieroglyphic Representation of a Century and a year among the Mexicans*. They explained their thoughts and gave an idea of tangible things through Hieroglyphs, in the manner of the ancient Egyptians. It is in this way that their Century and year were represented. A painted Wheel contained the span of a Century, distinguished by years and marked with memorable events. The Century consisted of 52 Solar Years, each of 365 days. Four <term>Indictions<note>In this context, the four 13-year cycles (trecenas) that make up the 52-year "Calendar Round."</note></term> of 13 years each formed the Division of the Wheel and corresponded to the 4 parts of the World in the following manner: A Serpent surrounded this wheel and marked the 4 divisions with its 4 knots. The Hieroglyph of the first, which marked the south, was a *Rabbit* on a blue background, which was called *Tochtli* <note>Original: "Tochtlila"</note>; of the second, for the East, a *Reed* on a red background, called *Acatl*; of the third, for the North, a *Sword* <note>Original: "Epée," likely referring to a flint knife or macuahuitl.</note> with a stone point on a yellow background, *Tecpatl*; of the fourth, for the West, a *House* on green, called *Calli*. Between these 4 divisions there were twelve small ones, in which the 4 Hieroglyphs were distributed successively, giving each its numerical value up to 13, which was the number of years that composed the indiction. The same was done in the second indiction, in the third, and in the fourth, with the same names, from one to 13, until the circle of 52 years was completed in the manner that follows:

It then transitions into Section VIII, covering the peoples of Panama, South America, and the Caribbean.</meta>

<image-desc>A diagram composed of small circles (dots) arranged in a right-angled triangular pattern. There are 13 rows in total: the top row contains one circle, the second row two, and each subsequent row adds one circle until the thirteenth row, which contains thirteen circles. The arrangement is left-aligned.</image-desc>

This way of counting by 13 was observed not only in the years but also in the months: they began again when they reached 13 <note>This refers to the *trecena*, a 13-day period used in the Aztec ritual calendar, the *Tonalpohualli*.</note>. The Solar Year was 365 days, and the Months were 20 days. Their Names and their Hieroglyphs are marked in the Figure by 1, 2, 3, and so on up to 18. To complete the Year of 365 days, the Mexicans added 5 days <note>Known as the *Nemontemi*, these were considered "unlucky" or "empty" days.</note> to the 18 months of 20 days each which made up the Year.

This Explanation, copied from the Plate itself, is taken from the *Travels of Gemelli Careri*, Vol. VI. Chap. 5 <note>Giovanni Francesco Gemelli Careri (1651–1725) was an Italian adventurer and traveler whose work was a major source for European knowledge of the Americas and Asia.</note>.

**PLATE XCI.** No. 1. *Desolation of the Mexicans at the end of a Century.* Since they believed that the end of the World would come at the end of one of their <term>Centuries<note>The Aztec "century" or "Calendar Round" lasted 52 years.</note></term> of 52 years, they prepared themselves regularly for death on the last day of each Century. They broke all their pottery as being useless for the future: they extinguished all fires and ran through the streets like madmen until the Dawn began to appear. Then they kept their eyes fixed toward the East, and when the Sun began to appear, they greeted it with all their Musical Instruments and with cries of joy: they congratulated one another that, as for the duration of the World, they could at least surely count on another Century. They went to the Temples to bless the Gods there, and to receive from the hands of the Priests the <term>new fire<note>The *Xiuhmolpilli* or "New Fire Ceremony," where all old fires were put out and a fresh fire was lit in the chest of a sacrificed captive.</note></term>, which the latter made on the altars by vigorously rubbing two pieces of wood against each other. Thereupon, everyone endeavored to find new furniture and new pottery, and that day was destined for all kinds of entertainment and public rejoicings; just as they are represented on the same Plate No. 2. *Rejoicings of the Mexicans at the beginning of a Century.*

**PLATE XCII.** No. 1. *Marriage of the Mexicans:* The Priest tied together the ends of the Groom's cloak and the Bride's veil to represent the unity of their will, and in this attire, he accompanied them to their home. No. 2. *Ceremony that the Mexicans practice regarding their Children.* Children were carried to the Temple, and the Priests spoke to them of the misery of this life: If they were the Children of the Great <note>Meaning the nobility or upper classes.</note>, a small sword was placed in their right hand and a small shield in the left, with which the Priests were always provided for this Ceremony. If they were the Children of Artisans, the ceremony was performed with some instruments of their trade or art: to the girls, distaffs and spindles were presented, in the manner of the Africans. After that, the Priest carried the Child before the altar, where he made some incisions with a certain thorn or a stone lancet on the ears and the <term>private parts<note>original: "partie honteuse". This refers to ritual bloodletting or autosacrifice, a common Mesoamerican religious practice.</note></term>: Finally, he sprinkled water over him and washed him under a Magical incantation.

**PLATE XCIII.** No. 1. *Mexican Funeral Procession.* All the Furniture and Weapons of the deceased were brought to the site of the pyre or burial, and if they were Great Lords, much gold and silver etc., to be burned or buried with them. Some of their Servants were killed, as well as their Chaplains and their jesters, to keep them company in the other world: even their legitimate Wives usually climbed onto the pyre and had themselves burned with them. No. 2. *Gifts that the Mexicans bring to their Dead.* No. 3. *The Inhabitants of Venezuela drink the ashes of their <term>Caciques<note>A Caribbean/South American term for a local chief or leader.</note></term> or Kings, after having burned their bodies.* No. 4. *They weep over the bodies of their Caciques.*

---

## SECTION VIII.

### Concerning the Eastern Americans, the Peoples of Panama, & other Southern Idolaters;
### Concerning the Savages of Paria: The Brazilians: The ancient Peruvians etc.

->*This Section contains 11 Plates, with their Explanations.*<-

**PLATE XCIV.** No. 1. *Idols of Campeche & Yucatan.* One formerly saw there a kind of Theater 4 cubits high, made of carved stone and filled with earth: On the Theater stood, on one side, a marble figure of a man, whom two frightful monsters seemed to want to tear apart:

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page continues the descriptions of indigenous American cultures, moving from Mexico (specifically Tabasco) into the marriage and religious customs of Panama, the Orinoco region, Brazil, and finally the Inca Empire of Peru.</meta>

( 0 )

On the other side was the figure of a Serpent, 47 feet long and thick in proportion, which was swallowing a Lion: bodies of sacrificed men (as was the custom of the People of these regions) were laid out in quantity near and over the Serpent. This is all that is known of these figures, which undoubtedly also had their own Meanings. No. 2. Idols of *Tabasco*. They would open the chests of human victims and tear out the heart. They then placed the bleeding body over a hole that a marble Lion had at the top of its neck, so that the blood flowed down into a stone reservoir located below the Lion's head: A Figure of a man, placed near the Reservoir opposite the Lion, seemed to be very attentive in observing the blood flowing into it. As for the heart, the Priest rubbed the face of his Idol with it, and then threw it into the fire he had lit beside the Idol.

### PLATE XCV. No. 1. Marriage of the Indians of *Panama*.
After having promised themselves to one another without much fuss, they proceed immediately to the Betrothal; those who are invited to the wedding bring all sorts of presents, such as axes, stone knives, poultry, fruit, cotton, etc. At the entrance of the cabin, the Groom presents each guest with a vial of <term>Chicati<note>A fermented beverage, likely a variation of chicha made from maize or fruit.</note></term>, which is the common drink of the country. Everyone drinks from it, even the Children. Upon this, the Fathers of the newlyweds enter, one leading his son and the other his daughter by the hand: The Father of the Groom harangues the assembly, armed with his bow and an arrow, the point of which he presents. After this, he begins to dance with extraordinary gestures until he is sweating and can do no more from exhaustion. The Father of the Bride does the same on his side, and after the dance, they both kneel facing each other, and in this posture mutually present their Children to one another, holding them by the hand: As soon as this Ceremony is finished, the <term>Paranymphe<note>The groomsman or ritual attendant; from the Greek 'paranymphos'.</note></term> and his suite run, equipped with axes, to the trees that are to be used to build the cabin for the newlyweds; they cut them down, build the cabin, clear the surrounding land, and plow it: And as the men prepare the earth, the women come to sow it, under the guidance of the Bride’s attendant. It is only after eight days that the Bride is surrendered to her Husband. No. 2. *The Manner in which Friends and Relatives clear the land destined for the Newlyweds.*

### PLATE XCVI. No. 1. Manner in which the *Cabre* Priests breathe Courage.
The Ceremony begins with singing and dancing, where everyone foams at the mouth and behaves as if possessed. A little while later, they calm down and sing and dance in circles more regularly. All those who ask for the *Spirit of Courage*, as they call it, take each other by the hand and continue to dance, while 3 or 4 Priests enter the middle of the circle, one with a vial on a rod, and the others equipped with tubes filled with lit tobacco, the smoke of which they blow onto the dancers, saying to them: *Receive all of you the Spirit of Valor, by which you shall conquer your Enemies.*

### PLATE XCVII. No. 1. Manner in which the Savages of *Paria* treat their Sick.
They plunge the person who has a fever into a river, and then drive them with lashes of rods around a large fire until they can hardly breathe, and then they carry them back into the cabin to put them to bed. No. 2. *Dance of the Savages of Paria around the Dying and their funeral Ceremonies*, where amidst the mournful songs and lamentations that accompany the burial, they provide the deceased with provisions, which they bury with him.

### PLATE XCVIII. No. 1. Funeral Ceremony of the peoples living near the *Orinoco* river.
After the flesh of the corpses has been consumed by worms, they hang the Skeletons from the ceilings of their cabins, decorating them with feathers and necklaces. No. 2. *Funeral Ceremony of the Brazilians.* Here one sees on one side a <term>Boie<note>A shaman or medicine man; also spelled Piaye or Boyé in colonial accounts.</note></term> or Priest, who is at the same time the Physician, visiting a Sick person in his bed—or suspended cloth—with his <term>maraque<note>A maraca; a ritual rattle made from a gourd.</note></term> in his hand, which is a kind of gourd shaped like an Ostrich egg. On the other side, the manner in which the dead are carried to the grave: they are wrapped in a cotton cloth, and if it is a Great person, in that of his Bed, and placed upright in a round, deep hole dug in the ground, similar to a well. Brazilian women mourn the deceased, making mournful gestures, etc.

### PLATE XCIX. No. 1. The Inca (this is what the Kings of Peru were formerly called) consecrates his vessel to the Sun.
This was practiced in the magnificent and rich Temple of the Sun at *Cusco*, whose 4 walls were covered in beaten gold. The Image of the Sun, above the high Altar, stretched from one end of a wall to the other, and its plate was twice as thick as the other plates. On either side of the Sun, one could see the Bodies of their deceased Kings, seated on golden Thrones, bowing their faces toward the ground, and so skillfully embalmed that they would have been taken for living people. No. 2. *The Inca comes to receive the offerings* that his Subjects make to the Sun. Several of the Principal men appeared there masked: some, among others, were winged, as Angels are painted: this is because they adorned themselves with the wings of the bird named <term>Cuntur<note>The Andean Condor.</note></term>.

### PLATE C. No. 1. Manner of lighting the sacred fire among the Peruvians, on the eve of the great festival of the Sun, named the great *Rami*.
It was necessary, they said, to receive the fire for the sacrifices made to the Sun from the Sun itself: They therefore took a large bracelet, from which hung a hollow vessel the size of an Orange, extremely polished and shining, which was held against the Sun in such a way that the rays, gathered into a single point, struck a tuft of cotton that served as tinder, where the fire ignited in a very natural way. It was with this fire that all the sacrifices were burned that day, and all the meats they intended to eat were roasted. No. 2. *On the first day of the great festival of the Sun, the Inca presents a vessel full of Liquor to it, and invites it to drink.*

->* ) ( 0 ) ( *<-

**PLATE CI.** No. 1. *Sacrifice of a black Lamb on the day of the great festival of the Sun.* No. 2. *Feast in honor of the Sun on the day of the <term>Grand Rami<note>Likely a corruption of "Inti Raymi," the most significant religious ceremony of the Inca Empire, celebrating the winter solstice and the Sun god, Inti.</note></term>.*

**PLATE CII.** No. 1. *Desolation of the Peruvians during an Eclipse of the Moon.* Since they believed that the Moon was either angry with them or sick—to such an extent that they feared she might die and fall upon them—they made a terrible noise to prevent this misfortune by shouting at the top of their lungs and sounding horns, trumpets, and drums. They also tied dogs to trees and whipped them to make them howl, so that the Moon (whom they believed had fallen into a faint and who singularly loved dogs) would wake up and return to herself. No. 2. *Captive sacrificed by the <term>Antis<note>The collective name used by the Incas for the various ethnic groups living in the Antisuyu, the eastern jungle regions of the Andes; they were often portrayed by the Incas as savage or cannibalistic.</note></term>.* This only happened to prisoners of high rank, who were tied to large stakes or trees. Pieces of flesh were sliced from their bodies one after another using stone razors and knives; men, women, and children ate these pieces before the eyes of the unfortunate victims while they were still alive, drenching themselves in their blood. Mothers would even rub the blood on their nipples so that their infants would suckle it with their milk. Those who suffered this cruel martyrdom with firmness were placed among the ranks of the Gods after their death, and their skeletons were hung in huts on the mountain peaks. Those, on the contrary, who suffered with impatience were thrown into the <term>common refuse pit<note>original: "voirie"</note></term>.

**PLATE CIII.** No. 1. *The manner in which the Inca marries those of his own blood.* There were certain times when the King would assemble all those of his lineage who had reached the age of manhood. He would then place himself in their midst, call out the names of the men and women he wished to marry to one another, and have them promise mutual fidelity, etc. No. 2. *The manner in which the hair of the Inca's sons was cut while giving them their names.* When the children reached the age of two, all the relatives would assemble before the King. The Godfather chosen for this ceremony would begin by cutting some of the child's hair with a sharpened stone razor; he was then followed in this task by all the child's relatives, each according to their rank, until all the hair was removed. Immediately after this ceremony, they gave the child a name according to their custom and placed before him the gifts they wished to offer. All of this was followed by a feast and a ball, and the festivities sometimes lasted for several days in a row.

**PLATE CIV.** No. 1. *Funeral honors rendered to the Great of Peru after their death.* No. 2. *The manner of burying them.* When an *Inca* or some other Great of the Empire died, so many of his servants, friends, and even women presented themselves wishing to accompany him to the next world to serve him there, that they could not all be buried with him; some had to be sent away. The body was carried on a litter, seated on a kind of throne, toward the place of burial. The wives and servants followed the body, laden with provisions and food for the next life. During the procession, one of the deceased's closest relatives would blow some nourishment into his mouth through a tube so that he could better endure the journey. A wooden image of the deceased was placed upon his tomb. A deceased artisan was buried laden with the tools of his trade and the masterpieces of his art, and a soldier with his weapons. The two figures on this final plate represent these ceremonies, as well as the manner of lowering the dead into their tombs.

# EXPLANATION OF THE FRONTISPIECE.

## REPRESENTATION OF THE WORLD'S PRINCIPAL RELIGIONS.

This is an allegorical representation of the perspectives held by each of them. The principal objects appearing in it are situated in the center of the plate; (a) on one side is the High Priest of the Roman Church <note>The Pope</note>, vested in his power and accompanied by Cardinals, Bishops, Jesuits, and other Clergy, to whom the Greek Patriarch—though smiling and covering his forehead with a mask—swears loyalty. On the other side are the Protestants, whose religion is represented by an open Bible, the Reformation <term>Hervorming: the Protestant Reformation</term> by a pruning knife, and its effect by a well-pruned tree. The individuals who can be distinguished therein are the principal reformers, such as: **LUTHER, ZWINGLIUS, CALVIN, MELANCHTHON, JAN HUSS, HENRY THE EIGHTH** *King of England*, **CRANMER** <note>OCR reads "KRAMER," referring to Thomas Cranmer</note>, **MARTYR** <note>Peter Martyr Vermigli</note>, **BUCER** <note>Martin Bucer</note>, etc. Not far from there appears **MENNO SIMONS** <note>founder of the Mennonites</note>, who receives an adult daughter into his community <note>a symbolic representation of adult baptism, a core Mennonite belief</note>, and at his side sits a Quaker in ecstasy, representing religious enthusiasm <term>geestdrijverij: a historical term often used to describe intense or "fanatical" spiritual fervor</term>.— (b) At the bottom of the plate appears **ALI** <note>Ali ibn Abi Talib</note> who explains the Quran <note>original: "Alkoran"</note> to Turks, Tartars, and Negroes, as well as a Persian holding the tenets (*) of his religion in his hand; the annual sacrifice of that nation, which consists of a camel, is represented by that animal. (c) Finally, there appear in the distance and in the empty spaces the Mexicans, Peruvians, South Indians, and other pagan and idolatrous nations.

# BERNARD PICART
### *THE ROMAN,* <note>original: "Den Romein." Picart was often called "Le Romain" (The Roman) because of his devotion to the classical style and his study of the great masters of Rome.</note>
## DRAFTSMAN AND ENGRAVER, <term>Engraver: A skilled artist who cuts designs into metal plates for printing; in Picart's era, this was the primary way to reproduce art and illustrations.</term>

->*Born in Paris on June 11, 1673, Died in Amsterdam on May 8, 1733.*<-

**HISTORY**, holding a closed book carelessly in her hand and leaning mournfully with her elbow on a table near her writing instruments, appears abandoned and inconsolable because the most beautiful of her monuments shall no longer be offered to the temple of memory by this illustrious draftsman. **THE GENIUS OF DRAWING** <note>In this context, a "Genius" is an allegorical figure—a tutelary spirit representing the essence and inspiration of an art form.</note>, overcome with grief, bitterly weeps over his loss, and the spirit of **ENGRAVING** tries to comfort him by showing him the crown of immortality, which the beautiful works of this great master have so deservedly received.

Bernard Picart, The Roman, Draftsman, Engraver, History, The Genius of Drawing, Art of Engraving

# Sacred Ceremonies and Church Customs
## of CHRISTIANS In the Whole World.

->by<-

# DAVID HERRLIBERGUER

<margin>The testimony of the times.</margin>

->shown in beautiful art plates<-
->after the drawings of the famous **PICART**,<note>Bernard Picart (1673–1733) was a French engraver whose work "Cérémonies et coutumes religieuses de tous les peuples du monde" revolutionized the study of world religions through detailed illustrations.</note><-

### FIRST EDITION.

## Comprising the ceremonies of the <term>Lutherans</term> of the <term>Augsburg Confession</term><note>The primary confession of faith of the Lutheran Church, presented in 1530.</note>, and of the <term>Reformed</term><note>The Calvinist tradition, which was the public church of the Dutch Republic at the time.</note>, of the Dutch and other churches.

> One must first carefully examine the truth of matters, so that one does not adopt groundless impressions, and expose oneself to bringing forth improper judgments in an ill manner.
> <note>original: "Men moet vooraf de waarheid... Phaedr." A quote attributed to the Roman fabulist Phaedrus, urging objectivity and research.</note>

No. 1.

## Congratulations as performed for the betrothed in Amsterdam.

**A.** The Bride and the Groom positioned under a Mirror.
**B.** The Mother and the blood relatives of the Bride.
**C.** The Mother and the blood relatives of the Groom.
**D.** The Attendants <note>original: "Speelnooten," referring to the bridesmaids or groomsmen</note>, who escort the ladies and present them to the betrothed.
**E.** The ladies, who, after having offered their congratulations, take their places on both sides of the room.
**F.** A separate room, where the gentlemen, after having paid their respects <note>original: "pligtplegingen," formal courtesies or social obligations</note> to the betrothed <term>betrothed</term> <note>original: "ondertrouwden," specifically those in the legal period between the notice of marriage and the ceremony</note>, perform the same for the blood relatives.
**G.** Confections and delicacies which are offered to the ladies from time to time.
**H.** A designated person, to serve confections, wine, and <term>Hippocras</term> <note>a historical drink made of wine mixed with sugar and spices, often served at formal occasions</note> during the wedding ceremonies.

---

No. 2.

## Ceremony called by the Dutch: *Palm-knotting*.

This consists of the braiding and arranging of flowers and the leaves of green herbs, with which the newlyweds are strewn on their wedding day. The young people of both sexes invited to this ceremony do this two days before the solemnization of the marriage; <gloss>one</gloss> also mixes gold and silver leaves among them, which are also used to decorate the leaves of the greenery.

Plate 12.

->No. 1.<-

## Meeting of those who in Amsterdam are called **Collegiants**.

These do not form a separate church or congregation, but only private societies or gatherings of religious, learned people from all Christian denominations. In these meetings, each person freely explains his thoughts or opinions regarding one or another doctrine of <term>Religion<note>The original Dutch uses "Godsdienst," which literally means "service to God."</note></term>, or a passage in the Holy Scripture. They are united solely by their love for liberty of conscience in religion.

---

->No. 2.<-

## Their **Communion celebration** at **Rijnsburg**, a village near **Leiden**.

This is where they primarily hold their assembly and where, notwithstanding the differences in their ideas, they unite and partake of the <term>Lord’s Supper<note>The "Avondmaals-viering" refers to the ritual of Communion or the Eucharist, which the Collegiants practiced in a simplified, egalitarian manner.</note></term> together as brothers. They do so while lamenting the misery of Christendom, which, being divided by so many conflicting opinions, finds itself exposed to the mockery of <term>freethinkers<note>In the 18th century, "vrijgeesten" or freethinkers were seen as those who rejected traditional religious authority in favor of reason, often viewed with suspicion by the orthodox.</note></term> and unbelievers.

<term>Collegiants</term>, <term>Amsterdam</term>, <term>Rijnsburg</term>, <term>Leiden</term>, <term>Communion celebration</term>, <term>Religion</term>, <term>Holy Scripture</term>, <term>liberty of conscience</term>, <term>freethinkers</term>

<summary>This page describes the Collegiants, a 17th and 18th-century Dutch religious group that valued open theological discussion and liberty of conscience over formal church structures. It highlights their practice of inclusive Communion at Rijnsburg as a response to the divisions within Christianity.</summary>
<keywords>Collegiants, Rijnsburg, religious tolerance, liberty of conscience, Dutch Reformation, Communion, ecumenism, freethinkers</keywords>

<meta>This page describes Plate 13, detailing the specific baptismal rites of the Rhijnsburgers, a group often associated with the Collegiant movement in the Netherlands.</meta>

<header>Plate 13.</header>

# The Baptism of the <term>Rhijnsburgers</term> <note>The Rhijnsburgers, or Collegiants, were a 17th-century Dutch religious group that emerged from the Remonstrant tradition. They met in "colleges" and were known for their practice of adult baptism by total immersion in the town of Rhijnsburg.</note>.

This is a ceremony in which the person who desires to be baptized—after having made their <term>confession of faith</term> <note>original: "belijdenis des geloofs"</note> before the entire assembly—descends into a type of fountain or pond located behind the Rhijnsburgers' meeting house, accompanied by the person who is to perform the baptism, in order to be <term>immersed</term> <note>original: "gedompeld"</note> in the water. 

If the person is a man, he is dressed in a white shirt and trousers; if it is a woman, she wears a shift and an outer garment, to the bottom of which several pieces of lead are attached <note>These weights were a practical measure to ensure that the woman’s skirts did not float to the surface during immersion, thereby preserving her modesty.</note>. Afterward, they are solemnly exhorted within the assembly to remain steadfast in the observance of the <term>Divine commandments</term> <note>original: "Goddelijke geboden"</note>.

<summary>A description of the adult immersion baptism practiced by the Rhijnsburgers (Collegiants), including details on the location, the confession of faith, and the specific attire worn for modesty.</summary>
<keywords>Rhijnsburgers, Collegiants, adult baptism, immersion, confession of faith, religious rituals, modesty weights, Rhijnsburg</keywords>

Plate 13.

# The Baptism of the Rijnsburgers

A ceremony in which the person who desires to be baptized—after having made their <term>confession of faith</term> <note>original: "belijdenis des geloofs"; a public declaration of one's religious convictions and commitment to the community's values.</note> before the entire congregation—descends with the person who is to perform the baptism into a type of fountain or pond located behind the meeting place of the <term>Rijnsburgers</term> <note>Also known as Collegiants, this was a Dutch religious group founded in the 17th century that rejected formal church hierarchy and practiced adult baptism by full immersion in the town of Rijnsburg.</note>, in order to be immersed in the water. If it is a man, he is dressed in a white shirt and trousers; if it is a woman, she wears a shift and an outer garment, to the bottom of which several pieces of lead are attached <note>These lead weights were a practical addition to ensure the woman’s clothing remained submerged for the sake of modesty during the full immersion.</note>. Following this, they are earnestly exhorted within the assembly to remain steadfast in the observance of the Divine commandments.

## The Baptism of the <term>Rijnsburgers</term><note>Also known as Collegiants, this was a 17th-century Dutch religious group that emphasized individual interpretation of the Bible and practiced adult baptism by full immersion.</note>

# Plate 2.

**A. 1. 2. Communion bread** of the Greeks. These are unleavened <note>While the text says 'unleavened' (ongerezen), most Eastern Orthodox traditions actually use leavened bread called Prosphora; the author may be mistaken or describing a specific variant.</note> breads, which the Greeks and Muscovites <note>A historical term for Russians</note> use in their Mass; they are marked with a single or a double cross. The letters found on the four corners mean: *Jesus Christ Conquers* <note>original Greek: Ιησῦς χρισός Νικᾶ</note>, that is: **JESUS C<gloss>H</gloss>RISTUS** is the victor.

**B. The Star** [as they call it] which they place over the dish with the consecrated bread so that the linen cloth, with which it is covered, does not touch the bread.

**C. The Bishop**, holding in both his hands—marked g. and h.—the candlesticks of two and three branches <note>These are the 'dikirion' and 'trikirion' used by a bishop to bless the faithful.</note>.

**D. The Fan** <note>A liturgical fan or 'ripidion,' often depicting a multi-winged seraph, used to keep insects away from the Eucharist and to symbolize the presence of angels.</note>.

**E. 1. 2. The Qurban** <term>Qurban: A word of Semitic origin meaning an offering or sacrifice to God</term>, or communion bread of the Copts <note>Egyptian Christians</note>, upon which various crosses can be seen in raised relief, all arranged in order with the inscription: *The Holy* and *the Strong* <note>original Greek: Ἄγιος Ἴχυρος (Agios Ischyros)</note>.

The priest (labeled C) appears here, dressed in all his vestments, such as:
**a. The long robe**.
**b. The Stole** or cross-band.
**c. The sleeves** of the robe.
**d. The round cloak**, which hangs from the front down to the belt and from the back down to the calf of the leg.
**e. A type of collar or neckband**.
**f. A square piece of cloth**, which hangs from the belt <note>This is likely the 'epigonation,' a symbolic thigh-shield representing the 'sword of the Spirit.'</note>.

<summary>This page describes various liturgical items and vestments used in the Greek, Russian, and Coptic Christian traditions, specifically focusing on the types of communion bread and the ritual tools of a bishop.</summary>

Plate 3.

<margin>No. 1.</margin>

### **The manner in which the Greeks await the descent of the Holy Fire into the Holy Sepulchre at Easter.**

This event, which the poor, ignorant masses <note>The Dutch phrase "onnozelen hoop" refers to a simple-minded or gullible crowd.</note> take for a miracle, occurs in a purely natural way by means of a fire-starting device <term>vuurgevend werktuig: literally a "fire-giving tool," likely referring to a flint and steel or an early chemical incendiary hidden by the clergy to create the illusion of spontaneous combustion</term> which the priest keeps concealed in his pockets.

### **The distribution of the Holy Fire by the high priest to the Greeks.**

Plate 4.

->No. 1.<-

## Mourning of the Greek women at Ramah.

Or hired professional mourners <note>original: "omgekogte klaagvrouwen"</note>, who, after having sat around the grave for half an hour, rise to form a circle and struggle together <note>The Dutch "worstelen" suggests a rhythmic, physical movement or a swaying ritual common in traditional mourning</note> while raising a terrible cry.

---

->No. 2.<-

## Solemn baptism of the Greek and Syrian Christians in the Jordan River.

Whereby everyone, without distinction of gender <term>gender: translated from the archaic Dutch "kunne"</term>, entirely naked and mixed together, throws themselves with the greatest piety into the Jordan to baptize themselves and one another; some take moistened cloths with them, while others scoop water into bottles, and still others even take some of the earth and the herbs that grow on the banks of the river back to their own country. <note>It was common for pilgrims to bring home "souvenirs" of soil or water from the Holy Land as contact relics.</note>

--- Page 228 ---
<meta>This page appears to be a descriptive guide for an illustrated plate (Plate 4), likely from a 19th-century travelogue or geographical study of the Levant and Holy Land.</meta>

Plate 4.

Nº. 1.

## Mourning of the Greek Women at Rama.

Or hired professional mourners <note>In many Eastern Mediterranean cultures, professional mourners—often women—were paid to lead the community in ritualized weeping and lamentation.</note>, who, after sitting around the grave for half an hour, stand up to form a circle and struggle together <note>The original Dutch "worstelen" (to wrestle) likely refers to a rhythmic, physical ritual of collective grief or a swaying dance common in traditional lamentations.</note> while raising a terrible cry.

---

Nº. 2.

## Solemn Baptism of the Greek and Syrian Christians in the Jordan River.

During which everyone, without distinction of gender, completely naked and mixed together, throws themselves into the Jordan with the greatest religious devotion to baptize themselves and each other; some take wet cloths with them, and others scoop water into bottles, and still others even take the earth and the herbs that grow on the banks of the river back to their own countries <note>These items served as "brandia" or "eulogia"—holy souvenirs believed to carry the blessing of the sacred site.</note>.

<note>Vocabulary: Mourning, Greek, Rama <note>Likely referring to Ramla, a common stop for pilgrims in Palestine.</note>, professional mourners, baptism, Syrian, Christians, Jordan.</note>

<summary>This page describes two cultural and religious scenes in the Holy Land: the intense ritualized mourning of Greek women and the communal, ecstatic baptismal practices of Eastern Christians at the Jordan River.</summary>
<keywords>pilgrimage, baptism, Jordan River, mourning rituals, Greek Orthodox, Syrian Christians, professional mourners, Ramla</keywords>

Plate 7.

## Memorial Service for the Deceased among the Armenians.

This practice is especially in use among the *Armenians of Julpha* <note>New Julfa, the Armenian quarter of Isfahan, Iran, known for its distinct cultural and religious traditions</note>; they celebrate it on August 26, [being the feast of the holy cross <note>original: "heilige kruis"</note>,] with burning wax candles and ignited fires into which they throw incense, all accompanied by shouting and wailing. This mournful ceremony is performed solely by women dressed in white, with the exception of some priests dressed in black, who are compensated for their service.

## Memorial celebration for the deceased among the Armenians,

Which is especially in use among the Armenians of *Julpha* <note>New Julfa, the Armenian quarter of Isfahan, established in 1606 by Shah Abbas I</note>; they celebrate this on the 26th of August, [being the feast of the <term>Holy Cross|an important religious observance in the Armenian Apostolic Church, traditionally involving the blessing of the four corners of the world</term>,] with burning wax candles and lit fires into which they throw incense, all accompanied by shouting and wailing. This mournful ceremony is performed solely by women dressed in white, except for some priests dressed in black, who are compensated for their service.

## The baptism of the Russians,

Which occurs with much ceremony, such as the immersion of the child in a deep vessel filled with water, giving a grain of salt into the mouth, anointing it after <gloss>the</gloss> baptism, and putting a clean shirt on it, etc. <note>This "clean shirt" refers to the *kryzhma*, a white garment symbolizing the soul's purity after the rite.</note>
</insert>

---

<insert>
No 2.

## The burial of the Russians,

Which is of little consequence among the poor and the lower classes, but magnificent among persons of rank, owing to the numerous following and the large number of hired professional mourners. <note>In many Eastern European cultures, professional mourners were women hired to wail and sing traditional laments.</note> One also gives to the deceased a safe-conduct letter, or rather a letter of absolution <note>original: "schuldvergiftenis"</note> in their coffin, and even today one sometimes hires a priest to pray for the deceased in a hut near the grave during the mourning period, which lasts forty <gloss>days</gloss>.
</insert>

# Plates 4 and 5, belonging together.

<insert>
The Pope solemnly takes possession of the High Priesthood in the Church of St. John Lateran, which is the principal church of all those existing within the Christian jurisdiction of the Pope.
</insert>

The manner in which the Pope solemnly takes possession of the High Priesthood <term>Opperpriesterschap: The office of the Supreme Pontiff</term> and the supreme authority over the Christian Church, in the Church of St. John Lateran, just as Clement XI <note>Pope Clement XI, who reigned from 1700 to 1721</note> did among others. In the first part, there appear the Musicians, the vanguard, all sorts of Pages, pack animals, etc. In the second part, the Roman Noblemen, the Papal officials, the Commissioners, the Governor of Rome, the Nephews and blood relatives of the Pope <note>Refers to the "Papal Nephews," a historical term for relatives of the Pope who held high office</note>, the Ambassadors of crowned heads, the Apostolic sub-deacon carrying the holy cross, etc. Then, the Pope seated in a litter <note>original: "draagkoets," referring here to a ceremonial sedan chair or carriage</note> or on a pack horse, amidst fifty Roman Noblemen, and led by a detachment of Cavalry under the command of a captain of the Swiss. Near His Holiness's litter was the Lord Chamberlain, then the Chief Cupbearer, the Private Secretaries, the Personal Physician, followed by the Swiss Guard. After these followed the Cardinals two by two, the Pope's bodyguard, the High Priests (*), the Archbishops, the Bishops, the Apostolic Chief Protonotary <note>original: "Oppergeregtsschrijver," a high-ranking legal official of the Papal court</note>, the members of the Court, and the Treasurers. Finally, the led horses, the trumpeters, and two companies of light Cavalry armed with lances.

This entire procession passed before the City Hall under a triumphal arch erected by the Roman people, and under another on the cattle market <note>The Campo Vaccino, which was the name for the Roman Forum at the time</note>, erected by the Duke of Parma.

The Clergy in <gloss>the</gloss> church of St. John Lateran come in a procession (+) to meet the parade, and there the Pope, seated upon a throne, receives the keys of the church as has been said before.

---

(*) The Patriarchs. (+) In Procession <note>original: "In Processie"</note>.

<summary>This text describes the "Possesso" procession of Pope Clement XI, detailing the elaborate hierarchy of officials, nobles, and guards that escorted the Pope from the Vatican to St. John Lateran. It highlights the use of triumphal arches and the symbolic handover of the church keys.</summary>
<keywords>Pope Clement XI, St. John Lateran, Possesso, Roman nobility, Swiss Guard, triumphal arch, Duke of Parma, Vatican hierarchy, Roman Forum</keywords>

Plate 12.

->No. 1.<-

### Consecration of the Great Cross.

Whereby the Bishop, in the presence of the clergy, delivers a sermon and offers his prayer, after an altar server has lit three wax candles at the foot of the cross.

---

->No. 3.<-

### The Baptism of the Bell.

Which is performed through sprinkling, washing, rubbing, and anointing <note>Historical term for the solemn blessing of a church bell, so called because the rituals of washing and naming mirror those of a human baptism.</note>, while the Bishop and the clergy sing and offer prayers.

---

->No. 5.<-

### Censing the Bell.

By means of the censer <term>censer: a metal vessel (also called a thurible) in which incense is burned during religious ceremonies</term>, which is placed beneath the hanging bell.

<column-break/>

->No. 2.<-

### The Censing of the Cross.

Which is performed during the consecration, after three lit wax candles have been placed on the cross—specifically, one at the top and two on the arms of the same.

---

->No. 4.<-

### The Consecration of the Bell.

Which is performed in the name of the Holy Trinity, and involves giving it the name of a particular saint.

---

->No. 6.<-

### Consecration of a Statue.

Which is performed with sprinkling, the naming of the saint whom the statue represents, and—if it is an image of the Blessed Virgin—through a triple censing, hymns, prayers, signs of the cross, etc.

<vocab>Consecration, Bishop, sermon, prayer, wax candles, censing, cross, bell, sprinkling, anointing, censer, Trinity</vocab>

<keywords>liturgy, consecration, church bells, religious rituals, censing, iconography, Catholic tradition</keywords>

<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the cataloging of religious artifacts and Catholic sacramentals, specifically those related to devotional practices and protective charms.</meta>

# The Agnus Dei

**A. Accurate copy of the printed sheet of the**
**Agnus Dei**, which is a type of medal made in one side of [wax mixed with balsam], featuring a Lamb, with the inscription: **Agnus Dei qui tollis peccata mundi, miserere nobis.** <note>The OCR text 'Agnus I miserer: nobis. VII' is a corrupted transcription of the Latin: 'Lamb of God, who takes away the sins of the world, have mercy on us.'</note> that is: *Lamb of God! who [takes away the sins of the world], have mercy on us*. as seen at letter **B**. The [other] side shows the image of one or another saint.
**C. The coat of arms of Pope Alexander VII**, who issued the first aforementioned sheet; the translation of the [blessing follows].
**D. The Scapulars**, being two pieces of certain woven [cloth] joined together, which most monastics wear to earn indulgences. <note>A scapular consists of two small squares of wool joined by strings, worn over the shoulders as a sign of devotion.</note> (\*) one piece [is worn on the chest, the other on the back].
**E. Rosary or Paternoster**, composed of five [decades]. <note>The term 'Paternoster' (Our Father) refers here to the string of prayer beads.</note>
**F. Ordinary Rosary decorated with Relics, and [crosses].**
**G. Ring**, which the Knights of Malta use to pray; the rose upon it turns by means of a spring, with a black point pointing to the fifteen numbers upon it, which represent the numbers or parts of the rosary mentioned above. <note>A mechanical 'rosary ring' used for counting prayers discreetly.</note>
**H. A portion or decade of the rosary.**
**I. Bread of St. Genevieve**, being a type of dry [biscuit or cake] which the followers of St. Genevieve distribute for the healing of [fevers]. <note>St. Genevieve is the patron saint of Paris; blessed bread was often distributed in her name for miraculous healing.</note>
**K. Consecrated copper medal** which the sisters of the rosary wear to preserve their chastity.

---

(\*) See *Halma, Dictionary, page 945, under the word: Scapulaire.*

<column-break/>

# Agnus Dei.

[...composed of] balsam and pure [wax], named: the ce- [cera] <note>original: 'ce-'. Likely 'cera,' the Latin word for wax.</note> of the consecration [which is] awakening on the [soul].

Mother of misery,

Whoever toils and struggles,
I offer myself as your surety
[for the goal] at which you aim.

[Regarding the] efficacy of the Agnus Dei.

[Used] to praise God.
[Against] the power of winds and thunder.
[By the] cross, which is depicted upon it, [one is saved from] snares, deceptions, vanities, and [perils].
[Neither] foul air, nor the falling sickness <note>A historical term for epilepsy.</note>, nor [unfavorable] weather shall harm or disadvantage him.
[May its] power be such, that they face no danger [at sea]; that no cruelty of [enemies] or men befall them; [it protects] from lightning, from storm, and [perilous] childbirth, and they shall be freed from [evil].
Apostolic Chamber, 1662.
[To the] authorities.

PLATE XVIII.

# THE VIRTUES OF THE AGNUS DEI,
<note>Latin: "Lamb of God"</note>

Composed of Holy Chrism <term>Holy Chrism: a consecrated oil used in baptism and other sacraments</term>, Balsam, and pure wax. Extracted from the book called the *Ceremonial*, where, speaking of their blessing, the following words are found recorded near the end in this manner:

->It is read that Pope Urban V sent three *Agnus Dei* to the Emperor of the Greeks, with these verses and words.<-

<columns>
It drives away thunder;
It erases sins;
It saves from fire,
And from drowning;
It guards against sudden death,
It puts Devils to flight,
And subdues enemies.
<column-break/>
Both the child and the Mother
Are placed out of danger;
For those who labor to make it;
It gives great power,
To those worthy to have it;
A part, however small,
Profits as much as the whole.
</columns>

### The Prayer that the Pope offers at the Blessing of the *Agnus Dei*.

>May it erase sins, may pardon be obtained, and may it confer graces. May Christians, upon touching and seeing it, be moved to praise God. May the noise of the air, hail, snow, and storms, the fury of the winds and thunder be moderated and halted. May the evil spirits be terrified and flee before the saving and glorious standard of the Cross, which is depicted upon it. May he who carries them have power against the illusions, deceptions, cunning, and frauds of the Devil and of evil spirits. May no storm, adversity, pestilential or corrupt air, nor falling sickness <term>falling sickness: an old term for epilepsy</term>, nor any torment, sea storms, any fire, or other injuries of the weather be able to offend, harm, or prejudice him who shall devoutly carry it upon himself. May the Mother and the child be preserved during childbirth. May all those who carry it be always in safety; may they fear no peril, may they have no fear of shadows, may no cruelty of the Devil harm them, may they not be deceived by men, and may they be exempt from all perils of fire, lightning, torment, and storms. And may women give birth without labor, and be delivered from evil and all danger.

->IN ROME, At the Printer of the Apostolic Chamber. 1662.<-
->with permission of the Superiors.<-

---

**A.** An exact copy of the printed sheet that the Pope gives with the *Agnus Dei*, which is a type of wax medal representing a Lamb, as seen at letter **B**. This medal has the image of some Saint on the reverse side.
**C.** The Coat of Arms of Pope Alexander VII, who provided the original of the aforementioned sheet **A**.
**D.** The Scapular <term>Scapular: two small pieces of cloth joined by string, worn over the shoulders as a sign of devotion</term>.
**E.** The Rosary, a prayer chain composed of 15 decades.
**F.** An ordinary rosary, enriched with a Reliquary, etc.
**G.** A ring which the Knights of Malta use to say their Rosary; the wheel on top turns and clicks, marking with a small black dot on the 10 numbers around it the number of decades already recited.
**H.** A decade <note>a single-decade rosary</note> for saying one's prayers.
**I.** Bread of Saint Genevieve (or a type of small dry biscuit), which the Monks of Saint Genevieve distribute for the relief of fever.
**K.** A Blessed copper medal that the Sisters of Charity in Paris wear on their rosary to preserve their virginity <note>or "purity of life"</note>.
