<meta>This page continues the catalog of Catholic devotional objects and sacramentals, specifically focusing on the "Agnus Dei" wax medals. While the previous page likely introduced the physical appearance of these medals, this page details their symbolic meanings, the history of their distribution by various Popes, and the specific spiritual and physical protections they were believed to offer the faithful.</meta>

Plate 18.

# The Agnus Dei.
<note>Latin: "Lamb of God"</note>

A. An accurate copy of the printed sheet which the Pope gives with the *Agnus Dei*, which is a type of medal made of wax, representing on one side a Lamb, with the inscription: *Agnus Dei qui tollis pecata mundi. miserere nobis. VII*—that is: *Lamb of God! who takes away the sin of the world, have mercy on us.* <note>The Roman numeral VII likely refers to the seventh year of the Pope's reign.</note> as seen at letter B. This medal has, on the reverse side, the image of one or another saint.

C. The coat of arms of Pope Alexander VII, who was the first to issue the aforementioned sheet A; the translation of the sheet can be found here alongside it.

D. The *Scapulars* <term>Scapular: two small pieces of sacred cloth joined by ribbons, worn over the shoulders as a sign of devotion</term>, being two pieces of certain consecrated fabric joined together with ribbons, which are worn under the shirt by most members of religious orders to earn indulgences. (*) one piece has a Latin inscription.

E. A Rosary or Paternoster <note>Latin: "Our Father"</note>, composed of fifteen decades of small beads.

F. An ordinary Rosary decorated with Relics, etc.

G. A ring, which the Knights of Malta use to pray their rosary; the rose upon it turns by means of a spring, and points with a small black dot to the fifteen numbers surrounding it, representing the number of decades or the parts of the rosary mentioned above.

H. A portion or decade of the rosary.

I. Bread of St. Genevieve, being a type of dry biscuit which the monks of St. Genevieve distribute for the healing of fevers. <note>Saint Genevieve is the patron saint of Paris, traditionally invoked against disasters and illness.</note>

K. A consecrated copper medal which the Sisters of Charity wear on their rosary for the preservation of their chastity.

<margin>(*) See Halma, Dictionary, page 945, under the word: *Scapulaire*.</margin>

<column-break/>

Continuation of Plate 18.

# The Power of the Agnus Dei.

Composed of holy Ointment, Balsam, and pure wax. An extract from the book named the *Ceremonial*, where, speaking of the consecration of all this, the following words are recorded at the end in this manner.

<image-desc>A circular diagram representing a medal. Inside a double-lined border, the Dutch translation of the Agnus Dei prayer is written in a ring: "Lamb of God who takes away the sin of the world, have mercy on us. VII".</image-desc>

<insert>
->The Coat of Arms<-
->of<-
->Pope<-
->Alexander VII.<-
</insert>

One reads that Pope Urban V sent three *Agnus Dei* to the Greek Emperor with the following verses and words.

| | |
|---|---|
| It can subdue the power of thunder. | It brings no distress to a mother, |
| It washes away sin purely. | Burdened with cares for her child. |
| Fierce fire shall never penetrate, | When you labor, toil, and struggle, |
| Nor shall water be your grave. | May prosperity be your guarantee |
| It guards you from a sudden death. | Of the enjoyment you aim for. |
| It puts the devils to flight, | Though the object is small in parts, |
| And causes the enemy's plot to fail. | It can heal you of much sorrow. (*) |

## The prayer which the Pope offers at the consecration of the Agnus Dei.

> That it may blot out sin, that forgiveness may be obtained, and that it may grant grace.
> That Christians, when they see and touch it, may be moved to praise God.
> That the noise in the air, hail, snow, storms, the violence of the winds and thunder may be moderated and held back.
> That before the salutary and glorious standard of the cross, which is depicted upon it, the evil spirits may be terrified and flee.
> That he who wears it may have strength against the delusions, deceptions, follies, and craftiness of the devil and of evil spirits.
> That no storm, adversity, pestilential or corrupted air, no falling sickness <term>falling sickness: an archaic term for epilepsy</term>, no torment, storm at sea, no fire, or other inclemency of the weather, may offend, hinder, or harm him who shall devoutly carry it with him.
> That in childbirth the mother and the child may be preserved.
> That all those who wear it may always be in safety, that they fear no danger, that they be not afraid of darkness; that no cruelty of the devil may damage them; that they be not deceived by men; that they be delivered from all danger of fire, of lightning, of storms and tempests; And that women may give birth without the perils of labor, and be delivered from pain and from all danger.

---

->AT ROME, At the Printer of the Apostolic Chamber, 1662.<-
->with the permission of the authorities.<-

<margin>(*) The translation of this verse is taken from *S. van Emdre, Historical Report of all Persuasions etc. second edition 1786*.</margin>

## The Prayer that the Pope says at the Blessing of the Agnus Dei.

> That it may erase sins, that pardon may be obtained, and that it may confer graces.
> That upon touching and seeing it, Christians may be moved to praise God.
> That the noise of the air, hail, snow, and storms, the fury of the winds, and the thunders be moderated and stopped.
> That before the Saving and glorious standard of the Cross, which is depicted there, evil spirits may be terrified and flee.
> That he who carries them may have power against the illusions, deceits, trickeries, and frauds of the Devil and of evil spirits.
> That no storm, adversity, pestilential or corrupt air, nor the falling sickness <note>historical term for epilepsy</note>; no torment, sea storms, no fire, nor other injuries of the weather, may be able to offend, harm, or prejudice him who carries it devoutly upon himself.
> That in childbirth, the Mother and the child may be preserved.
> That all those who carry it may always be in safety, that they may fear no peril, that they may have no fear of shadows, that no cruelty of the Devil may harm them, that they may not be deceived by men, that they be exempt from all perils of fire, lightning, torment, and storms; and that women may give birth without labor, and be delivered from evil and all danger.

Plate 19.

# Order of the procession for the canonization of several saints, in the church of St. Peter, under the Papacy of Clement XI, in 1712.

No. 1. The Abbreviators <note>original: "Abbreviatoren" – officials of the papal chancery who drafted documents using a specific shorthand.</note>
2. The Voting Members of the Signature of Justice <note>original: "Votans van de geteekende opene brieven" – these were high-ranking legal officials within the Apostolic Signatura.</note>
3. The Clerks of the Apostolic Camera <note>The <term>Apostolic Camera</term> was the central financial administrative board of the Papal States.</note>
4. The members of the court.
5. The altar servers carrying the seven candlesticks.
6. The Subdeacon with the cross.
7. The Penitentiaries <note>Priests specialized in hearing confessions and granting absolution for specific, serious sins.</note>
8. The Abbots.
9. The Bishops, both in active service and those without a current see.
10. The Cardinal-Deacons.
11. The Cardinal-Priests.
12. The Cardinal-Bishops.
13. The Legate of Bologna, with the Governor of the Province.
14. The Conservators of the Roman people <note>original: "Beschermer van 't Romeinsche volk" – senior municipal magistrates of Rome.</note>
15. The High Official of the Colonna family <note>The <term>Colonna</term> family held the hereditary position of Prince Assistant to the Papal Throne.</note>
16. The Governor of Rome.
17. The serving Cardinal-Deacons and the Deacon of the Gospel.
18. The Pope under a canopy carried by the Referendaries.
19. The serving Chamberlains and the Auditor of the Mitre.
20. The Apostolic Protonotaries and their associates.
21. The Generals of the Religious Orders.
22. The People.
23. The Entrance of the church.

# Plate 46.

<meta>No. 1</meta>

### The Hall of the Inquisition where prisoners are interrogated.

**A.** The Inquisitor who conducts the interrogation.
**B.** The Secretary who writes down the prisoner's answers.
**C.** The Prisoner on the bench, with their face turned toward the side of the Inquisitors' table and toward a large crucifix.
**D.** The Gospel upon which the prisoner is made to swear that he will answer truthfully to everything he is asked.
**E.E.E.E.E.E.E.E.** Armchairs for the absent Inquisitors.

---

<meta>No. 2</meta>

### Various ways in which the Holy Tribunal <note>Original: *heilige vierschaar*, a historical Dutch term for a court of law</note> causes criminals to be tortured in a subterranean vault.

**A.** The Inquisitor who orders the tortures and who increases, decreases, or stops them as and when he pleases.
**B.** His two assistants with the Secretary.
**C.** A criminal who is being given the torture of water.
**D.** A criminal who is being given the torture of the strappado <note>Original: *wip*, literally "the swing." This involved hoisting the victim by the wrists, which were tied behind their back.</note>.
**E.E.E.** The executioners disguised as ghosts <note>It was common for executioners to wear hoods or robes to remain anonymous and to psychologically terrorize the victim.</note>, who give the strappado to a criminal, which is: that they pull him up to the vault with his hands tied behind his back, and then let him fall toward the ground until his hips are dislocated.
**F.** An elevated bench which can be widened or narrowed as necessary, and upon which the criminal is laid to receive the torture of water; across this device goes a wooden clamp that raises the body of the criminal in the middle, usually breaking the loins and causing unspeakable pain.
**G.** The criminal is filled with water by means of a funnel placed in the mouth.
**H.H.H.** Sergeants of the court, who always keep water ready to fill the funnel.
**I.** Torture by fire, which is performed by stretching the criminal on the ground with their feet against a large fire to roast them until they confess everything one wishes to know from them.
**K.** An executioner who smears the feet of the sufferer with lard <note>Original: *spek*</note> and certain caustic or burning oils to increase his pains and torments.
**D.** <note>Likely a labeling error in the original for L</note> The Physician <term>Medicijnmeester|A medical professional attached to the court</term> of the tribunal, who warns when there is a danger to life.

## Place of Execution of the Condemned.

**A.A.** The effigies <term>effigies: life-sized figures or dolls representing the accused</term> of those who were condemned after their death. <note>In the Inquisition, individuals already deceased could be tried posthumously; if found guilty, their likenesses were burned in their place.</note>

Plate 1.

# The Dedication of the Synagogue of the Portuguese Jews in Amsterdam.

**A.** The <term>Hechal</term> <note>the Ark</note>, in which the scrolls of the law are kept.
**B.** The <term>Tebah</term> <note>the raised reading platform or bimah</note>, or lectern before which the <term>Chazzan</term> <note>cantor</note> and <term>Hakham</term> <note>rabbi or "wise man"</note> read the law.
**C. The two galleries for the women.**

This dedication of the Synagogue and the ceremonial bringing in of the scrolls of the law was celebrated for eight days, beginning on the 10th of the month of <term>Elul</term> <note>the last month of the Hebrew ecclesiastical year</note> 5435, corresponding to the 2nd of the month of August 1675, a day on which a commemoration is still held every year.

# The Dedication of the Synagogue of the Portuguese Jews in Amsterdam.

# Plate 3.

**A. Arba Kanfot** <note>original: "Arban canfod"</note>, or garment, which the Jews were formerly accustomed to wear and still wear today under their clothing, so as not to be mocked by those among whom they live. <note>This "Four Corners" garment, also known as a *Tallit Katan*, allows the wearer to fulfill the commandment of wearing ritual fringes (tzitzit) discreetly.</note>
**B.B.B.B. The Tzitzit** <note>original: "Sitsith"</note> cords which are at the four corners.
**C. One of** the aforementioned cords shown in its natural size, with the five knots, which signify the name of **God**. <note>In Jewish tradition, the arrangement of knots and windings represents the numerical value of God's name.</note>
**D. A Sabbath lamp.** <note>Typically a star-shaped oil lamp used to light the home before the start of the Sabbath.</note>
**E. An unleavened <gloss>bread</gloss>** called: **Shmura** <note>original: "Simurin"</note>, with which they observe the Passover. <note>Shmura matzah is "guarded" from the time the grain is harvested to ensure no fermentation occurs.</note>
**F. Ordinary** unleavened <gloss>bread</gloss> called: **Matzot** <note>original: "Mathroth"</note>, which they eat during the days of the festival.
**G. Branch of Palm, Willow, and Myrtle** bound together, which they call a **Lulav**, and hold in the left hand.
**H. Citron**, <note>Known in Hebrew as an *Etrog*.</note> which they hold in the right hand, and join with the **Lulav** and move toward the four winds. <note>This ritual of waving the branches is performed during the holiday of Sukkot (the Feast of Tabernacles).</note>
**I. Shofar** <note>original: "Sophar"</note>, or Ram’s horn with which the New Year and other festivals are announced.
**K. Mezuzah**, a Case or hollowed cane, fastened to the right side of the doorposts of their houses, in which a parchment is hidden and rolled up, upon which is written the following words:

> Deuteronomy 6. verses 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9 “ Hear, O Israel: The Lord our God is
> “ one Lord. And thou shalt love the Lord thy God with all thine
> “ heart, and with all thy soul, and with all thy might.
> “ And these words, which I command thee this day, shall be in thine heart:
> “ And thou shalt teach them diligently unto thy children, and shalt talk of them when thou
> “ sittest in thine house, and when thou walkest by the way, and when thou liest down, and when thou risest up.
> “ And thou shalt bind them for a sign upon thine hand, and they shall be
> “ as frontlets between thine eyes. And thou shalt write them upon the
> “ posts of thy house, and on thy gates.”

> Deuteronomy 11. verses 13, 14, 15, 16, 17, 18, 19 & 20. “ And it shall come to pass, if ye
> “ shall hearken diligently unto my commandments which I command you this day, to
> “ love the Lord your God, and to serve him with all your
> “ heart and with all your soul, that I will give you the rain of your land in
> “ due season, the first rain and the latter rain, that thou mayest gather in
> “ thy corn, and thy wine, and thine oil. And I will send grass in
> “ thy fields for thy cattle, that thou mayest eat and be full. Take heed
> “ to yourselves, that your heart be not deceived, and ye turn aside, and
> “ serve other gods, and worship them; And then the Lord's wrath be
> “ kindled against you, and he shut up the heaven, that there be no rain,
> “ and that the land yield not her fruit; and lest ye perish quickly
> “ from off the good land which the Lord giveth you. Therefore shall ye lay up
> “ these my words in your heart and in your soul, and bind them for a sign upon your
> “ hand, that they may be as frontlets between your eyes. And ye shall teach
> “ them your children, speaking of them when thou sittest in thine house, and
> “ when thou walkest by the way, when thou liest down, and when thou risest up. And thou shalt write
> “ them upon the door posts of thine house, and upon thy gates.”

Plate 6.

<margin>No. 1.</margin>

# The search for the leavened bread

Or the manner in which the leavened bread <note>original: "gedeessemde brood," referring to chametz</note> is gathered together and cleared out of the house. **A.** The mistress of the house who lays some leavened bread here and there, so that her husband, who performs the search, may be able to find something. <note>This refers to the ritual of *Bedikat Chametz*, where crumbs are intentionally hidden so the blessing said before the search is not in vain.</note>

---

<margin>No. 2.</margin>

# The Passover meal of the Portuguese Jews.

<note>The "Portuguese Jews" refers to the Sephardic community, many of whom fled the Inquisition in Spain and Portugal to find refuge in Amsterdam and other Dutch cities.</note>

1. The dish upon which is the bone of a sheep’s shoulder, with a hard-boiled egg.
2. The dish upon which the bitter herbs are placed.
3. A dish with figs, apples, almonds, cinnamon, etc., chopped and cooked together, representing the substance from which their fathers made bricks in Egypt. <note>This sweet paste is known as *charoset*.</note>
4. A dish with sauce, for dipping the bitter herbs.
5. A portion of the Levitical bread <note>original: "Levitische brood"; likely referring to the ceremonial unleavened bread</note>, from which the father of the house breaks pieces to distribute to those at the table. On this occasion, all the Jewish servants sit at the same table with their masters and mistresses. <note>This tradition emphasizes the theme of universal freedom from bondage celebrated during Passover.</note>
6. The napkin with which the bread has been covered.
7. A basket in which the <term>Matzot <note>original: "Mathroths"</note></term> or Passover breads are kept.

<meta>This page follows descriptions of Jewish festivals and customs, likely from an 18th or 19th-century Dutch work describing religious life. It focuses specifically on the celebration of Simchat Torah within the Sephardic (Portuguese) community of Amsterdam.</meta>

Plate 8.

->No. 1.<-

## Simchat Torah, or the Joy of the Law.

**A. The <term>Chatan Bereshit</term> [Groom of Genesis]. B. The <term>Chatan Torah</term> [Groom of the Law]**, both designated as "Grooms of the Law." They represent the kings of *Israel*, who were required on the final day of the <term>Festival of Tabernacles</term> <note>Also known as Sukkot; Simchat Torah marks the end of this festival period.</note> to begin and end the public reading of the Law. The **Chatan Torah** is the individual who finishes the final verses of the Law, and the **Chatan Bereshit** is the one who begins the reading again from the very beginning.
**C.C. Two <term>Parnassim</term> or Elders.** <note>Parnassim (plural of Parnes) were the elected lay leaders or wardens who managed the synagogue's affairs.</note>

->This building represents the Synagogue of the Portuguese Jews in Amsterdam.<- <note>This refers to the "Esnoga," the famous 17th-century Sephardic synagogue in Amsterdam, known for its grand architecture.</note>

---

->No. 2.<-

## The Manner of Escorting the Grooms of the Law Home.

**A. The Chatan Torah**, on the evening of the day he has completed the reading of the law, is escorted to his house by the **Chatan Bereshit**, who walks at his right hand, and by a **Parnassim or Elder**, who walks at his left. They are followed by other **Parnassim, relatives, and friends**, and are protected by several officers of the law. On the day that the **Chatan Bereshit** begins the reading of the Law, the **Chatan Torah** escorts him in turn in the same manner, with the distinction that the **Chatan Bereshit** then occupies the middle position.

<summary>This page describes the festive roles and public processions of the "Grooms of the Law" during Simchat Torah in the Portuguese Jewish community of Amsterdam.</summary>
<keywords>Simchat Torah, Chatan Torah, Chatan Bereshit, Parnassim, Portuguese Jews, Amsterdam Synagogue, Jewish processions, Sukkot, Dutch Jewish history</keywords>

Plate 9.

## Tools used for circumcision

1. Small dish upon which the dressings lie <note>original Dutch "pleisters" refers to medicated bandages or plasters used to promote healing</note>.
2. Another dish containing sand, intended for the disposal of the foreskin.
3. Another dressing.
4. Small bottle containing astringent or <term>hemostatic</term> <note>a substance used to stop bleeding</note> powder.
5. The knife used to perform the circumcision.
6. Instrument used to secure the foreskin.
7. Swathe or bandage to hold the dressings in place.
8. Small silver rod over which the foreskin is drawn.

## The Book of the Law

A. A small box containing the names of all the members of the <term>Synagogue</term> <note>a Jewish house of worship and communal study</note>, from which seven persons are chosen by lot to carry and read from the Law. This box is divided into four compartments: the first, marked 1, contains the names of the <term>Levites</term> <note>descendants of the Tribe of Levi who traditionally assist in religious duties</note>; 2 is for placing the names of the Levites after they have read; 3 contains the names of all other members of the Synagogue; 4 is the place for the names of those among the latter group who have already read.
B. <term>Sefer Torah</term> <note>original: "Sepher Tora," meaning the "Book of the Law" or the Five Books of Moses handwritten on parchment</note> or the opened scroll of the Law.
C. The scroll of the Law, closed and bound with a very wide ribbon.
D. The scroll of the Law with its ornaments.
E. The <term>Rimonim</term> <note>original: "Rimonims," literally "pomegranates," referring to the decorative silver finials adorned with small bells</note> or silver ornaments with small bells, which are placed atop the protruding ends of the scroll.
F. Pointer, for the guidance of the one who reads <note>this instrument, known as a "Yad," is used so the reader does not touch the sacred parchment with their hands</note>.

## The circumcision of the Portuguese Jews.

**A. The Father** of the child.
**B. The Mother** of the child in another room with the godmother; <note>The text notes a gender distinction in 18th-century Sephardic traditions where women typically remained in a separate space during the ritual.</note> for Jewish women do not attend this ceremony; the women seen in the plate are Christians.
**C. The Godfather,** holding the child on his knees during the circumcision.
**D. An empty chair** for the prophet Elijah, on the right side of the Godfather. <note>In Jewish tradition, a chair is often set aside for Elijah, who is considered the "Angel of the Covenant."</note>
**E. The Mohel,** <term>Mohel: the person who performs the circumcision</term>.
**F. The Rabbi,** a relative, or a friend, who offers the cup.

**NB. The Persons** seen here with uncovered heads, as well as the women, are Mr. *Picart* with his household—then consisting of his wife, three daughters, two pupils, and a maidservant—who attended a circumcision ceremony. <note>Bernard Picart (1673–1733) was the famous engraver of these scenes; he often included himself and his family in his illustrations of contemporary life.</note>

->No. 2.<-

## The redemption of the firstborn.

**A. A Priest** <note>original: "Priester," referring to a Kohen</note> descended from the lineage of Aaron, lifting up the child.
**B. The Father** offering money for the redemption, usually consisting of about six guilders, corresponding to five shekels <note>original: "Sikkelen"</note> of the Old Covenant. <note>This refers to the "Pidyon HaBen" ceremony where a firstborn son is redeemed from service to the Temple.</note>
**C. The Mother** of the child.
**D. The Midwife.**
**F. The Wet Nurse.**
The others are relatives or friends invited to this ceremony.

## Marriage Ceremony of the Portuguese Jews
<note>Refers to the Sephardic Jewish community, many of whom settled in Amsterdam after fleeing the Spanish and Portuguese Inquisitions.</note>

A. The newly married man, breaking the glass. <note>A traditional act symbolizing the destruction of the Temple in Jerusalem, reminding the couple of sorrow even in their moment of greatest joy.</note>
B. The newly married woman under a veil.
C.C. Two persons, serving as bridesmaids to the newly married woman. <note>Original Dutch: "meeters" — traditionally female sponsors or witnesses, often godmothers in other contexts.</note>
D.D. Two men, serving as groomsmen to the newly married man. <note>Original Dutch: "peeters" — male sponsors or witnesses.</note>
E. The Rabbi.
F. The musicians and singers.
G. A person who writes down the alms <note>charitable donations</note> promised by those present.

## Marriage Ceremony of the German Jews
<note>Refers to the Ashkenazi Jewish community, originally from Central and Eastern Europe.</note>

A. The newly married man, giving the ring to the newly married woman, both covered with the *Tallit* <note>original: "Taled"</note> or white veil. <term>Tallit: A fringed prayer shawl traditionally worn by Jewish men, here used to cover the couple during the ceremony.</term>
B.B. The two bridesmaids of the newly married woman.
C.C. The two groomsmen of the newly married man.
D. The Rabbi.
*. The rear part of the synagogue.
E. The Cantor <note>original: "Voorzanger"</note>, holding the bottle to give the newlyweds a drink. <term>Cantor: The liturgical leader who chants the prayers and hymns during the service.</term>
F. Two boys with decorated staffs, walking before the newlyweds.

<note>Hebrew: *hakafot*; ceremonial circuits or processions performed around a coffin during a funeral service.</note>
## Or the seven circuits around the coffin.

<note>original: "Fettano. Anno 5455. Sendo. David. Espinoza. Gatela. Administrador." The year 5455 in the Hebrew calendar corresponds to 1694 or 1695 in the Gregorian calendar.</note>

# Plate 31.

# The Bayram <note>original: "De Bairam"; referring to Eid al-Fitr, the "Lesser Bayram," which marks the end of Ramadan</note>
## or the Easter Festival of the Turks.

On which they engage in all sorts of public amusements; some let themselves be swung through the air, hanging from ropes, others let themselves be spun on a wheel of fortune <note>original: "rad van Avanturen"; likely a reference to a primitive Ferris wheel or a fairground ride</note>, etc. This festival is almost like our ceremonies and amusements at the New Year. But since this festival immediately follows Ramadan <note>original: "Ramadhan"</note>, or the great fast of the Turks, it is compared to the Easter festival <note>the author uses the term "Easter" to help Western readers understand the holiday's significance as a joyful feast following a long period of religious fasting</note>.

<summary>This page describes the celebration of Bayram (Eid al-Fitr), detailing public fairground amusements and comparing the Islamic holiday to European New Year and Easter traditions.</summary>
<keywords>Bayram, Eid al-Fitr, Ramadan, Ottoman customs, Turkish festivals, wheel of fortune, public amusements</keywords>

--- Page 664 ---
<meta>This page continues the ethnographic descriptions of religious customs and festivals from around the world, specifically focusing on Islamic traditions in the Ottoman Empire and Persia.</meta>

Plate 32.

<margin>No. 1.</margin>

## The Carnival of the Turks.

This they hold two months before the fast of *Ramadan*, on the first night of the month of *Ramadan* <note>The original Dutch uses "Remesan," likely a phonetic spelling of the month. While the text says two months prior, it specifically refers to the commemorative night of the descent of the Koran.</note>, in memory of the descent of the *Koran* <note>original: "Alcoran"</note> from heaven. The streets are then illuminated and swarm with masked musicians, people on foot and on horseback or seated on camels, and above all a great number of *Santons* or holy men <term>Santons: ascetic wandering monks or dervishes</term>, who were mentioned on Plate 29 No. 4, and who make a terrible noise with their dances, songs, shouts, and howling. In all this crowd appears, seated on a mule, *Sheikh-el-Arfat* <note>original: "Scheï-kel-Arfat"</note>, or prince of the horned ones, who is highly esteemed among the Turks and to whom they give great cheers.

---

<margin>No. 2.</margin>

## The Festival of Hussein in Persia.

At which the deaths of *Hussein* and *Hassan* <note>original: "Hassein"</note>, sons of *Ali* and *Fatima*, who fell in battle, are mourned. Some appear half-naked, painted with blood, and others with black to depict the heat and thirst suffered by *Hussein*; they run together through the streets, bowing and twisting in a hundred ways, making all kinds of distorted facial expressions, and shouting at the top of their lungs: "Hussein! Hassan!" Some dig holes in the ground, bury themselves up to the neck, and remain there the entire day with their heads covered by an earthen pot; others finally appear in military gear to depict the war in which *Hussein* lost his life. A Preacher <term>Prediker: here referring to a religious orator or mullah</term> delivers a eulogy for the saint which lasts two hours, and all these religious ceremonies are followed by the strangest excesses and all kinds of unbridled behavior.

<summary>This page describes two significant Islamic observances: a festive street celebration in the Ottoman Empire marking the revelation of the Koran, and the somber, performative mourning rituals of Muharram in Persia honoring the martyrdom of Hussein.</summary>
<keywords>Islam, Ottoman Empire, Persia, Ramadan, Koran, Hussein, Hassan, religious festivals, Muharram, Santons, rituals</keywords>

Plate 32.
No. 1.

# The Carnival of the Turks.

They hold this two months before the fast of *Ramadan* <note>original: "Ramadhan"</note>, on the first night of the month of *Ramadan* <note>original: "Remesan"; the text contains a chronological contradiction here, likely referring to the festivities of the month of Rajab or Sha'ban leading up to the great fast</note>, in memory of the descent of the *Quran* <note>original: "Alcoran"</note> from heaven. The streets are then illuminated and teem with masked musicians, people on foot, on horseback, and seated on camels, and above all by a large number of *Santons* <note>a term used by early European travelers to describe dervishes or wandering monks</note> or holy men, who were mentioned on Plate 29 No. 4, and who make a terrible noise with their dancing, singing, shouting, and howling. In the midst of all this crowd, *Sheikh-el-Arfat* <note>likely "Sheikh al-Arafat"</note>, or the prince of the horned, appears seated on a mule; he is highly esteemed by the Turks, who give him great cheers.

---

No. 2.

# The Festival of Husayn in Persia.

On which the deaths of *Husayn* and *Hasan* <note>original: "Hussein" and "Hassein"</note>, sons of *Ali* and *Fatima*, who fell in battle, are mourned; some appear half-naked, painted with blood, and others with black, to depict the heat and the thirst of *Husayn*, and they run together through the street, bowing and spinning in a hundred ways, making all kinds of ugly facial expressions, and shouting at the top of their lungs: “*Husayn! Husayn!*” Some dig holes in the ground, bury themselves therein up to the neck, and remain there the whole day, having their heads covered with an earthenware pot; others finally appear in military gear to represent the war in which *Husayn* lost his life. A Preacher delivers a eulogy for the saint which lasts two hours, and all these religious observances are followed by the strangest excesses and every kind of licentiousness.

<summary>This page describes two major Islamic religious observances as viewed by a European observer: the festive "Carnival" street processions in the Ottoman Empire and the mourning rituals of Ashura in Persia.</summary>
<keywords>Ottoman Empire, Persia, Islam, Ramadan, Quran, Husayn ibn Ali, Hasan ibn Ali, Santons, Dervishes, Ashura, religious festivals</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the description of Eastern religious customs, specifically referencing holy men (Santons) previously mentioned on Plate 29.</meta>

<margin>No. 1.</margin>

## The Carnival of the Turks.

They hold this two months before the fast of *Ramadan* <note>original: "Ramadhan"</note>, on the first night of the month of *Remesan* <note>The author may be referring to the start of the three sacred months in the Islamic calendar, or perhaps the Night of Power, though the timing mentioned is unusual</note>, in memory of the descent of the *Quran* <note>original: "Alcoran"</note> from heaven. The streets are then illuminated and swarm with masked musicians, people on foot and on horseback and seated on camels, and above all a large number of *Santons* <term>Santons: A term used by early European travelers to describe Muslim dervishes or wandering monks</term> or holy men, who were mentioned on Plate 29 No. 4, and who make a terrible noise with their dancing, singing, shouting, and wailing. In all this crowd of people appears, seated on a mule, *Sheikh-al-Arfat* <note>original: "Scheïkel-Arfat"</note>, or prince of the horned, who is much esteemed by the Turks, and to whom they give great cheers.

---

<margin>No. 2.</margin>

## The Festival of Hussein in Persia.

During which the deaths are mourned of *Hussein* and *Hasan* <note>original: "Hassein"</note>, sons of *Ali* and *Fatima* <note>original: "Fatime"</note>, who were killed in battle; some participants appear half-naked, painted with blood, and others with black to depict the heat and the thirst of *Hussein*. They run together through the streets, bowing and turning in a hundred different ways, making all kinds of grimaces <note>literally "ugly facial features"</note>, and shouting at the top of their lungs: "*Hussein! Hasan!*" Some dig holes in the ground, bury themselves there up to the neck, and remain there the entire day, having their heads covered with an earthenware pot; others finally appear in military gear to represent the war in which *Hussein* lost his life. A Preacher delivers a eulogy for the saint which lasts two hours, and all these religious observances are followed by the strangest excesses and every kind of unrestraint.

<summary>This page describes two major Islamic observances as seen through an 18th or 19th-century European lens: a festive Turkish celebration preceding Ramadan and the Persian mourning rites of Ashura.</summary>
<keywords>Ramadan, Quran, Santons, Dervishes, Sheikh-al-Arfat, Hussein, Hasan, Ali, Fatima, Persia, Ottoman Empire, Ashura</keywords>

# Plate 33.

---

<meta>No. 1.</meta>

## Solemn Procession on Horseback of
## the Grand Lord,

->To display his greatness to the envoys of the Great Mughal.<- <note>The "Grand Lord" usually refers to the Ottoman Sultan, suggesting a diplomatic encounter between the Ottoman and Mughal empires.</note>

---

<meta>No. 2.</meta>

## The Festival of the Weighing of the
## Mughal, on his Birthday.

This Monarch, magnificently dressed, seats himself upon a golden scale decorated with diamonds to be weighed; the weights, covered with a certain fabric, are also made of gold. It is announced with a loud voice how much he weighs, and this is recorded. If he weighs more than the previous year, bonfires are lit and public festivities are held. <note>This refers to the <term>Tula-dan</term>, a ritual where the emperor was weighed against gold, silver, or grains, which were then distributed to the poor.</note>

<summary>This plate describes the diplomatic splendor of a royal procession and the traditional "Tula-dan" ceremony, where the Mughal Emperor was weighed on his birthday to ensure the prosperity of the realm.</summary>

# The Festival of the Weighing of the Mughal, on his Birthday.

This Monarch, magnificently dressed, seats himself upon a golden scale decorated with diamonds, to be weighed; the weights, covered with certain fabrics, are also made of gold. ## *THIRD*

# GENERAL MAIN SECTION,

->Containing the various types of idolatry<-
->of the Pagan nations.<-

->Nº 1.<-

## Brahmin,

Who has made a vow to wear an iron collar weighing 24 pounds, and measuring 4 feet square, until he has collected enough through alms <note>original: "aalmoesen" – charitable donations given to holy men</note> that he can have a **guesthouse** <note>original: "gasthuis" – a hospice or shelter for travelers and pilgrims</note> built.

---

->Nº 3.<-

## Brahmin,

Who has had himself fastened to a tree trunk with an iron chain on one of his legs, having resolved to end his days in this state.

<column-break/>

->Nº 2.<-

## Brahmin,

Who, out of religious devotion in honor of the god **Eswara** <note>A phonetic transcription of Ishvara, a title often used for the god Shiva, meaning "Lord"</note>, leaps over a fire for half an hour, which he keeps burning with the wood that he has laid ready on both sides.

---

->Nº 4.<-

## Two Brahmins,

Of the caste of the **Shudras** <note>original: "Soudracs" – the fourth varna or social class in the traditional Hindu hierarchy</note> or **Yogis** <note>original: "Joquis" – practitioners of yoga or asceticism</note>, dragging behind them long and heavy iron chains, which are fastened to their legs and run over their shoulders from above.

Plate 2

# Brahma or Bruma.

The first of the three principal deities of the East Indians <note>A historical European term for the people of the Indian subcontinent and Southeast Asia.</note>; his name signifies: *Wisdom*. The <term>Brahmins</term> <note>The priestly class or varna within Hindu society.</note> attribute to him the creation of all visible and invisible things, humans, and as many worlds as there are parts in his body, from each of which a world has emerged, namely:
    The first, which is above the heavens, from his brain.
    The second from his eyes. The third from his mouth.
    The fourth from his left ear.
    The fifth from his palate and from his tongue.
    The sixth from his heart. The seventh from his belly.
    The eighth from his genitals. The ninth from his left hip.
    The tenth from his knees. The eleventh from his heels.
    The twelfth from the toes of his right foot.
    The thirteenth from the sole of his left foot.
    and the fourteenth from the air and the atmosphere by which *Brahma* 
    is surrounded. <note>This likely refers to the concept of the fourteen "Lokas" or planetary systems in Hindu cosmology.</note>

They further say that there is a great correspondence or mutual affinity between these worlds and the limbs of *Brahma*, in such a way that the people of the different worlds have the same affections and inclinations as are characteristic of the nature of the different limbs of *Brahma*.

<summary>This page describes the Hindu creator god Brahma and a cosmogony wherein fourteen distinct worlds emerged from specific parts of his physical form. It notes that the inhabitants of these worlds inherit the spiritual and natural characteristics associated with those specific limbs.</summary>
<keywords>Brahma, Brahmins, East Indians, creation myth, fourteen worlds, Hindu cosmology, wisdom</keywords>

Plate 2

# Brahma or Bruma.

The first of the three principal deities of the <term>East Indians</term> <note>A historical term used by Europeans to refer to the people of the Indian subcontinent and Southeast Asia.</note>; his name means: *Wisdom*. The <term>Brahmins</term> <note>The priestly varna or social class in Hinduism, responsible for teaching and maintaining sacred knowledge.</note> attribute to him the creation of all visible and invisible things, humans, and as many worlds as he has parts in his body, from each of which a world has emerged, namely:

The first, which is above the heaven, from his brains.
The second from his eyes. The third from his mouth.
The fourth from his left ear.
The fifth from his palate and from his tongue.
The sixth from his heart. The seventh from his belly.
The eighth from his private parts. The ninth from his left hip.
The tenth from his knees. The eleventh from his heels.
The twelve from the toes of his right foot.
The thirteenth from his left sole.
and the fourteenth from the air and the atmosphere by which *Brahma* is surrounded.

They say further that there is a great correspondence or mutual attraction between these worlds and the members of *Brahma*, in such a way that the people of the different worlds have the same affections and inclinations as are characteristic of the nature of the various limbs of *Brahma*.

<summary>This page describes the Hindu creator god Brahma and a specific cosmological belief where fourteen distinct worlds were created from various parts of his physical form. It suggests that the character of the inhabitants of these worlds is determined by the specific body part from which their world originated.</summary>

Plate 9.

<insert>No. 1.</insert>

# The Idol Temple of Kamaetsma
## Wife of *Ixora* or *Eswara*; <note>These names are colonial-era Dutch transliterations of "Ishvara," a title for the Hindu god Shiva. Kamaetsma likely refers to the goddess Kamakhya or a local variant of Parvati.</note>

In which, besides various monstrous <note>original: *gedrogtelijke*; this reflects the 18th-century European perspective which often viewed non-Christian religious iconography as "grotesque."</note> displays, the deception of the priests is presented. On the feast day of this goddess, the priests place a child near the fruits that the people offer in abundance, positioned beside a subterranean opening. Then, at the fall of night, they close the doors of the temple and pull the child—along with the fruits and offerings—down through the underground opening to themselves. The following day, they return the child to its original place, decorated with flowers <insert>to suggest a divine miracle has occurred</insert>.

---

<insert>No. 2.</insert>

# The Procession of *Vishnu*,

Or a representation of the idol on horseback, carried upon a plank. He is preceded by torchbearers and covered by parasols <note>original: *zonneschermen*</note> to provide him with shade and to drive away the mosquitoes. For entertainment, a goat or a fox is released, which the Indians <note>In this historical context, "Indians" refers to the Hindu people of the Indian subcontinent.</note> attempt to beat to death with sticks which they hold in their left hands.

<margin>No. 1.</margin>

# The Idol Temple of Kamactsma
## Consort of *Ixora* or *Eswara*; <note>The names Ixora and Eswara are early European transcriptions of Ishvara, a title for the Hindu god Shiva. Kamactsma likely refers to the goddess Kamakshi.</note>

In which, besides various monstrous representations, is depicted the deception of the priests. On the feast day of this goddess, the priests place a child alongside the fruits that the people offer in great abundance. This child is positioned next to a subterranean opening; subsequently, at nightfall, the doors of the temple are closed, and the priests pull the child, along with the fruits and offerings, through the subterranean opening to themselves. The following day, they return the child to his place, decorated with flowers. <note>This account reflects common 17th- and 18th-century European travelogues that often characterized non-Christian rituals as "priestly deceptions" to explain the "disappearance" of offerings.</note>

---

<margin>No. 2.</margin>

# The Procession of *Vistnou*, <note>Vistnou is a historical Dutch transcription of the god Vishnu.</note>

Or an image of the idol on horseback, carried upon a plank. He is preceded by torchbearers and covered by sunshades <note>Traditional ceremonial parasols or umbrellas used as a mark of royalty or divinity.</note> to provide him with shade and to drive away the mosquitoes. For entertainment, a goat or a fox is let loose, which the Indians <note>The original Dutch *Indianen* refers here to the people of India.</note> attempt to beat to death with sticks which they hold in their left hand.

<header>Plate 13.</header>

## Various Idol Temples and Penitents of the Fakirs <note>The original Dutch footnote defines "Faquirs" as "Heidensche geestelijken" or heathen clergy.</note>

1. The great <term>Banyan</term> tree <note>The Banyan (*Ficus benghalensis*) is a sacred tree in India, often serving as a natural temple or meeting place due to its massive canopy and aerial roots.</note>.
2. The temple of the idol Mamanira; on one side, those who come to perform their prayers are marked on the forehead with vermilion, and on the other side, one sees a <term>Brahmin</term> <note>A member of the highest or priestly caste in Hindu society.</note> receiving offerings of rice and other gifts.
3. The temple of Rama.
4. Another temple consecrated to Rama.
5. Temple in which the penitents of the <term>Fakirs</term> <note>In this context, the term refers to Hindu ascetics or yogis who practice self-discipline and austerity.</note> seclude themselves.
6. A kind of coffin or grave in which a Fakir secludes himself several times a year, receiving no light therein except through a small opening.
7. A Fakir hanging over a rope and sleeping in this manner.
8. Fakirs who remain in the depicted posture for their entire lives.
9. Various Brahmins who are worshipped and consulted as saints by the women.
10. Different postures in which many Fakirs keep themselves at various hours of the day.
11. A Brahmin who has his nose and mouth wrapped for fear of killing some small insects through inhalation or swallowing; for this reason, he also sweeps the ground before him to clear away worms or other insects, for fear of otherwise treading upon them. <note>This depicts the extreme practice of *Ahimsa* (non-violence), often associated with Jain monks or specific Hindu ascetics who believe all life is sacred.</note>
12. Fakirs warming themselves.
13. A Fakir who feeds animals out of charity.

# The ceremony observed by the <term>Banians</term> <note>a historical term derived from the Sanskrit 'vaniya' (merchant), used by Europeans to describe Hindu traders or Hindus in general</note> at the birth of a child.

**A.** The mother, offering her breast to the child.
**B.** The child, having refused the breast, is placed under a tree, or laid in a piece of linen which is fastened by its four corners to the branches of a tree.
**C.** The child, having refused the breast for three days, is thrown into the river Ganges <note>This likely refers to historical accounts of ritual practices or misconceptions regarding infant mortality and purification in the Ganges river.</note>.
</insert>

---

<insert>
<margin>No. 2.</margin>

# Ceremony for naming a child among the Banians.

The child is laid in a bedsheet, strewn with rice by the attending <term>Brahmin</term> <note>a member of the priestly and teacher caste in Hindu society</note>; this sheet is held on all sides by twelve <gloss>(*)</gloss> children, in which the child is shaken and tossed to and fro for a quarter of an hour until finally the sister gives it a name as she sees fit.
</insert>

--- Page 792 ---
Plate 18.

No. 1.

## The Water Festival of the Peguans.

At which the King, the Queen, the Nobility, the Soldiers, and all the people <note>original: "dopen"</note> anoint or douse themselves and one another; the former with rosewater and the others with ordinary water. <note>This refers to the Thingyan festival, the traditional New Year celebrated in Myanmar (formerly Burma), where water is used to symbolize the washing away of the previous year's sins and bad luck.</note>

---

No. 2.

## Funeral Ceremony of the Peguans for their Deceased King.

First they dance around the corpse; afterwards they place it upon a pyre of fragrant wood, constructed in the manner of a scaffold and placed upon two boats joined together, in order to burn it upon the water while the <term>Talapoins</term> <note>A term used by early European travelers to refer to Buddhist monks in Southeast Asia, likely derived from the Mon word for "lord."</note> perform their songs and prayers. The deceased of the common people are transported on a stretcher and laid upon a burning pyre without much ceremony.

<summary>This page describes two significant cultural practices of the Mon people in the Kingdom of Pegu (modern-day Myanmar): the ritual splashing of water during New Year celebrations and the elaborate floating cremation ceremonies held for royalty.</summary>
<keywords>Pegu, Myanmar, Mon people, Water Festival, Thingyan, funeral rites, Talapoins, Buddhist monks, royal customs</keywords>

Plate 23.

# Magnificent funeral ceremony of the Kings of Tonkin.

<note>Tonkin, referred to here as "Tunquin," was a kingdom in northern Vietnam. These descriptions are typical of 17th and 18th-century European travelogues, which often blended eyewitness accounts with sensationalized reports.</note>

1. Two bailiffs <note>original: "deurwaarders", likely ceremonial ushers or heralds</note> carrying their staves or maces.
2. Twelve officials, pulling forward the catafalque <note>a decorative funeral platform</note> upon which the name of the deceased King is written.
3. Twelve Elephants which the King usually took with him in war.
4. The Master of the Stables and two Pages on horseback, followed by twelve led horses <note>original: "handpaarden", horses led by hand rather than ridden</note>, which sometimes precede the Elephants.
5. The body of the King, drawn by eight deer.
6. The new King and his brothers.
7. The princesses and ladies of state, who carry food for the deceased.
8. The Princes of the Blood, surrounded by musicians <note>original: "speellieden"</note>.
9. Four Governors of the four principal Provinces, carrying bags of gold and incense on their backs.
10. Two wagons with eight horses filled with gold, silk fabrics, etc.
11. A large multitude of the King's officials and the nobility, some on horseback and others on foot, according to their rank and dignity.
A. Galley, upon which the body of the King is placed.
B. Galley, upon which are the Gentlemen who allow themselves to be buried alive with the King. <note>This refers to the practice of ritual accompaniment in death, where courtiers or servants were buried with the monarch.</note>
C. Galley, with the Ladies, who also allow themselves to be buried alive with the King.
D. Two Galleys, carrying the treasures which are buried with the King.

---

This fleet follows the river which flows through the desert, until a very distant and remote place, where the King and all those who choose to keep him company are buried; the secret is not entrusted to anyone except six <term>eunuchs</term> <note>original: "gesnedenen", literally "the cut ones"</note> who are made to swear never to reveal the place of burial.

<vocab>Tonkin, funeral ceremony, catafalque, elephants, master of the stables, led horses, princesses, ladies of state, musicians, governors, galley, eunuchs, burial</vocab>

<summary>This text describes the elaborate funeral procession of a King of Tonkin (Vietnam), detailing the order of the parade including elephants, treasures, and individuals who were to be buried alive with the monarch. It highlights the extreme secrecy surrounding the final burial location, known only to six sworn eunuchs.</summary>
<keywords>Vietnam, Tonkin, funeral rites, royalty, human sacrifice, elephants, eunuchs, burial customs</keywords>

Plate 23.

# Magnificent Funeral Ceremony of the Kings of Tonkin <note>“Tunquin” refers to Tonkin, the historic name for northern Vietnam.</note>

1. Two bailiffs <note>original: “deurwaarders,” referring to ushers or ceremonial officers</note> carrying their staffs or clubs.
2. Twelve officials, pulling the ceremonial tomb <note>original: “praalgraf,” a catafalque or decorative hearse</note> on which the name of the deceased King is written.
3. Twelve elephants which the King usually took with him into war.
4. The Grand Master of the Stables and two pages on horseback, followed by twelve led horses, which sometimes precede the elephants.
5. The body of the King, drawn by eight deer. <note>While deer-drawn carriages appear in early European engravings of Tonkin, they likely represent stylized depictions of the region's nobility using local fauna for ceremonial purposes.</note>
6. The new King and his brothers.
7. The princesses and ladies of the court, who carry food for the deceased.
8. The Princes of the blood, surrounded by musicians.
9. Four Governors of the four primary Provinces, carrying bags of gold and incense on their backs.
10. Two wagons with eight horses filled with gold, silk fabrics, etc.
11. A large multitude of the King’s officials, and the nobility, some on horseback and others on foot, according to their rank and dignity.

A. Galley, on which the body of the King is placed.
B. Galley, carrying the Lords who allow themselves to be buried alive with the King. <note>European accounts of this period frequently included sensational claims of voluntary human sacrifice or ritual suicide among courtiers following a monarch's death.</note>
C. Galley, with the Ladies, who also allow themselves to be buried alive with the King.
D. Two Galleys, carrying the treasures which are buried with the King.

This fleet follows the river which flows through the desert, to a very distant and remote place, where the King and all those who choose to keep him company are buried; the secret is not entrusted to anyone but six eunuchs <note>original: “gesnedenen,” literally translated as 'cut ones'</note> who are made to swear never to reveal the place of burial.

<header>Plate 23.</header>

# Magnificent Funeral Rites of the Kings of Tonkin <note>original: "Tunquin"; referring to the northern region of Vietnam during the Lê and Trịnh dynasties.</note>

1. Two bailiffs <note>court officials</note> carrying their staffs or clubs.
2. Twelve officials pulling the catafalque <term>catafalque: a decorated framework or "magnificent tomb" used to support a coffin during a funeral</term> upon which the name of the deceased King is written.
3. Twelve elephants which the King usually took with him into war.
4. The Master of the Horse and two pages on horseback, followed by twelve led-horses <note>spare horses led by hand as a sign of status</note>, which sometimes precede the elephants.
5. The body of the King, drawn by eight deer.
6. The new King and his brothers.
7. The princesses and ladies-in-waiting, who carry food for the deceased.
8. The Princes of the Blood <note>royals related by birth</note>, surrounded by musicians.
9. Four Governors of the four principal provinces, carrying bags of gold and incense on their backs.
10. Two wagons with eight horses, filled with gold, silk fabrics, etc.
11. A great multitude of the King’s officials and the nobility, some on horseback and others on foot, according to their rank and dignity.
A. Galley <note>a large, low ship propelled by oars</note> upon which the body of the King is placed.
B. Galley upon which are the gentlemen who allow themselves to be buried alive with the King.
C. Galley with the ladies who also allow themselves to be buried alive with the King.
D. Two galleys carrying the treasures which are buried with the King.

This fleet follows the river that flows through the wilderness to a very distant and remote place, where they bury the King and all those who choose to keep him company; the secret is not entrusted to anyone except six eunuchs <term>eunuchs: "gesnedenen" in the original Dutch, referring to castrated male officials who held high-trust positions in the palace</term> who are made to swear never to reveal the place of burial.

<meta>This page provides a detailed description of Plate 23, illustrating the funeral procession and naval rites for the Kings of Tonkin (northern Vietnam).</meta>

Plate 23.

# Magnificent Funeral Ceremony of the Kings of Tonkin <note>Tonkin, historically "Tunquin," refers to the northern region of Vietnam.</note>

1. Two bailiffs carrying their staffs or maces.
2. Twelve officials pulling the cenotaph <term>cenotaph: a grand ceremonial hearse or monument</term> on which the name of the deceased King is written.
3. Twelve elephants which the King usually took with him into war.
4. The Master of the Horse and two pages on horseback, followed by twelve spare horses <term>original: "handpaarden" — horses led by hand rather than ridden, kept in reserve</term>, which sometimes precede the elephants.
5. The body of the King, drawn by eight deer.
6. The new King and his brothers.
7. The princesses and ladies-in-waiting, who carry food for the deceased.
8. The Princes of the Blood <note>Members of the royal family by direct descent.</note>, surrounded by musicians.
9. Four Governors of the four principal provinces, carrying sacks of gold and incense on their backs.
10. Two wagons with eight horses filled with gold, silk fabrics, etc.
11. A great multitude of the King's officials and the nobility, some on horseback and others on foot, according to their rank and dignity.

A. Galley on which the body of the King is placed.
B. Galley on which are the gentlemen who allow themselves to be buried alive with the King. <note>Early modern European accounts often described "living burial" or ritual suicide of attendants to serve the monarch in the afterlife.</note>
C. Galley with the ladies who also allow themselves to be buried alive with the King.
D. Two galleys carrying the treasures which are buried with the King.

This fleet follows the river that flows through the wilderness to a very far and remote place, where they bury the King and all those who choose to keep him company; the secret is entrusted only to six eunuchs <term>original: "gesnedenen" — literally "the cut ones," referring to castrated male officials who served the inner court</term> who are made to swear never to reveal the place of burial.

<summary>This text describes an elaborate royal funeral procession in Tonkin involving elephants, deer-drawn hearses, and a secret river burial. It highlights the ritual sacrifice of attendants and the extreme secrecy maintained by eunuchs regarding the king's final resting place.</summary>
<keywords>Tonkin, Vietnam, royal funeral, elephants, living burial, ritual sacrifice, eunuchs, cenotaph, Southeast Asian history</keywords>

->Plate 29.<-

# The Gods of the Chinese.

### Taken from the work of Kircher, titled: *China.* <note>Referring to *China Monumentis Illustrata* (1667) by the Jesuit polymath Athanasius Kircher.</note>

# Fo or Fé,

Highest deity among the *Chinese* (of which the name means: *Savior* <note>original: "Zaligmaker"</note>). He is represented with hidden or covered hands, signifying that his power operates all things in an invisible manner. At his sides, he has the two principal reformers of the *Chinese* religion: namely, at his right hand, the philosopher *Confucius* <note>original: "Confutius"</note>, who taught that there is but one single God; and at his left hand *Laozi* <note>original: "Lanzu"</note>, which means "the old philosopher," who likewise recognized and taught nothing other than a single Supreme Being.
D.D.D. are philosophers elevated to the rank of gods.
E. A military commander placed in the rank of gods.
F. A dragon covered with the shell of a tortoise, also representing the god *Fo*. These are the deities of the first rank.
Those of the second rank: C.C.C.H.H. are sons of *Mars* <note>The Roman god of war, used here as a western equivalent for Chinese martial deities.</note>, the god of war, and teachers of that art.
I.K.L.M. Deities of the third rank, set over sublunary <term>sublunary: literally "under the moon," referring to the physical, changing world as opposed to the eternal heavens</term> things, of which one has governance over the water, another over the earth, and another over the fire, etc.

<summary>This page provides a key to an illustration of the Chinese pantheon, centered on the figure of "Fo" (Buddha) flanked by Confucius and Laozi. It categorizes various figures into three ranks of divinity, including philosophers, military leaders, and elemental spirits.</summary>
<keywords>Athanasius Kircher, Fo, Buddha, Confucius, Laozi, Taoism, Chinese Mythology, Hierarchy of Gods, 17th-century Orientalism</keywords>

Plate 29.

---

# The Gods of the Chinese.
## Taken from the work of Kircher, titled: *China Illustrated.* <note>The original text refers to the French edition, *La Chine Illustrée*, of Athanasius Kircher's 1667 work *China Monumentis Illustrata*.</note>

---

# Fo or Fé,

Supreme deity among the Chinese (whose name means: Savior). He is depicted with hidden or covered hands, signifying that his power works all things in an invisible manner. He has at his sides the two primary reformers of the Chinese religion: namely, on his right hand, the philosopher Confucius <note>original: Confutius</note>, who taught that there is but one single God; and on his left hand, Lao-Tzu <note>original: Lanzu</note>, which means: "the old philosopher," who also acknowledged and taught nothing other than a single Supreme Being.

D.D.D. are philosophers elevated to the rank of gods.

E. A military commander, placed in the rank of gods.

F. A dragon covered with the shell of a tortoise, also representing the god Fo. These are the deities of the first rank.

Those of the second rank: C.C.C.H.H. are sons of Mars <note>The author uses the name of the Roman god of war to explain the function of these Chinese deities to a Western audience.</note>, the god of war, and teachers of that art.

I.K.L.M. Deities of the third rank, set over sublunary <term>sublunary: an old scientific term meaning "under the moon," referring to the physical, changeable world of Earth</term> things, of which one governs the water, another the earth, and another the fire, etc.

---

<insert>No. 1.</insert>

# Quantecong,

Chinese deity, who according to their accounts was their first Emperor. <note>original: "Quantecong"; likely a phonetic rendering of Guan Gong, the deified general Guan Yu. While the text refers to him as the "first Emperor," historical accounts place Guan Yu in the 3rd century AD; early European travelers often conflated legendary heroes with foundational monarchs.</note> They attribute to him the invention of various sciences, the establishment of several useful laws, the introduction of clothing, and of their civil form of government; he also does not appear in the form of an ordinary man, but rather as a hero of miraculous stature and strength. Behind him stands his page Lincheu, a Black man <note>original: "neger"; in historical European depictions of Guan Yu, his loyal attendant Zhou Cang (Lincheu) was often portrayed with very dark skin and a fierce beard, leading Western observers to categorize him using the racial terminology of their time.</note> who was in no way inferior to his master in courage and strength.

Household god of the Chinese who lived in solitude and performed many miracles. <note>original: "Quonin"; likely Guanyin, the Bodhisattva of Compassion, who is one of the most widely venerated figures in Chinese Buddhism and folk religion, often acting as a protector of the household.</note>

# A monk, who is punished when he is caught with a woman.

His neck is pierced with a red-hot iron, and a nail is driven straight through this opening, from which hangs a chain ten *Toises* <term>Toise: An old French unit of length equal to approximately 1.95 meters or 6 feet</term> long, which the unfortunate man is obliged to drag through the city until he has collected a certain sum of money for his monastery. Plate 38 No. 1. <column-break/> Plate 38, No. 2.

# Idolatrous Temple of China.

1. The entrance or great gate of the temple.
2. The wall on both sides provided with large stone or wooden pillars, on which statues of armed giants in Chinese clothing are placed; this wall hides the interior of the temple, so that one can see nothing of what goes on in the temple, even though the gate is open.
3. A large stone vase on which incense burns.
4. The interior or inner part of the temple where the religious practitioners gather.
5. The table before the altar, provided with incense, offerings, and two candlesticks; one also sees there the bamboo <note>original: "Bambou"</note> or the great reed, in which many smaller ones are located, and on which prophecies are to be read; furthermore vases, in which the tools of fate <note>These are likely divination sticks, used in a practice known as *kau chim*.</note> are hidden.
6. The altar of the idols.
7. Two other altars with idols on the right and left sides of the great altar.
8. The religious practitioners, who perform their religious duties.
9. Locations of the pillars on which the roof rests.
10. Two water basins on the right and left side between the forecourts or the front part of the temple and the temple properly so called; fish are kept in them; through these two openings and through the gate alone comes the little light that is in the temple.
11. The location of the two rooms or chapels filled with idols.
12. The tables on the wall of the temple on which the instructions for the ceremonies are found, when the lot is cast.
13. Chinese drum on a table.
14. Chinese bell, hanging on the side of the wall of the temple, of a crude or coarse workmanship upon which a wooden hammer is struck, as also on the large drum five or six times after <insert>the</insert> religious practitioners have finished their prayers before the idols.
15. A bench with holes in which the Mandarins <note>High-ranking officials in Imperial China.</note> and those who are used for processions place their sunscreens <note>Likely ceremonial parasols.</note>, which serve to protect the idols.
A.A. Outer wall of the temple.
B.B. The gates.

<column-break/>

# Great Idolatrous Temple in China.

1. The great gate.
2. The small gates on the right and left sides of the great gate.
3. The forecourt.
4. Water basins similar to those in temple No. 1, except that these are located between the forecourt and the gate of the temple.
5. The bridge that runs between the two water basins.
6. The hall which follows the forecourt, but more elevated than the same.
7. Another larger hall, paved with a marble floor, but without a roof, like the temples of the Orientals.
8. Rooms or chapels on the right and left sides of the halls, similar to temple No. 1; in these rooms, the Emperor and the high-ranking members of his retinue formerly washed themselves before presenting themselves to the gods.
9.* Stone vase, in the form of the sacred vessel similar to the other temple No. 1; besides incense, pieces of painted paper <note>joss paper or "spirit money"</note> are also burned there, which the religious practitioners offer to the gods; in some temples, instead of these stone vases, two or three artfully crafted braziers are also found.
9.** Religious worshippers.
9. Third hall, even larger and more elevated than the first two; this has much in common with the choirs <note>original: "Choren", referring to the chancel or the area of a church near the altar.</note> of our churches.
12. Columns and pillars of the building.
13. The doors of the rooms in front of which are covered walkways, through which one must pass to enter the rooms.
14. Table similar to that in the other temple No. 1, upon which one sees, among other things, two candlesticks, hanging lamps, and other incense burners.
15. The altar of the idols.
16. Two stone tables, provided with idols and surrounded by worshippers.
17. Tables hung on the walls with inscriptions and regulations for the lot <note>divination</note>, similar to the other temple No. 1.
18. The Bell.
19. The Drum.
20. The bench for the sunscreens, etc., similar to the other temple No. 1.

Plate 59.

No 1.

# Quack doctors or monks in *China* who occupy themselves with selling wind.

These individuals usually travel in pairs. One of them, with great solemnity, carries a bag on his right shoulder filled with the alleged wind, which he sells for money to those who are naive <note>original: "onnozel" - while it can mean innocent, in this context it refers to being simple-minded or gullible.</note> enough to buy it. These deceivers <term>Kwakzalvers<note>original: "Kwakzalvers." A Dutch term for "quacks" or charlatans who peddled fake remedies and performed street shows.</note></term> usually hold a wooden hammer in their left hand, with which they frequently strike the ground to summon the god or spirit of the wind. According to their claims, this spirit then flies through the air in a human form, seated upon a bird.

---

No 2.

# Religious beggars and *Chinese Quacks*.

These figures ride upon tamed tigers without bit or bridle; they are generally accompanied by religious beggars and penitents who, with a great display of piety, strike their heads against one another.

<note>This passage reflects early European fascinations with Chinese street life, often blurring the lines between actual religious practice and street performance.</note>

<summary>This page describes two types of Chinese street performers or charlatans: those who theatrically "sell wind" by summoning spirits with hammers, and others who ride tamed tigers accompanied by penitents.</summary>
<keywords>China, quacks, monks, wind sellers, street performers, religious beggars, tigers, penitents, charlatans</keywords>

Plate 41.

# Funeral Procession
## of a great person
## in China.

The coffin, covered by a <term>canopy<note>original: "gehemelte", referring here to a decorative textile covering or baldachin placed over the hearse.</note></term>, is preceded by
a great number of young men and young maidens, carrying flowers,
images of men, women, animals, etc., to be burned in honor
of the deceased. The priests carry
large and heavy censers, and are followed by musi-
cians and <term>performers<note>The original Dutch uses "kwakzalvers" (quacks or charlatans), likely referring to the street entertainers or acrobats who often accompanied large public spectacles in Chinese cities.</note></term> on foot and on horseback, performing all
sorts of maneuvers; behind the coffin walk the children
of the deceased on <term>crutches<note>These are "mourning staffs" (typically bamboo or willow); in traditional Chinese rites, they symbolize that the bereaved are so physically exhausted by grief that they cannot walk without assistance.</note></term> to thereby demonstrate
their sorrow; the wives of the deceased follow the procession
sitting in sedan chairs, and behind them the other relatives
and friends.

<meta>This page describes a Chinese funeral procession, likely accompanying an engraving (Plate 41). The Dutch text uses some archaic terminology regarding social roles and ritual objects.</meta>

# Funeral Procession
### of a High Official
## in China.

The coffin, covered by a <term>canopy<note>original Dutch: "gehemelte," referring to a decorative fabric covering or baldachin used to shade the coffin</note></term>, is preceded by a large number of young men and young women. They carry flowers and figurines of men, women, animals, and so on, which are intended to be burned in honor of the deceased. The priests carry large and heavy <term>censers<note>vessels used for burning incense</note></term> and are followed by musicians and <term>performers<note>original: "kwakzalvers." While this literally translates to "quacks" or "charlatans," in the context of historical travelogues, it refers to the street performers, acrobats, or jugglers who often accompanied large public processions</note></term> on foot and on horseback, performing all manner of feats and tricks. Behind the coffin, the children of the deceased walk on crutches to demonstrate their grief; the wives of the deceased follow the procession seated in <term>sedan chairs<note>enclosed portable chairs carried by attendants</note></term>, and behind them follow the other relatives and friends.

<summary>A detailed description of a Chinese funeral procession for a person of high rank, highlighting the ritual burning of figurines and the symbolic use of crutches by the mourning children.</summary>
<keywords>China, funeral ritual, coffin, incense, mourning customs, sedan chairs, afterlife offerings, 18th-century ethnography</keywords>

# *IDOLATROUS PEOPLES,*

## *Fourth Division.*

### *Regarding the Japanese, Tartars, and Laplanders.*

<note>The word "idolatrous" (original: *afgodische*) was commonly used in the 17th and 18th centuries by European writers to describe any religion that was not Christian, Jewish, or Muslim, often reflecting a lack of understanding of the actual spiritual practices being described.</note>

<meta>This page continues the description of Japanese religious figures and customs, likely from a 17th or 18th-century European ethnographic study of East Asia.</meta>

Plate 42.

->No. 1.<-

## Tidebaïk
### Japanese deity.

Richly decorated with gold and precious stones, she has a boar’s head <note>The description of a boar's head and multiple arms suggests this is Marishiten (Marici), a deity often associated with light and protection in Japanese Buddhism, frequently depicted riding or possessing the features of a boar.</note> crowned with a golden crown set with diamonds, and has four arms, of which one holds a Scepter, the second a flower, the third a dragon’s head, and the fourth a ring. She treads with her feet upon a terrible monster, which may rightly be called the devil. One also sees, as in the following illustration, the various ways of worshipping and offering incense to this idol. The true meaning of this idol is unknown.

<column-break/>

->No. 2.<-

## Amida
### Another deity of Japan.

Who represents the supreme being <note>Amida (Amitābha) is the Buddha of Infinite Light, central to Pure Land Buddhism.</note>; the preservation and liberation of souls from well-deserved punishments is attributed to her <note>While Amida is typically male, early European accounts frequently referred to Buddhist deities using female pronouns based on their graceful appearance.</note>. She appears upon the altar, sitting on a horse with seven heads, and having a dog’s head with a ring in its mouth, to represent the return of time or perhaps eternity; each horse head from the front signifies a thousand centuries. This idol is magnificently decorated with gold and precious stones.

---

->No. 3.<-

## Another representation of the same idol Amida and the manner in which one drowns oneself in her honor.

In the hope of a much richer reward in the paradise of Amida, one places oneself in a small boat, with large stones around the neck, the arms, and the body, and—after having music played for several hours—plunges into the water. Others prepare themselves for this over several days through long speeches on the vanity and nothingness of the world and of all earthly goods. Both allow themselves to be accompanied to the vessel after having enjoyed themselves with their relatives and friends. There are also those who make holes in the bottom of the vessel to drown themselves in that manner, etc. Amida appears here with a female face and with large ear ornaments. In other places, this idol appears in the form of a young man, and sometimes in the form of a man with three heads.

<summary>This page describes Japanese deities Tidebaïk and Amida, focusing on their iconography and the ritualistic practice of religious suicide by drowning to reach Amida's paradise.</summary>
<keywords>Japan, Amida Buddha, Tidebaïk, Marishiten, iconography, religious suicide, Pure Land, Japanese mythology</keywords>
