<meta>This page continues the descriptions of Japanese religious figures and architecture, likely following a general introduction to the region's spiritual practices.</meta>

Plate 43.

No. 1.

### Idol Temple of Kannon, deity of Osaka,

In which the preceding and following idols are also located. The building has three stories, accessed by a long staircase. The windows are latticed, through which the idols can be seen. The surrounding walls are decorated with images, and the temple's location on the edge of a forest is very pleasant. <note>Kannon is the Bodhisattva of Mercy, known in Sanskrit as Avalokiteshvara. The "idols" mentioned reflect the 17th-century European view of Buddhist statuary.</note>

---

No. 3.

### Another representation of Kannon,

With seven heads upon the chest and thirty arms, all armed with arrows. In this manner, she also appears in the Temple of the Thousand Idols. See plate 48. <note>This refers to the Sanjūsangen-dō temple in Kyoto, famous for its 1,001 statues of Kannon.</note>

<column-break/>

No. 2.

### Kannon, deity of Japan,

According to some reports, the daughter of *Amida* <note>Amida Nyorai, the Buddha of Infinite Light. In Japanese mythology, these relationships were often interpreted through a Western lens of "gods" and "lineages."</note>. She governs the water and the fish, and created the sun and the moon. She is depicted with four arms and crowned with flowers; she is enclosed in a fish up to the waist. The first arm carries a scepter, the second a flower, the third a ring, and the fourth is raised high with a closed fist. Opposite this idol, one sees the image of a man (half-enclosed in a snail shell) offering his prayer to Kannon. At some distance on an altar stand four figures in a prayerful posture with folded hands, while fountains spring from their fists.

---

No. 4.

### Xantal, an entirely new deity of Japan,

Or the Emperor *Nobunaga* <note>Oda Nobunaga (1534–1582), a powerful warlord who unified much of Japan. Jesuit accounts from the time claimed he demanded to be worshiped as a god under the name "Xantai" or "Santei."</note>, who during his life claimed divinity for himself. He had a magnificent temple built on a hill and placed the kingdom's principal idols within it, setting himself upon a pedestal much higher than all the others with the express command to worship no other deity but his own.

<summary>This page describes various iconographic depictions of the deity Kannon (Avalokiteshvara) and details the self-deification of the Japanese unifier Oda Nobunaga.</summary>
<keywords>Kannon, Osaka, Amida, Oda Nobunaga, Japanese Buddhism, religious iconography, Sanjūsangen-dō, Xantai</keywords>

--- Page 942 ---
Plate 44

->Nº 1.<-

## Toranga, deity of Japan,

Who from a Hunter became an Emperor, and after his death, was placed in the rank of the <term>Camis<note>original: "Camis", a Dutch transliteration of *Kami*, the spirits or deities worshipped in Shinto.</note></term> or blessed spirits, and finally in the rank of the gods. He is depicted fighting with an axe against a tyrant, who was supported by eight kings, which is why the tyrant is shown with eight armed arms. Toranga did this during his life, having freed the empire from a feared tyrant, whom he tramples underfoot in the form of a fire-breathing serpent. <note>The figure "Toranga" is a historical European corruption of *Tōshō-daigongen*, the deified name of the Shogun Tokugawa Ieyasu.</note>

<column-break/>

->Nº 2.<-

## Idol-temple of Toranga,

With a square roof, on each corner of which—as on all other <term>Mias<note>original: "Mias", from the Japanese *Miya*, meaning a Shinto shrine.</note></term> or Japanese temples—four gilded oxen are placed. Its walls are decorated with images of the ancient *Camis* or demi-gods.

---

->Nº 3.<-

## Japanese preacher,

On a pulpit almost identical to those in our churches; he has at his side the patron god of his order, to whom offerings are made. From the canopy of the pulpit, lamps are hung; below is the place where the young priests are situated, some sitting and others standing. The preacher is covered by a very large hat like a parasol, and he has a fan in his hand; from time to time he warns the audience to be attentive by striking a bell standing near him. The book from which he takes his subject is a collection of fundamental principles of the doctrine and ethics of his sect. There are travelers who assert that one often hears beautiful pieces of eloquence and piety there.

<summary>This page describes the deification of "Toranga" (the Shogun Tokugawa Ieyasu), the architecture of Shinto shrines, and the ritual practices of Japanese preachers as viewed through an early European lens.</summary>
<keywords>Toranga, Tokugawa Ieyasu, Kami, Miya, Japanese temples, Shintoism, Buddhism, religious rituals, Japan</keywords>

--- Page 946 ---
<meta>This page continues the descriptions of Japanese religious figures and customs, likely from a 18th or 19th-century Dutch ethnographic or travel text. The descriptions reflect a European attempt to categorize Eastern theology using familiar, albeit sometimes imprecise, terminology.</meta>

Plate 45.

->Nº. 1.<-

# The supreme deity who, according to the Japanese, created the world,

He sits, according to the custom of the country, on twelve cushions placed on a large tree trunk, floating in a pond and supported by a turtle. The creator is black as a <note>The original Dutch uses "Moor" and "Negers" to describe the deity's appearance; this reflects historical European artistic conventions where "foreign" or "ancient" deities were often depicted with dark skin and features associated with Africa to denote antiquity or "otherness."</note> Moor, and has a crown on his head that rises to a sharp point; He has a bare chest and has short, thick hair like the Negros. He carries a ring in one of his hands, a scepter in the other, a flower in the third, and a vase in the fourth, from which water flows; all of this undoubtedly has its significance. The idol is gold, as is everything he wears, even the tree trunk, and his clothes are decorated with precious stones. According to the Japanese, this creator drew the first substance of all things from the tree trunk. Two monsters or devils on one side, and three kings on the other—one of whom is a <term>Sin<note>Likely a transliteration of "Shin" or "Kami," terms for Japanese spirits or deities.</note></term> or half-god, and another has four faces, signifying that he has lived for four thousand years—pull together on a snake which is wound twice around the tree trunk to prevent the creation of the world. The sun emerges from the water up to the waist, in the form of a bearded man of middle age; he pricks the turtle with a needle, having his left hand full of these tools.

<column-break/>

->Nº. 2.<-

# Xaca,
### another deity
### of
## Japan,

This is even one of the first, also named <term>Fotoge<note>From the Japanese "Hotoke," meaning Buddha.</note></term>; it is exactly the <term>Xekia<note>A historical transliteration of the Chinese name for Shakyamuni Buddha.</note></term> of the Chinese.

---

->Nº. 3.<-

# The Idol Temple of the Monkeys.

In the middle of which is the large image of a monkey, placed on an altar provided with offerings and a copper basin, which the <term>Bonze<note>A term used by early Western travelers to refer to Buddhist monks.</note></term> or priest strikes with a tool to awaken the devotion of the people, or to remind them of certain duties. On the two sides of this master monkey is a quantity of other monkeys, each on a pedestal, with offerings before them. This entire worship is symbolic and is based on the <term>transmigration of souls<note>The doctrine of reincarnation, central to Buddhist thought.</note></term> or the transition of souls.

Plate 47.

<insert>No. 1.</insert>

# The Idol-Temple of the Bull

The Bull, which one sees here upon a large altar, is made of pure gold, wearing a richly decorated ornament around its neck. It strikes an egg with its horns, which floats in water within a carved rock. By this, the Japanese—as well as other pagans—understand the *Chaos* <note>The state of the universe before the creation of order.</note> from which the world was drawn. By the bull, they signify the Supreme Being, because in the Hebrew language, one of the names for the Ox is also attributed to God. <note>Likely a reference to 'Aleph', the first letter of the Hebrew alphabet, which originally represented an ox and is associated with 'Elohim' or 'Adonai'.</note>

<column-break/>

<insert>No. 2.</insert>

# The Idol-Temple of the Deity Daibutsu

This temple has a forecourt in which, on both sides, two armed monsters with several arms stand opposite one another. Beyond this forecourt is a large square area with covered walkways, and after this area is another forecourt, decorated with two lions carved from stone, between which one enters the temple. The persons seen on the plate are the Dutch Ambassador with his retinue, accompanied by Japanese officials.

---

<insert>No. 3.</insert>

# Daibutsu, a Japanese Deity

One of the most important of the empire, and as her name implies: the "Great Goddess." <note>original: "de groote godinne." While the <term>Daibutsu</term> (Great Buddha) is typically male, early European accounts often misidentified the gender of Buddhist statues.</note> It is perhaps the same as the *Amida* or the *Buddha* <note>original: "Budhu"</note>, which is to say: the Supreme Being, derived according to some from its specific attributes. The idol sits in the manner of the Easterners upon a low altar-table; she is of an immense stature and rises almost to the vault of the temple. One of her hands has the thickness of a man of average height. She is made of wood that is plastered and covered with gilded copper. Her face and bosom suggest that she is a woman, and her hair is short and thick like that of Africans <note>original: "negers"; a historical reference to the snail-shell curls typical of Buddha statues, which Europeans of the time compared to African hair textures.</note>; she is entirely surrounded by a crown of gilded rays, in which many images of *Kami* <note>original: "Camis"; the spirits or deities of the Shinto religion.</note> are located. To her right and left sides are many other statues, all crowned with rays, much like those of the saints among Christians, and at the foot of the altar are several burning lamps.

<summary>This page describes Plate 47 of a Dutch work on Japan, detailing the symbolic "Temple of the Bull" and the "Daibutsu" (Great Buddha) of Kyoto. It interprets Japanese religious iconography through the lens of European scholarship, comparing it to Hebrew linguistics and Christian saint imagery.</summary>

Plate 48.

# The Temple
->of the<-
## thousand idols
### in *Japan.*

In the center of the temple, the great idol appears in an immense stature, with pierced ears, a bald head, and a shaved chin, like a Brahmin <term>Brahmin: historically, European travelers used this term to describe Buddhist monks or priests, though it specifically refers to the Hindu priestly caste</term>. Above the idol hangs a canopy <note>original: "gehemelte," meaning a ceiling or overhead covering</note> covering five small bells. On the right and left sides of the idol are many images of armed men, dancing Black people <note>original: "negers"; historically used by early European travelers to describe dark-skinned figures or "asuras" found in Buddhist iconography</note>, sorcerers, sorceresses, devils, and even of the winds and lightning. The walls all around are decorated with a thousand idols, crowned, and in every way like Kannon <term>Kannon: (original: "Canon") the Japanese Bodhisattva of Mercy, known for having multiple limbs to help all beings</term>, that is to say, with thirty arms and seven heads on the chest. All the idols and all the ornaments of the temple are made of fine gold. If one were to count all the statues present in the temple, they would far exceed the number of one thousand.

Plate 48.

# The Temple
### of the
## thousand idols
### in *Japan.*

In the center of the temple, the great idol presents itself, of an immense stature, with pierced ears, a bald head, and a shaven chin, much like a *Brahmin* <note>original: "Bramin." Early European explorers often used the term "Brahmin" to describe Buddhist monks or deities they encountered across Asia.</note>. Above the idol hangs a canopy covering five small bells. To the right and left sides of the idol are many statues of armed men, dancing black figures <note>original: "negers." In the context of 17th and 18th-century travelogues, this likely refers to the dark-skinned, muscular guardian deities (Nio or Fudo Myo-o) often found in Japanese temples.</note>, sorcerers, sorceresses, devils, and even personifications of the winds and the lightning. The surrounding walls are decorated with a thousand idols, crowned, and in every way resembling *Kannon* <note>original: "Canon." This refers to the Bodhisattva of Mercy, Kannon (Avalokiteśvara).</note>, which is to say, depicted with thirty arms and seven heads upon the chest. All of these idols, and all the ornaments of the temple, are made of fine gold. If one were to count every statue present in the temple, they would far exceed the number of a thousand.

Plate 49. Based on a Japanese drawing.

# Kannon <note>original: "Quanwon"; referring to the Bodhisattva of Compassion, Avalokiteśvara, known in Japan as Kannon</note>.

Who, by the number of her arms, corresponds to the idol Puzza <note>original: "Puzza"; an early European transliteration for the Chinese deity Cundī, often depicted with many arms</note>. Two of her arms are raised upward and carry children. Six small children surround the head of Kannon in the shape of a crown of rays. One child appears above the head of Kannon, and another sits upon her very head. The idol rests, like nearly all Japanese deities, upon a <term>Tarate</term> <note>a Dutch rendering of the Sanskrit "utpala" or "padma," referring here to the sacred lotus</note> or sea-flower.

This image is intended to represent the principal manifestation of Amida <note>Amitābha Buddha; in this period, Europeans often conflated Kannon and Amida or saw Kannon as an extension of Amida's power</note>, and that which she has devised for the benefit of the human race. This much is true: the various arms of an idol always indicate the distinct attributes and actions of the divinity represented therein.

# Plate 51.

---

->No. 1.<-

## Darma, a saint of Japan,

The son of an Indian king who, in his life, never had an equal. His existence is placed at the beginning of the sixth century, during which he preached to the Chinese, confirming his teachings with an extraordinarily strict lifestyle. It is said that once, when he allowed himself to be overcome by sleep, he cut off both his eyelids (which is why he is depicted here without eyelids); these were afterwards transformed into two small bushes upon which tea grew, which until that time was unknown. <note>This refers to Bodhidharma, the founder of Zen Buddhism. The legend of his eyelids becoming tea plants is a traditional folk explanation for why tea helps monks stay awake during meditation.</note> This saint is shown standing on a reed in the middle of the water, for according to their account, he traveled across seas and rivers in this manner.

<column-break/>

->No. 2.<-

## Japanese Rosaries or Prayer Beads,

According to the various religious sects of the country.

The first is that of the Jōdo-shū <note>original: "Seodosju", a major sect of Pure Land Buddhism</note> sect.
The second of that of the Zen-shū <note>original: "Sensju"</note>.
The third of that of the Hyakumanben <note>original: "Fiakmanben", referring to the "million repetitions" of prayer</note>.
And the fourth of that of the Ikkō-shū <note>original: "Ikosju", another name for Jōdo Shinshū</note>.
The Japanese and the Chinese count their prayers on them, which are much longer than those of the Christians.

---

->Nos. 3 and 4.<-

# Biosju,

Or small shrines for the deceased <note>likely referring to *ihai* or spirit tablets kept in a *butsudan*</note>, which the Japanese place at the entrances of their houses, just as in the Netherlands notices are posted above the doors of the houses of mourning of prominent persons, containing the coat of arms, the name, the titles, the year <gloss>etc.</gloss> and the day of death (of the deceased).

Plate 52.

# Marriage Ceremony

The Bride and the Bridegroom each come separately outside the city to a certain hill; the Bridegroom presents gifts to the relatives of the Bride. Steps have been deliberately constructed for this ceremony leading to the tent of the god of marriage, which the Bridegroom enters from the other side; no one approaches there except the relatives, who place themselves behind the bride, while others stand behind the tent, and the rest of the retinue remains below. The statue before which the newlyweds present themselves sits under <note>the text is missing a word here, likely 'a canopy'</note> and has a dog's head, signifying the fidelity that each owes to the other; it also has <note>likely 'the head of a bull' based on the context of the next sentence</note> to demonstrate the strength and the necessity of the <note>likely 'marriage bond'</note>. Between the newlyweds stands the <term>Bonze</term> <note>a term used by early European travelers to refer to Buddhist monks or priests in East Asia</note> who blesses them. At the side of the tent, torches are burning; the bride lights a torch, which she holds in her <note>hand</note>, repeating the words that the priest dictates to them. Then she gives her torch to the bridegroom, who in turn <note>lights his own or performs a similar action</note>. This being done, the entire retinue raises shouts of joy and a fire is lit below, into which everything is thrown that the newlyweds <note>used in their single lives</note>. Others offer them a <term>distaff</term> <note>a tool used in spinning wool or flax, symbolizing domestic duty</note> provided with <note>flax</note>, as a sign of the care they must have for the household they are about to enter. A sacrifice of two bulls for this ceremony concludes the event; the company returns to the <note>city</note>, and the woman goes to the house of the young man.

---

# Funeral Ceremony

The procession is led by the <term>Bonzes</term> or priests, who carry <note>likely 'banners'</note>; they sing and play musical instruments. Some follow the relatives of the deceased, and others go before the corpse with torches, and the women wear veils; following this, the body is placed as if on a bed of state, in a praying posture. The place where it goes is situated on the ground, covered with a white linen cloth and surrounded by walls. At the side of the funeral pyre are

<column-break/>

the funeral pyre <note>the text continues mid-sentence</note>. The relatives who were in the procession take the belongings of the deceased with them; once they are <note>at the site</note>, they throw the effects of the deceased into the fire, which consists of precious materials, which then burn upon that woodpile. While it burns, it is scented with incense and perfumes. The priests and poor people eat the food that was brought for the deceased. The graves are covered with stones, upon which are expressed the <note>deeds or status</note> of the deceased. The persons of rank have magnificent tombs, upon which the ancestors are depicted. On the day of his <note>burial</note>, our magnificent tombs <note>likely comparing European tombs to these</note> depict the deceased in marble with images of <note>various figures</note>; one named <note>unclear deity name</note> has many arms; in one he holds a <note>unclear</note> and with the fourth <note>he signifies</note> divine providence. There, the old people <note>represent</note> souls and the young <note>represent</note> a figure with four arms holding a snake, and in the third <note>arm</note> a shield sown with <note>stars or symbols</note> which they <note>show</note> to their <note>descendants</note> as a sign of their loyalty to their masters.

--- Page 980 ---
Plate 52.

->No. 1.<-

# Marriage Ceremony in Japan.

The bride and the groom each arrive separately with their relatives outside the city at a certain hill. The groom is followed by wagons full of gifts for the bride's relatives. They climb the hill on stone steps made specifically for this ceremony and place themselves under the tent of the god of marriage. The groom enters from one side and the bride from the other; no one approaches except the closest relatives and the musicians. These musicians place themselves behind the bride, while none stand behind the groom; they remain entirely outside the tent, and the rest of the entourage stays below at the steps. The idol before which the newlyweds stand sits under the tent on an altar; it has a dog's head <note>original: "hondenkop"; this likely refers to a guardian figure or a specific representation of fidelity</note>, signifying the faithfulness they owe each other in the married state. It also holds a cord in its hands to demonstrate the strength and necessity of their mutual union. Between the newlyweds stands the <term>Bonze<gloss>a historical term for a Buddhist monk</gloss></term> or priest who blesses the marriage. At the side of the tent, torches burn, from one of which the bride lights a torch she holds in her hand; while she does this, she repeats the words dictated to her by the priest. She then offers her torch to the groom, who also lights his from it. This being done, the entire entourage breaks into cries of joy and congratulations. A fire is lit at the bottom of the hill into which everything the newlyweds previously used for entertainment is thrown <note>Likely referring to toys or items associated with their childhood or single lives</note>. Others offer them a distaff provided with flax to remind them of the care they must have for the household they are entering. A sacrifice of two bulls for the god of marriage concludes the ceremony, the company returns to the city, and the young woman is escorted to the young man's house.

---

->No. 2.<-

# Funeral Ceremony in Japan.

The procession is led by the <term>Bonzes<gloss>Buddhist monks</gloss></term> or priests, who carry burning torches; they sing and play musical instruments. The closest relatives of the deceased and others walk before the corpse—the men with extinguished torches and the women with veils. Then follows the corpse on a litter, arranged as if on a state bed, in a praying posture. The funeral pyre where they are headed is placed on the ground, covered with a white linen cloth, and surrounded by walls. At the side of the pyre are tables provided with food

<meta>catchword: and</meta>

<column-break/>

and braziers in the form of censers. After circling the pyre three times, the corpse is placed upon it while the priests and the relatives call upon its guardian deity. The priest who was at the head of the procession then takes a burning torch, and after touching the head of the deceased with it three times and circling the pyre three times, he throws the torch at the feet of the relatives or the youngest son of the deceased. He picks it up and throws it onto the ground where the pyre is, which is abundantly provided with oil, incense, and other flammable substances; these then catch fire and consume the entire pyre. While the woodpile burns, the relatives fill the censer on one of the tables with incense and fragrant wood and return home; no one remains except some priests and poor people—the former to say their prayers and the latter to eat what is on the tables. The following day, the ashes and bones of the deceased are collected and placed in precious urns, which are buried and covered with copper plates or hewn stones of various shapes. Upon these are inscribed the name of the deceased and the deity they served. People of distinction also have marble statues placed on the graves, on which are engraved the noble deeds or offices of the deceased, as well as their date of birth and that of their death, which corresponds to our ornate tombs and epitaphs. Often, a marble or stone image of the deceased is placed upon it.

Furthermore, the plate also intends to represent the images of two Japanese deities who oversee the dead. One, named *Jene* <note>possibly a transliteration of Enma or Jizō</note>, has four faces and as many arms: in one, he holds a sun; in the second, a floral wreath; in the third, a branch; and with the fourth, he points his finger at a censer. The sun represents divine providence, etc. This deity protects married persons and the elderly. The other deity, named *Xiquani* <note>possibly a transliteration of Shaka (Shakyamuni) or Kannon</note>, oversees souls and young people; she is depicted as a beautiful young woman, but with four arms: in one, she carries a child; in the second, a snake; in the third, a <term>saber<gloss>a curved sword</gloss></term>; and in the fourth, a ring with knots. Her dress is sown with stars. It is not known what the significance is of the parrot she has at her side.

When a great person dies, it often happens that several of their vassals, subjects, and slaves kill themselves to serve their masters in the next world <note>This refers to the practice of *junshi*, or following one's lord in death</note>.

It also identifies specific deities associated with these life stages and mentions the practice of ritual suicide following the death of a lord.</summary>

Plate 53.

Nº 1.

# The Festival of Souls in Japan

<note>This refers to the Buddhist festival known as Obon, a time when the spirits of ancestors are believed to return to the world of the living to visit their relatives.</note>

Towards evening, the people go outside the city to speak with the deceased; they bring food to their graves and invite them to come into the city. Convinced that the spirits are coming, the people lead them into the city with burning <term>torches<gloss>translated from 'flambouwen', large ceremonial torches used to light the way for spirits</gloss></term>.

---

Nº 2.

# The manner in which the souls are brought back to their graves on the third evening

They bid the spirits farewell by throwing stones at them—stones which have been gathered from the streets and roads for this purpose—in order to drive them out of the city in this way. For if even a single soul remained behind in the city, it would be considered one of the greatest misfortunes. <note>While the use of stones to "drive out" spirits is a specific observation here, most traditional Obon ceremonies involve "Okuribi" (sending-off fires) or floating lanterns to guide spirits back to the world of the dead peacefully.</note>

<summary>This page describes the Japanese Obon festival, detailing the ritual invitation of ancestral spirits into the city with torches and their subsequent "expulsion" back to their graves on the third night.</summary>
<keywords>Japan, Obon, Festival of Souls, funerary rites, ancestral spirits, torches, folklore, superstition</keywords>

## Jumala, deity of the Laplanders,

Whom they formerly worshipped as the supreme being; he sits in human form on an altar. The idol is made of wood, having a vase on the knees in which the offerings are placed. <note>Jumala is a Finnic word for "God." In Sámi mythology, this term was often used by outsiders to describe the high god or creator.</note>

---

->No. 3.<-

# Thoron, deity of the Laplanders.

<column-break/>

->No. 2.<-

## Wirku-Accha, deity of the Laplanders.

---

->No. 4.<-

## Stor-junkare, deity of the Laplanders,

These idols are very crudely worked and poorly formed; the first two are made of wood, of which the upper protruding parts represent a human head, and the others are made of stone. Thoron or <term>Thor</term> <note>A variation of the Norse god Thor, adapted into Sámi belief as Horagalles, the thunder god.</note> has a nail in his head, from which a small stone hangs, so that he can strike fire when it pleases him; he stands on a kind of scaffold instead of on an altar, and although this sanctuary looks very little or not at all like a temple, it is nonetheless surrounded by trees and thorn hedges. He is currently the supreme deity of the Laplanders, for they say he is the master of thunder, and they attribute absolute supremacy to him over men and over spirits. The wooden hammer serves him to punish wicked people and devils. <term>Storjunkare</term> <note>Likely from the Norwegian/Swedish 'Store Junker' meaning 'Great Nobleman' or 'Lord.' He was considered a bridge between humanity and the high gods.</note> is a lesser god than Thor, and in a certain sense his deputy; Junkare means: a Governor or deputy. He is the fountainhead from which all the good that men obtain flows. He has also been made a god of the hunt and a protective deity, and every household possesses one.

*N.B. This description could also apply to No. 3.*

# IDOLATROUS NATIONS,

## Fifth Section.

### Concerning the Guebres or Persians
### and concerning the African sorcerers.

<meta>This page continues the descriptions of religious sites and customs, specifically focusing on Plate 59 which illustrates Zoroastrian architecture and funerary practices.</meta>

Plate 59.

<margin>No. 1.</margin>

# The Fire Temple.

---

<margin>No. 2 and 3.</margin>

# Burial Place of the <term>Guebres<note>Historically referred to as 'Gauren' in Dutch; these are the followers of Zoroastrianism.</note></term> or Persians,

Who have preserved their ancient magical religion <note>The original Dutch 'toverkunstige' (magical/sorcery-like) refers to the 'Magi,' the Zoroastrian priestly class from which the word 'magic' originates.</note>, which they derive from Zoroaster. Their burial places are round stone towers, without doors, at a height of thirty-five feet, and ninety feet in diameter <note>original: 'over 't kruis', literally 'across the cross', a traditional measurement for diameter</note>. The corpses are placed upon these towers, a prey to the air and the birds, so that they may be devoured; and when there are more corpses than the space on the tower can contain, they are lowered through an opening which is in the center of the tower, and they are hung from the wall inside; all the bones which remain from the corpses fall into a very deep pit, which is in the base of the tower.

--- Page 1024 ---
<meta>This translation follows Plate 61 of the Dutch manuscript, describing the religious customs of the "Gauren" (Zoroastrians) in Persia.</meta>

Plate 61.

<margin>No. 1.</margin>

# Marriage Ceremony of the Guebers or Persians.

The newlyweds sit together on a bed. Two *herbads* <note>original: "Hijrbads"; lower-level Zoroastrian priests</note> or priests position themselves before them, one for the Groom and the other for the Bride, having beside them the blood relatives of the newlyweds, their hands [filled] with rice, which is the symbol of fertility. The Groom's priest places his finger on the Bride's forehead and asks her: "Do you wish for the man here present to be your husband?" The Bride's priest does the same to the Groom. Thereupon, the newlyweds give each other their hands. The Groom gives some pieces of gold to the Bride, and those present sprinkle them with rice, while the priests and the next of kin bless them and pray for them. This entire ceremony is performed before the fire <note>A reference to the central role of the sacred fire in Zoroastrianism</note>.

---

<margin>No. 2.</margin>

# The Baptism of the Guebers or Persians by Water and Fire.

After a *dastur* <note>original: "Daroo"; likely a variation of Dastur, a high-ranking priest</note> or cleric has made his prediction concerning the child by consulting the planets of its birth, the Mother gives the child its name; subsequently, it is brought into the temple, where the priest sprinkles it with pure water, which he holds in a vessel made from the bark of the tree called *Hom* <note>original: "Holm"; the sacred Haoma or Hom tree used in rituals</note>, praying to God for the purification of the child; and to sanctify it, he holds it for some time over the fire. When the child has reached seven years of age, it is brought to the temple to be initiated <note>Dutch: "gevormd," referring to a rite of passage similar to confirmation</note>. Two persons hold it over the fire, with the nose and mouth wrapped in a cloth <note>The *padan*, a ritual veil worn by priests and participants to prevent breath from polluting the sacred fire</note> for fear of contaminating this holy element with the breath, and in this position, the child must recite several prayers which the priest has taught it. After this ceremony, the priest gives it water to drink and pomegranate peels to chew, in order to purify it internally as well. He then places it in a water vat and washes its entire body. This being accomplished, the priest clothes it in the *sudra* <note>original: "Soudra"; the sacred undershirt worn by Zoroastrians</note>, being a reddish garment with wide sleeves, and girds it with a wool or goat-hair belt <note>The *kusti*, a sacred girdle</note>, which goes twice around the body and is knotted at the back.

<summary>This page describes two major life-cycle rituals of the Zoroastrian (Gueber) community in Persia: the marriage ceremony and the two-stage initiation/baptism of children involving purification by fire and water.</summary>
<keywords>Zoroastrianism, Persia, marriage rituals, baptism, sacred fire, herbads, sudra, kusti, ritual purification</keywords>

--- Page 1026 ---
<meta>This page continues the ethnographic description of religious customs, specifically focusing on the Zoroastrian community, referred to here as Guebres or Persians.</meta>

Plate 61.

<meta>Nº 1.</meta>

## Marriage Ceremony of the Guebres or Persians

The newlyweds sit together on a bed. Two <term>Hirbads</term> <note>A term for Zoroastrian priests of a specific rank</note> or priests place themselves before them, one for the Bridegroom and the other for the Bride, having beside them the blood relatives of the newlyweds, and their hands filled with rice, which is the symbol of fertility. The priest of the Bridegroom places his finger on the forehead of the Bride and asks her: " *Do you wish the man here present to be your husband?*" The priest of the Bride does the same to the Bridegroom. Thereupon the newlyweds give each other their hands. The Bridegroom gives some pieces of gold to the Bride, and those present sprinkle them with rice, while the priests and the next of kin bless them and pray for them. This entire ceremony is performed before the fire <note>Fire is the most sacred symbol in Zoroastrianism, representing the light of God and ritual purity</note>.

---

<meta>Nº 2.</meta>

## The Baptism of the Guebres or Persians by Water and Fire

After a <term>Daroo</term> <note>An archaic term for a Zoroastrian priest or Dastur</note> or clergyman has made his prediction concerning the child by consulting the planets of its birth, the Mother gives the child a name; subsequently, it is brought into the temple, where the priest sprinkles him with pure water, which he has in a vessel made from the bark of the tree called <term>Holm</term> <note>Referring to the Haoma plant, which is central to Zoroastrian ritual and believed to have healing properties</note>, praying to God for the purification of the child; and to sanctify it, he holds it for some time over the fire. When the child has reached seven years of age, it is brought to the temple to be formed <note>This refers to the initiation ceremony known as the Navjote</note>. Two persons hold it over the fire, with the nose and the mouth bound with a cloth out of fear of polluting this holy element with the breath, and in this posture, the child must recite some prayers which the priest has taught him. After this ceremony, the priest gives him water to drink and pomegranate peels to chew, in order to also purify him internally <note>The pomegranate is highly symbolic in Persian culture, often representing immortality and the fruitfulness of the soul</note>. He then places him in a water vessel and washes his entire body. This being performed, the priest clothes him with the <term>Sudra</term> <note>The sacred shirt, or Sudreh, typically made of white cotton, though described here as reddish</note>, being a reddish garment with wide sleeves, and girds him with a wool or goat-hair belt <note>The Kusti, a sacred cord tied around the waist</note>, which goes twice around the body and is knotted on the back.

<summary>This page describes the ritual life of the Zoroastrian community ("Guebres"), detailing the symbolic use of rice and gold in marriage ceremonies and the dual role of water and fire in childhood initiation rites.</summary>
<keywords>Zoroastrianism, Persian customs, Guebres, marriage rituals, Hirbads, Sudreh, Kusti, sacred fire, Haoma, initiation</keywords>

--- Page 1028 ---
<meta>The source text is in Dutch. It describes Zoroastrian (Parsi) funerary customs, specifically the "Sagdid" (dog-sight) ritual and the preparation for excarnation.</meta>

Plate 62.

<meta>No. 1.</meta>

# Dying Parsi or <term>Guebre</term>, whose soul is received by a dog.

<note>The term "Guebre" was an archaic, sometimes derogatory term used by Europeans and Muslims to refer to Zoroastrians. The text reflects an 18th or 19th-century European fascination with Persian and Indian religious rites.</note>

<image-desc>A large rectangular blank space framed by lines, intended for an illustration of the described scene of a dying Parsee.</image-desc>

---

<meta>No. 2.</meta>

# Funerals of the Parsis.

Before giving the corpse as prey to the birds <note>This refers to the practice of excarnation in a Tower of Silence (Dakhma), where bodies are exposed to vultures to avoid contaminating the earth or fire.</note>, it is placed on the ground and a piece of bread is put in its mouth. One of the blood relatives of the deceased brings a dog near the corpse, as close as possible, but without touching the dog; the closer the dog comes to the corpse, the closer (according to their beliefs) the deceased is to salvation. If the dog jumps onto the corpse to take the piece of bread away, it is a certain sign that the deceased is already numbered among the blessed.

After the trial with the dog, two *Daroo's* <note>original: *Daroo's*; likely a phonetic rendering of <term>Dasturs</term>, who are Zoroastrian high priests</note>, standing two hundred paces from the funeral bier, perform a prayer of half an hour in great haste, with their hands piously folded. During this ceremony, the deceased has a piece of paper at each ear which hangs down four finger-widths below the chin. Finally, the corpse is brought to the designated place, and all those present follow behind two by two with folded hands.

<summary>This page describes the funerary rites of the Parsi (Zoroastrian) community, focusing on the "Sagdid" ritual where a dog is used to confirm death and ward off evil spirits, followed by a priestly procession.</summary>
<keywords>Parsis, Zoroastrianism, funeral rites, Sagdid, Dastur, Guebre, burial customs, India, Persia</keywords>
